  are separate obligations.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Polynomial-map formula, degree invariant, and linearity]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-polynomial-condenser}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.modularFrobenius_eq_pow_modByMonic,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.modularFrobenius_degree_lt,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.polynomialCondenser_apply,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.polynomialCondenser_zero_coordinate,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.polynomialCondenser_zero,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.polynomialCondenser_add,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.polynomialCondenser_smul}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-polynomial-condenser}
  Coordinate $i$ equals $(f^{h^i}\bmod E)(y)$. Over a nontrivial
  commutative ring, every stored polynomial has degree below that of
  monic $E$. For sources already below that degree, coordinate zero
  equals $f(y)$. In characteristic two the output map preserves zero,
  addition, and scalar multiplication over $\mathbb F_2$ for every
  fixed seed. No irreducibility hypothesis is needed for these algebraic
  facts. The finite expansion theorem below additionally requires an
  irreducible modulus; the full encoded construction is separate.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Induct over modular squaring and use remainder compatibility with
  multiplication for the power formula. Monic remainder has smaller
  degree. Squaring is additive in characteristic two, and remainder
  and evaluation preserve addition. An additive map between
  $\mathbb F_2$ modules respects their scalar action.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[The first monomial features and their substitution]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-condenser-monomials}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.monomialDigit,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.digitMonomial,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.substitutedSum}
  \leanok
  Let $e_i(j)=\lfloor j/h^i\rfloor\bmod h$ and
  $M_j(z)=\prod_{i<m}z_i^{e_i(j)}$. For $K$ coefficient polynomials
  $p_j$ define
  \[
    Q_f(Y)=\sum_{j<K}p_j(Y)M_j\bigl((f^{h^i}\bmod E)(Y)\bigr)_{i<m}.
  \]
  Only the first $K$ features are used, where $K$ will be the actual
  source cardinality, rather than the full capacity $h^m$.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Digit substitution and degree bounds]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-condenser-monomials}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.sum_monomialDigit_mul_pow,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.sum_monomialDigit_le,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.digitMonomial_pow,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.digitMonomial_substitution,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.fin_monomial_sum_degree_lt,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.fin_monomial_sum_ne_zero_iff,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.substitutedSum_eval,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.substitutedSum_natDegree_le,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.substitutedSum_degree_lt}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-condenser-monomials}
  If $j<h^m$, then $M_j(u,u^h,\ldots,u^{h^{m-1}})=u^j$.
  Thus the first $K\le h^m$ features become a polynomial of degree
  below $K$, nonzero whenever some coefficient is nonzero. For monic
  $E$, $h>0$, $A>0$, and $\deg p_j<A$, the substituted sum satisfies
  \[
    \deg Q_f<A+(\deg E-1)(h-1)m.
  \]
  Evaluation commutes with the displayed substitution. No source-degree
  or irreducibility premise is needed for this degree estimate.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Base-$h$ digits reconstruct $j$ and have total at most $m(h-1)$.
  Each reduced coordinate has natural degree at most $\deg E-1$.
  Apply product, power, and sum degree bounds.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Interpolation with the actual feature count]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-condenser-interpolation}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.exists_polynomial_interpolant}
  \leanok
  For a field $F$, a finite set $T$, seed values $y_x\in F$, and
  weights $w_{x,j}\in F$, if $|T|<AK$ there is a nonzero family
  $(p_j)_{j<K}$ of polynomials of degree below $A$ satisfying
  $\sum_{j<K}p_j(y_x)w_{x,j}=0$ for every $x\in T$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  The linear map from $AK$ coefficients to the $|T|$ constraints
  has a nontrivial kernel. Grouping coefficients into $K$ polynomials
  is injective, so the resulting family is nonzero.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Primitive coefficients and quotient-field root count]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-condenser-quotient}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.exists_primitive_polynomialFamily,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.substitutedSum_quotient_eval,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.card_lt_of_quotient_polynomial_roots}
  \leanok
  \uses{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-condenser-monomials,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-condenser-interpolation}
  If a positive-degree monic $E$ does not vanish at the constrained
  seeds, an interpolating family can be chosen with some coefficient
  not divisible by $E$, preserving its degree bound and constraints.
  For irreducible $E$, the polynomial
  $\sum_{j<K}[p_j]Z^j$ over $F[Y]/(E)$ is then nonzero of degree
  below $K$. Consequently fewer than $K$ distinct source polynomials
  of degree below $\deg E$ can give roots of this quotient polynomial.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Divide common factors of $E$ out of the coefficient family; each
  division strictly lowers its common degree bound. Cancel $E(y_x)$
  in each constraint. In the quotient, digit substitution gives the
  stated polynomial, while distinct bounded-degree representatives
  have distinct classes. Apply the field root bound.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[The polynomial neighbor graph]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-condenser-neighbors}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.polynomialNeighbor,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.polynomialNeighborSet}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-polynomial-condenser}
  The full neighbor of a source $f$ at seed $y\in F$ is
  $(y,((f^{h^i}\bmod E)(y))_{i<m})$. The neighbors of $P$ are the
  union of these pairs over $f\in P$ and every seed in $F$.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Finite GUV polynomial expansion]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-condenser-expansion}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.le_card_polynomialNeighborSet,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.polynomialNeighborSet_expansion,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.card_polynomialNeighborSet_of_degree_one,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.polynomialNeighbor_pow_two}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-condenser-neighbors,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-condenser-quotient}
  Let $F$ have cardinality $q$, let $E$ be monic and irreducible of
  degree $n\ge2$, and let $h>0$. Every set $P$ of polynomials of
  degree below $n$, with $|P|\le h^m$, satisfies
  \[
    |\Gamma(P)|\ge\bigl(q-(n-1)(h-1)m\bigr)|P|,
  \]
  where subtraction is truncated at zero. This is the finite
  expansion argument of Guruswami--Umans--Vadhan (2009), Theorem 3.3.
  For degree-one moduli and at least one output coordinate, the exact
  neighbor count is $q|P|$. For $h=2^r$, the full neighbor equals the
  seed paired with the modular-squaring condenser output.
  The field and modulus are supplied; this theorem alone makes no
  uniform construction or distributional claim.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Put $K=|P|$ and $A=q-(n-1)(h-1)m$. If expansion fails and $A>0$,
  interpolate with exactly $K$ features against the neighbor union.
  Primitive normalization is possible because $E$ has no roots in $F$.
  Each substituted polynomial has degree below $q$ and vanishes at all
  $q$ seeds, hence is zero. The quotient-field root count would then
  give $|P|<K$, a contradiction. If $A=0$, the bound is immediate.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Seeded neighbors and uniform test probabilities]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-flat-lossless}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.seededNeighborSet,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.seededTestProb}
  \leanok
  For a finite source support $P$ and seeded map $C$, let
  $\Gamma_C(P)=\{(y,C(x,y)):x\in P,\ y\in S\}$. The seeded test
  probability is the fraction of independent uniform source and seed
  pairs whose joint seed-output belongs to the test set. Repeated
  outputs retain their multiplicity in this probability.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Seed-preserving flat lossless condensation]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-flat-lossless}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.exists_seedwise_injection,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.seededTestProb_sub_le_disagreement,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.exists_seedwise_injection_of_expansion,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.polynomialCondenser_flat_lossless}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-flat-lossless,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-condenser-expansion}
  Suppose $P,S$ are nonempty, the output alphabet has at least $|P|$
  elements, and $|\Gamma_C(P)|\ge(1-\epsilon)|P||S|$. There are
  injections $g_y:P\hookrightarrow\Omega$, one for each seed,
  such that every test on $(y,C(x,y))$ differs in probability by at
  most $\epsilon$ from the same test on $(y,g_y(x))$.
  Conditional on each seed, this ideal output is uniform on exactly
  $|P|$ values. The construction preserves exactly $|\Gamma_C(P)|$
  original source-seed pairs.

  For the polynomial map with $h=2^r\le q$, supplied irreducible
  modulus of degree $n\ge2$, and a nonempty qualifying source set
  $|P|\le h^m$, the error is $(n-1)(h-1)m/q$. The source-dependent
  witness is a distributional comparison, not an evaluator or a
  theorem about arbitrary weighted sources.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  For each seed retain one actual source representative for every
  reached output. Extend this partial assignment to an injection using
  the alphabet capacity. Agreeing pairs map bijectively to reached
  neighbors. Every change in a test outcome occurs on a disagreeing
  pair, so division by $|P||S|$ gives the bound. Apply finite polynomial
  expansion, including its valid trivial bound when the loss exceeds $q$.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[A sparse binomial modulus family]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-binomial-modulus}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.binomialModulus}
  \leanok
  For a coefficient $a$ in the supplied field, set
  $E_{a,s}(X)=X^{3^s}-a$.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Noncubes yield irreducible moduli at rounded degrees]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-binomial-modulus}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.binomialModulus_monic,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.binomialModulus_natDegree,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.binomialModulus_irreducible,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.exists_noncube,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.three_dvd_card_sub_one_of_even_exponent,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.binomialModulus_natDegree_clog_bounds}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-binomial-modulus}
  If $a$ is not a cube, every $E_{a,s}$ is monic and irreducible,
  with degree exactly $3^s$, including $s=0$. A noncube exists in any
  finite field with $3\mid(q-1)$, in particular when $q=2^b$ and $b$
  is even. For every $n>0$, choosing $s=\lceil\log_3 n\rceil$
  gives $n\le\deg E_{a,s}<3n$. These are semantic statements;
  they do not provide encoded field or coefficient selection.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Reuse Mathlib's odd-prime-power binomial irreducibility theorem in
  its Kummer development. Cauchy's theorem gives a nontrivial cube
  root of unity, so cubing on the finite field is not injective and
  therefore not surjective. The degree bounds follow from the
  defining inequalities of the integer ceiling logarithm.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[The binomial root supplies another noncube]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-binomial-extension}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.norm_root_binomialModulus,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.root_binomialModulus_noncube,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.binomialModulus_root_properties}
  \leanok
  \uses{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-binomial-modulus}
  The root $t$ in $F[X]/(X^{3^s}-a)$ has norm $a$ over $F$.
  Consequently, if $a$ is a noncube, $t$ is also a noncube and
  $Z^{3^v}-t$ is monic and irreducible of degree exactly $3^v$.
  Both exponents may be zero. This supplies a second algebraic
  modulus without another coefficient-existence premise; an encoded
  field representation remains a separate construction.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Mathlib's power-basis norm formula gives $(-1)^{3^s}(-a)=a$.
  Applying the norm to a hypothetical equation $b^3=t$ contradicts
  the noncube premise on $a$. Apply the binomial irreducibility theorem
  in the first quotient field.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Sparse field and powering parameters]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-sparse-condenser-parameters}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.sparseFieldExponent,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.sparseFieldBits,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.sparsePowerBits,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.condenserCoordinates}
  \leanok
  Set $s=\lceil\log_3\lceil (u+1)T/2\rceil\rceil$,
  $b=2\cdot3^s$, $r=b-T$, and $m=\lceil k/r\rceil$.
  The Lean definitions are total natural-number functions; the
  following bounds specify their positive-parameter behavior.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Sparse rounding preserves the output rate and error budget]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-sparse-condenser-parameters}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.sparseFieldBits_lower,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.sparseFieldBits_upper,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.sparsePowerBits_add,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.sparsePowerBits_pos,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.sparsePowerBits_rate,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.condenserCoordinates_capacity,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.condenserCoordinates_le,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.condenserCoordinates_output,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.condenserCoordinates_loss,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.sparseCondenser_output,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.sparseCondenser_loss}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-sparse-condenser-parameters}
  For $u,T>0$ these choices satisfy
  \[
    (u+1)T\le b\le6(u+1)T,\qquad r>0,\qquad
    ub\le(u+1)r,\qquad k\le rm,\qquad m\le k.
  \]
  The seed has $b$ bits and the output has $mb$ bits, with
  $umb\le(u+1)k+ub$. If $Dk\le\epsilon2^T$, then
  \[
    \frac{(D-1)(2^r-1)m}{2^b}\le\epsilon.
  \]
  These are finite parameter inequalities, without an asymptotic or
  runtime assertion. Choosing the powering exponent after the sparse
  field size adapts Cheraghchi's condenser parameter tradeoff to the
  binomial degree grid; this choice is a deduction in this development.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Ceiling logarithms bound the rounded field width. Its lower bound
  gives $ub\le(u+1)(b-T)$. Ceiling division gives $(m-1)r\le k$,
  from which the output bound follows. Bound the numerator of the
  error by $Dk2^r$ and use $b=r+T$.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Binary polynomial coefficients and multiplication]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-bit-polynomial}
  \lean{Complexity.BitPolynomial.ofBits,
    Complexity.BitPolynomial.mulBits,
    Complexity.BitPolynomial.mulEval}
  \leanok
  A bit list represents a polynomial over $\mathbb F_2$, constant
  coefficient first, with high zero padding permitted. Carryless
  multiplication computes the parity of each coefficient convolution.
  The evaluator reads both operands through the standard pairing codec.
  Both polynomials are runtime inputs.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Uniform polynomial-time carryless multiplication]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-bit-polynomial}
  \lean{Complexity.BitPolynomial.ofBits_coeff,
    Complexity.BitPolynomial.mulBits_length,
    Complexity.BitPolynomial.ofBits_mulBits,
    Complexity.BitPolynomial.mulEval_pair,
    Complexity.BitPolynomial.mulEval_mem_FP}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-bit-polynomial}
  The coefficient-list product denotes the product of the represented
  polynomials and has length equal to the sum of the operand lengths.
  The theorem covers empty operands and high zero padding. On a paired
  input the evaluator returns exactly this product list, and the single
  evaluator belongs to $\mathrm{FP}$ uniformly over all input lengths.
  This arithmetic component is used with the explicit binary modulus
  generator and bounded-state evaluators below.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Expand the product coefficient as a convolution, restrict to indices
  within the first operand, and count the true coefficient pairs modulo
  two. Beyond the chosen output length every contributing coefficient is
  zero. Polynomial-time bounded counting, parity, bit access, and output
  generation give the machine certificate through Cobham's algebra.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Binary polynomial remainder by a runtime modulus]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-bit-remainder}
  \lean{Complexity.BitPolynomial.significantLength,
    Complexity.BitPolynomial.remainderStep,
    Complexity.BitPolynomial.remainderScan,
    Complexity.BitPolynomial.remainderBits,
    Complexity.BitPolynomial.remainderEval}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-bit-polynomial}
  Significant length is one past the highest true coefficient, or zero.
  For a nonzero modulus of degree $d$, scan the dividend from high to
  low, keeping $d$ bits. Shift, insert the next coefficient, and XOR
  the modulus when needed to cancel the degree-$d$ term. A zero modulus
  returns the dividend unchanged, following the polynomial remainder
  convention. The paired evaluator reads both lists at runtime.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Exact and uniform polynomial-time binary remainder]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-bit-remainder}
  \lean{Complexity.BitPolynomial.significantLength_le,
    Complexity.BitPolynomial.significantLength_eq_zero_iff,
    Complexity.BitPolynomial.remainderStep_length,
    Complexity.BitPolynomial.remainderScan_length,
    Complexity.BitPolynomial.remainderBits_length,
    Complexity.BitPolynomial.remainderBits_length_le,
    Complexity.BitPolynomial.ofBits_remainderBits,
    Complexity.BitPolynomial.remainderEval_pair,
    Complexity.BitPolynomial.significantLength_unary,
    Complexity.BitPolynomial.remainderEval_mem_FP}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-bit-remainder}
  For every dividend and modulus list, the output represents their
  exact monic polynomial remainder, with no nonzero or padding premise.
  Its length is the dividend length for a zero modulus, and otherwise
  one less than the modulus's significant length. In particular, a
  constant-one modulus gives the empty list. Significant length is a
  polynomial-time unary function, and the paired remainder evaluator
  belongs to $\mathrm{FP}$ uniformly in both input lists.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Identify significant length with the represented polynomial degree
  and prove the single cancellation step by coefficient extensionality
  and uniqueness of monic remainder. Induct over the scan. A bounded
  maximum, bitwise step, and bounded-state fold give the machine
  certificate; routine closure proofs use \texttt{polytime}.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Finite mixtures of seeded sources]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-seeded-mixture}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.seededMixtureTestProb}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-flat-lossless}
  Given finitely many seeded maps $C_i$ and real weights $w_i$, define
  the mixture test value as $\sum_i w_i\Pr[(Y,C_i(X_i,Y))\in T]$.
  Each $X_i$ is uniform on its own finite source type, and $Y$ is
  an independent uniform seed shared by all components.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Subset averaging and lossless mixtures]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-lossless-mixture}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.sum_powersetCard_sum,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.powersetCard_average,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.seededTestProb_powersetCard,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.seededMixtureTestProb_sub_le,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.exists_flat_mixture_of_exact_size,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.polynomialCondenser_flat_mixture}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-seeded-mixture,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-flat-lossless}
  For $0<K\le |P|$, the uniform source on $P$ equals the uniform
  mixture of its $K$-element subsets on every seeded test. Nonnegative
  weights summing to one preserve a common discrepancy bound.
  Consequently, lossless witnesses for every $K$-subset give an explicit
  mixture of seedwise $K$-flat witnesses for the larger support.

  For the polynomial condenser, this applies whenever $K\le(2^r)^m$
  and $2^r\le q$, with the same normalized error
  $(\deg E-1)(2^r-1)m/q$. There is no upper bound on $|P|$ beyond
  its membership in the bounded-degree source domain. This does not
  assert decomposition of arbitrary capped probability weights.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Each point of $P$ belongs to exactly $\binom{|P|-1}{K-1}$ subsets
  of size $K$. Double counting proves the averaging identity with
  all source and seed multiplicities retained. The triangle inequality
  proves mixture closure, and the existing lossless theorem supplies
  each polynomial-condenser component.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Explicit sparse binary moduli]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-binary-modulus}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.binaryModulus}
  \leanok
  Set $M_s(X)=X^{2\cdot3^s}+X^{3^s}+1$ over $\mathbb F_2$.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Binary field family with a named noncube]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-binary-modulus}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.binaryModulus_monic,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.binaryModulus_natDegree,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.binaryModulus_irreducible,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.card_adjoinRoot_binaryModulus,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.binaryModulus_comp_cube,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.root_binaryModulus_noncube}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-binary-modulus,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-binomial-modulus}
  For every $s\ge0$, $M_s$ is monic and irreducible of degree
  $2\cdot3^s$. Its quotient has cardinality $2^{2\cdot3^s}$, and its
  named root is a noncube. Moreover $M_s(X^3)=M_{s+1}(X)$.
  Thus this sparse family supplies both a binary field and the
  coefficient for a subsequent binomial extension without search.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  The root of $X^2+X+1$ is a noncube in the four-element field.
  Mathlib's irreducible-composition theorem and odd-prime-power Kummer
  theorem give irreducibility of every $M_s$. The monic power basis
  gives the quotient cardinality. If its named root had a cube root,
  that element would be a root of $M_{s+1}$, whose minimal-polynomial
  degree would exceed the quotient dimension.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Encoded addition and bounded modular squaring]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-bit-frobenius}
  \lean{Complexity.BitPolynomial.addBits,
    Complexity.BitPolynomial.addEval,
    Complexity.BitPolynomial.frobeniusStep,
    Complexity.BitPolynomial.frobeniusBits,
    Complexity.BitPolynomial.frobeniusEval}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-bit-remainder}
  Addition XORs the coefficient lists with zero padding. Modular
  squaring reduces after each square, once per bit in a unary count
  word; it starts by reducing the dividend. The evaluator reads
  dividend, modulus, and count through nested pairing. A zero modulus
  gives the empty output.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Uniform polynomial-time bounded modular squaring]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-bit-frobenius}
  \lean{Complexity.BitPolynomial.addBits_length,
    Complexity.BitPolynomial.ofBits_addBits,
    Complexity.BitPolynomial.addEval_mem_FP,
    Complexity.BitPolynomial.ofBits_natDegree,
    Complexity.BitPolynomial.frobeniusBits_length,
    Complexity.BitPolynomial.frobeniusBits_of_modulus_zero,
    Complexity.BitPolynomial.ofBits_frobeniusBits,
    Complexity.BitPolynomial.frobeniusEval_pair,
    Complexity.BitPolynomial.frobeniusEval_mem_FP}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-bit-frobenius,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-bit-remainder}
  Encoded addition denotes polynomial addition and has the maximum
  operand length. For a nonzero represented modulus $E$, the squaring
  loop on a count word of length $t$ denotes $f^{2^t}\bmod E$.
  Every loop state has exactly $\deg E$ bits, including zero remainders.
  Both evaluators belong to $\mathrm{FP}$ uniformly in all runtime
  words; squaring time is polynomial in the unary count length $t$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Coefficient extensionality proves addition. Induction on the count
  word and modular-power identities prove squaring correctness.
  The remainder width gives the state invariant for Cobham's bounded
  fold, using the registered multiplication and remainder certificates.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Blockwise modular Horner evaluation]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-bit-horner}
  \lean{Complexity.BitPolynomial.coefficientBlock,
    Complexity.BitPolynomial.blockEvalStep,
    Complexity.BitPolynomial.blockEvalScan,
    Complexity.BitPolynomial.blockEvalBits,
    Complexity.BitPolynomial.blockEval}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-bit-frobenius}
  Split a coefficient word into consecutive blocks of runtime width
  $b$. For a runtime block count $t$, evaluate the first $t$ blocks
  by Horner's rule from high to low, reducing modulo the supplied
  binary modulus after every step. Width and count are unary word
  lengths. Missing bits are zero, and a zero modulus gives empty output.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Exact polynomial-time block evaluation]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-bit-horner}
  \lean{Complexity.BitPolynomial.blockEvalBits_length,
    Complexity.BitPolynomial.blockEvalBits_of_modulus_zero,
    Complexity.BitPolynomial.ofBits_blockEvalScan,
    Complexity.BitPolynomial.ofBits_blockEvalBits,
    Complexity.BitPolynomial.blockEval_pair,
    Complexity.BitPolynomial.blockEval_mem_FP}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-bit-horner,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-bit-frobenius}
  If $c_j$ is the polynomial represented by coefficient block $j$,
  then for nonzero modulus $E$ the output represents
  $\bigl(\sum_{j<t}c_jy^j\bigr)\bmod E$. Every state has exactly
  $\deg E$ bits. The encoded evaluator belongs to $\mathrm{FP}$
  uniformly in the coefficient word, unary width, seed, modulus,
  and unary count, including truncated and padded coefficient data.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  A partial-fold invariant records the interval of coefficients
  already consumed. The step adds the next block to the seed times
  the current state. The fixed remainder width bounds every state;
  the arithmetic and pairing certificates discharge the fold's
  polynomial-time premises.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Runtime binary trinomial generation]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-bit-trinomial}
  \lean{Complexity.BitPolynomial.trinomialBits,
    Complexity.BitPolynomial.trinomialEval,
    Complexity.BitPolynomial.roundedTrinomialEval}
  \leanok
  Generate $2d+1$ bits, true exactly at positions $0,d,2d$.
  The rounded generator takes $d=3^{\lceil\log_3 n\rceil}$,
  where $n$ is the input word length; at $n=0$, it takes $d=1$.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Exact polynomial-time trinomial generation]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-bit-trinomial}
  \lean{Complexity.BitPolynomial.trinomialBits_length,
    Complexity.BitPolynomial.ofBits_trinomialBits,
    Complexity.BitPolynomial.trinomialEval_mem_FP,
    Complexity.BitPolynomial.trinomialBits_mem_FP,
    Complexity.BitPolynomial.roundedTrinomialEval_mem_FP,
    Complexity.UnaryFn.pow_clog}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-bit-trinomial}
  The generated polynomial is $X^{2d}+X^d+1$, even at $d=0$.
  Both generators are uniformly polynomial-time. More generally,
  rounding a polynomial-time unary target to a power of a
  polynomial-time unary base is polynomial-time, including bases
  zero and one. This closure is registered for \texttt{polytime}.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  At positive $d$ the three positions are distinct. At zero the
  three unit coefficients sum to one in $\mathbb F_2$.
  The bound $b^{\operatorname{clog}_b n}\le bn+1$ permits the existing
  capped-power program to compute rounding. A bitwise output program
  generates the coefficients from unary arithmetic tests.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Canonical binary quotient representations]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-binary-codec}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.BinaryFieldCodec.Bits,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.BinaryFieldCodec.decode,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.BinaryFieldCodec.encode,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.BinaryFieldCodec.equiv}
  \leanok
  \uses{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-binary-modulus}
  Decode a bit list by polynomial interpretation and the quotient map
  into $\mathbb F_2[X]/(M_s)$. Encode a quotient element by the
  coefficients of its unique reduced representative, retaining
  exactly $2\cdot3^s$ bits. This gives a semantic equivalence with
  lists of that exact length; it is not an algorithm on abstract
  quotient objects.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Exact quotient-codec round trips and arithmetic]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-binary-codec}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.BinaryFieldCodec.length_encode,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.BinaryFieldCodec.ofBits_encode,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.BinaryFieldCodec.decode_encode,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.BinaryFieldCodec.encode_decode,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.BinaryFieldCodec.decode_addBits,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.BinaryFieldCodec.decode_mulBits,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.BinaryFieldCodec.decode_remainderBits,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.BinaryFieldCodec.decode_frobeniusBits}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-binary-codec,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-bit-frobenius}
  Both round trips hold, with high zero coefficients retained at
  the canonical width. Decoding respects encoded addition and
  multiplication. Reduction by any representation of $M_s$ leaves
  the quotient element unchanged, and the bounded squaring loop
  decodes to the corresponding power of that element.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Mathlib's monic-remainder map is inverse to the quotient map on
  reduced representatives. Every coefficient is either zero or one.
  The arithmetic laws follow from the encoded polynomial semantics
  and the quotient ring homomorphism.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Modular powering with a generated modulus]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-generated-frobenius}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.encodedBinaryFrobenius}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-bit-trinomial,
    def:lowerbounds-algebraic-bit-frobenius}
  Read a dividend, a unary half-degree target, and a unary squaring
  count. Generate the rounded trinomial modulus and run the bounded
  modular-squaring evaluator on these runtime data.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Uniform generated-modulus powering]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-generated-frobenius}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.ofBits_trinomialBits_pow_three,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.ofBits_roundedTrinomialEval,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.roundedTrinomialEval_degree_bounds,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.ofBits_frobeniusBits_binaryModulus,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.frobeniusBits_binaryModulus_length,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.encodedBinaryFrobenius_pair,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.ofBits_encodedBinaryFrobenius,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.encodedBinaryFrobenius_length,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.encodedBinaryFrobenius_mem_FP}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-generated-frobenius,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-binary-modulus,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-bit-trinomial,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-bit-frobenius}
  For target length $n$, the generated polynomial is the irreducible
  modulus $M_{\operatorname{clog}_3 n}$, with degree between $2n$
  and $6n+2$. The output has exactly that degree in bits and denotes
  the condenser's modular Frobenius operation for the supplied count.
  This single composed program belongs to $\mathrm{FP}$ uniformly.
  The extension-field packing and complete seeded evaluator are
  connected to this operation in the results below.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Identify the generated coefficient list with $M_s$, then apply
  the modular-squaring semantics and degree invariant. The registered
  generator, pairing, and squaring certificates compose using
  \texttt{polytime}.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Binary presentation of the binomial tower]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-binary-extension}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.extensionModulus,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.binaryExtensionEquiv}
  \leanok
  \uses{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-binary-modulus}
  Write $K_s=\mathbb F_2[X]/(M_s)$ and let $t$ be its named root.
  For $d=3^v$, define $E_{s,v}(Z)=Z^d-t$ over $K_s$.
  The binary quotient $K_{s+v}$ is identified as an
  $\mathbb F_2$ algebra with $K_s[Z]/(E_{s,v})$.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Exact tower equivalence and coefficient laws]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-binary-extension}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.binaryModulus_comp_pow_three,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.extensionModulus_monic,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.extensionModulus_natDegree,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.extensionModulus_irreducible,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.binaryExtensionEquiv_root,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.binaryExtensionEquiv_symm_root,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.root_extensionModulus_pow,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.binaryExtensionEquiv_symm_of_root,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.binaryExtensionEquiv_mk,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.binaryExtensionEquiv_symm_of_mk,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.binaryExtensionEquiv_symm_mk}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-binary-extension}
  We have $M_s(X^{3^v})=M_{s+v}(X)$, and $E_{s,v}$ is monic
  and irreducible of degree $3^v$. If $w$ and $u$ are the roots
  of the binary and tower presentations, the equivalence sends
  $w$ to $u$ and sends the embedded $t$ back to $w^{3^v}$.
  It transports every polynomial evaluation with these same
  coefficient and generator maps. All statements include $s=0$
  and $v=0$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Substitute the power in the explicit trinomial. Mathlib's
  \texttt{AdjoinRoot.compAlgEquiv} identifies a quotient by
  $f(g(X))$ with the tower obtained by adjoining a root of
  $g(X)-\operatorname{root}(f)$. Transport along the two polynomial
  equalities. The noncube root and Kummer theorem give irreducibility;
  evaluation naturality gives the coefficient laws.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Runtime transposition of coefficient bits]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-bit-transpose}
  \lean{Complexity.BitPolynomial.transposeBits,
    Complexity.BitPolynomial.transposeEval}
  \leanok
  A row-major $d$ by $b$ bit matrix is transposed by sending
  input position $jb+i$ to output position $id+j$. The output
  has exactly $db$ bits. Absent input bits are false; excess bits
  are ignored. Both dimensions are supplied as unary word lengths.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Exact polynomial-time transposition]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-bit-transpose}
  \lean{Complexity.BitPolynomial.transposeBits_length,
    Complexity.BitPolynomial.transposeBits_zero_rows,
    Complexity.BitPolynomial.transposeBits_zero_cols,
    Complexity.BitPolynomial.transposeBits_getD_coord,
    Complexity.BitPolynomial.transposeBits_transposeBits,
    Complexity.BitPolynomial.transposeBits_transposeBits_of_length,
    Complexity.BitPolynomial.transposeEval_pair,
    Complexity.BitPolynomial.transposeEval_length_le,
    Complexity.BitPolynomial.transposeEval_mem_FP,
    Complexity.BitPolynomial.transposeBits_mem_FP}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-bit-transpose}
  The coordinate map is exact, and transposing twice recovers
  the input truncated or zero-padded to $db$ bits. For exact-length
  inputs it is an inverse. The total runtime evaluator belongs
  to $\mathrm{FP}$ and has a quadratic output bound. Either zero
  dimension gives the empty list.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Quotient and remainder recover the two matrix indices. Their
  inverse laws prove the round trip. Unary arithmetic and bit access
  compute each output bit through the polynomial-time bitwise closure.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[The complete encoded polynomial condenser]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-encoded-condenser}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.condenserCoordinateBits,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.condenserBits,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.condenserEval,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.sourcePolynomial,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.decodedCondenser}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-bit-transpose,
    def:lowerbounds-algebraic-bit-horner,
    def:lowerbounds-algebraic-bit-trinomial,
    def:lowerbounds-algebraic-binary-extension}
  The runtime inputs are a coefficient word, unary half-degree,
  unary extension degree, seed word, unary stride, and unary
  output-coordinate count. Transpose the source coefficient blocks,
  square modulo the larger generated binary modulus, transpose back,
  and evaluate the blocks by Horner modulo the base modulus.
  Coordinate $i$ uses $ri$ squarings; concatenate the coordinates.

  For semantic comparison, interpret the first $d$ blocks of width
  $b=2\cdot3^s$ as a source polynomial over $K_s$. The decoded map
  canonically encodes a field seed before running the program and
  decodes each output block. The runtime output omits the seed;
  strong distributional comparisons retain it externally.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[One uniform polynomial-time condenser program]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-encoded-condenser-fp}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.condenserCoordinateBits_length,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.condenserBits_length,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.coefficientBlock_condenserBits,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.condenserEval_pair,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.condenserEval_length_le,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.condenserCoordinateBits_mem_FP,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.condenserBits_mem_FP,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.condenserEval_mem_FP}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-encoded-condenser,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-bit-transpose,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-bit-horner,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-bit-frobenius,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-bit-trinomial}
  Every coordinate has exactly twice the half-degree word length
  in bits. The concatenation has $mb$ bits, and taking consecutive
  blocks recovers the coordinates in order. The single paired-input
  evaluator belongs to $\mathrm{FP}$ uniformly in all six runtime
  words and has output length at most twice the square of its
  encoded input length, even for malformed data or zero dimensions.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  The generated trinomial has a true leading coefficient at the
  prescribed degree. Every Horner result has that fixed width.
  Compose the registered arithmetic and transposition certificates,
  then apply polynomial-time concatenation over the unary output range.
  The stride times coordinate index is also a polynomial-time unary value.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[The encoded program computes the proved polynomial map]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-encoded-condenser-correct}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.sourcePolynomial_degree_lt,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.binaryExtensionEquiv_decode_transpose,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.decode_blockEvalBits,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.sourcePolynomial_unpacked_frobenius,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.decode_condenserCoordinateBits,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.condenserBits_correct,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.condenserEval_correct,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.decodedCondenser_eq}
  \leanok
  \uses{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-encoded-condenser-fp,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-binary-extension,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-binary-codec,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-polynomial-condenser}
  If the half-degree and extension-degree words have lengths $3^s$
  and $3^v$, the decoded output vector is exactly
  $\operatorname{polynomialCondenser}(E_{s,v},r,m,f,y)$.
  This applies to the actual paired-input $\mathrm{FP}$ evaluator,
  with arbitrary source and seed word lengths. Missing source bits
  are zero and excess source bits are ignored; the semantic source
  has degree below $3^v$. The statement includes zero exponents,
  zero stride, and an empty output vector.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  A coefficient bit contributes $t^iu^j=u^{3^vi+j}$, which is
  exactly the transposition index. Reindexing the finite coefficient
  sum proves the packing law. Binary modular squaring preserves the
  quotient power and has the exact width needed for inverse
  transposition. Both resulting extension polynomials have degree
  below the monic modulus, so equality of their quotient classes
  implies polynomial equality. Horner decoding and block extraction
  then prove the complete vector statement.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Source packing preserves every fixed-length support]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-encoded-source-injective}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.sourcePolynomial_inj_of_length,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.sourcePolynomial_injectiveOn_length}
  \leanok
  \uses{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-encoded-condenser-correct,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-bit-transpose}
  For every $n\le(2\cdot3^s)3^v$, source-polynomial encoding is
  injective on bit lists of length $n$, including a partial final
  coefficient block and $n=0$. Thus zero padding does not merge
  distinct points of a fixed-length flat source.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Equal source polynomials give equal classes in the larger binary
  quotient. The canonical-width codec recovers equal packed words.
  Transposing twice recovers equal padded inputs, and their common
  original length recovers the original words.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Source linearity of the actual encoded program]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-encoded-condenser-linear}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.sourcePolynomial_addBits,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.sourcePolynomial_replicate_false,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.sourcePolynomial_nil,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.decodedCondenser_addBits,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.decodedCondenser_replicate_false,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.decodedCondenser_nil}
  \leanok
  \uses{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-encoded-condenser-correct,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-polynomial-condenser}
  Padded coefficientwise XOR represents addition of source polynomials.
  At every field seed, the decoded runtime map preserves this addition
  and sends each all-zero source to zero. No source-length or capacity
  hypothesis is needed, and unequal source lengths are allowed.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Decode each coefficient block using the binary addition law and apply
  the polynomial map's characteristic-two additivity. The exact program
  identity transfers the result to the computed output.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Lossless condensation by the encoded program]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-encoded-condenser-lossless}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.decodedCondenser_flat_lossless,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.decodedCondenser_flat_mixture}
  \leanok
  \uses{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-encoded-source-injective,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-encoded-condenser-correct,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-flat-lossless,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-lossless-mixture}
  Let $b=2\cdot3^s$, $d=3^v$ with $v>0$, $r\le b$, and $n\le bd$.
  On every nonempty flat support of $n$-bit words with at most $2^{rm}$
  points, the decoded runtime program has a seedwise injective ideal
  map, with joint seed-output test error at most
  \[
    \frac{(d-1)(2^r-1)m}{2^b}.
  \]
  For any $0<K\le2^{rm}$ and a larger flat support with at least $K$
  points, the same bound holds against an explicit mixture of seedwise
  $K$-flat witnesses. Irreducibility and the field cardinality are proved
  for the generated moduli; they are not external hypotheses.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Injective source encoding preserves every subset's cardinality. The
  exact program identity identifies its neighbors with the polynomial
  neighbors. Apply expansion, the seedwise injection theorem, and the
  exact-$K$ subset averaging identity for larger supports.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Explicit finite condenser parameters]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-explicit-condenser-parameters}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.explicitCondenserBudget,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.explicitCondenserExtensionExponent,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.explicitCondenserExtensionDegree}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-sparse-condenser-parameters}
  Given source length $n$, entropy budget $k$, inverse-error exponent $e$,
  and rate parameter $u$, set
  $T=e+\lceil\log_2(9(n+1)(k+1))\rceil+1$.
  Retain the sparse choices $b=2\cdot3^s$ and $r=b-T$.
  Set $v=\lceil\log_3\max(3,\lceil n/b\rceil)\rceil$,
  $d=3^v$, and $m=\lceil k/r\rceil$, with total natural-number
  division in degenerate cases. These rounding choices are deductions
  in this formalization from the credited sparse rate and loss bounds.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Capacity, rate, error budget, and uniform parameter generation]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-explicit-condenser-parameters}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.explicitCondenserBudget_pos,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.explicitCondenserFieldBits_pos,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.explicitCondenserPowerBits_pos,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.explicitCondenserExtensionExponent_pos,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.explicitCondenser_degree_lower,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.explicitCondenser_source_capacity,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.explicitCondenser_degree_le,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.explicitCondenser_entropy_capacity,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.explicitCondenser_fieldBits_le,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.explicitCondenser_output_rate,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.explicitCondenser_error_budget,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.explicitCondenserBudget_unaryFn,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.sparseFieldExponent_unaryFn,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.sparseFieldHalfDegree_unaryFn,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.sparseFieldBits_unaryFn,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.sparsePowerBits_unaryFn,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.condenserCoordinates_unaryFn,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.explicitCondenserExtensionExponent_unaryFn,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.explicitCondenserExtensionDegree_unaryFn}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-explicit-condenser-parameters,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-sparse-condenser-parameters}
  The budget, field width, and extension exponent are positive, and
  \[
    n\le bd,\qquad 3\le d\le9(n+1),\qquad
    b\le6(u+1)T,\qquad umb\le(u+1)k+ub,\qquad
    dk\,2^e\le2^T.
  \]
  For $u>0$, also $r>0$ and $k\le rm$. Every parameter, including
  $3^s$ and $3^v$, has a registered unary polynomial-time certificate
  when its inputs do. All finite inequalities include zero source
  length, zero entropy, and zero inverse-error exponent.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Ceiling division gives capacity, and rounding up to a power of three
  loses at most a factor of three. The logarithmic budget pays for the
  extension-degree times entropy bound. Unary arithmetic and bounded
  power-of-ceiling-logarithm computation generate the parameters;
  no general polynomial-time exponentiation rule is used.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[The scheduled binary condenser]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-explicit-condenser-program}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.explicitCondenserBits,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.explicitCondenserEval,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.decodedExplicitCondenser}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-explicit-condenser-parameters,
    def:lowerbounds-algebraic-encoded-condenser}
  Compute the explicit dimensions from $n,k,e,u$ and run the binary
  condenser on source and seed words. The paired evaluator takes the
  four numerical parameters as unary word lengths. The decoded map
  canonically encodes a field seed and reads the computed coordinates.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[The complete scheduled program is linear and polynomial-time]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-explicit-condenser-program}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.explicitCondenserBits_length,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.explicitCondenserBits_output_rate,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.explicitCondenserEval_pair,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.decodedExplicitCondenser_eq,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.decodedExplicitCondenser_addBits,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.decodedExplicitCondenser_replicate_false,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.explicitCondenserBits_mem_FP,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.explicitCondenserEval_mem_FP}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-explicit-condenser-program,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-explicit-condenser-parameters,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-encoded-condenser-linear,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-encoded-condenser-fp}
  One $\mathrm{FP}$ evaluator computes the parameters and all output bits.
  Its output has exactly $mb$ bits and satisfies the selected rate bound.
  Its decoded output equals the polynomial condenser and preserves source
  XOR for each seed. These program identities require no source-length
  promise or positive-parameter hypothesis.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Instantiate the exact runtime semantics and source-linearity laws.
  Compose the registered unary parameter certificates with the uniform
  condenser certificate using the polynomial-time tactic.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[The scheduled program meets the requested lossless error]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-explicit-condenser-lossless}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.explicitCondenser_loss,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.decodedExplicitCondenser_flat_lossless,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.decodedExplicitCondenser_flat_mixture}
  \leanok
  \uses{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-explicit-condenser-program,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-explicit-condenser-parameters,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-encoded-condenser-lossless}
  For $u>0$, the actual scheduled program on every nonempty flat
  $n$-bit support of size at most $2^k$ is within $2^{-e}$ of a
  seedwise flat witness of the same support size. On every flat
  support of size at least $2^k$, it is within $2^{-e}$ of an explicit
  mixture of seedwise $2^k$-flat witnesses. Every test retains the seed.
  No field, capacity, stride, or error-budget inequality is supplied by
  the caller. The following one-shot extraction layer composes this
  finite lossless guarantee with linear hashing; the recursive extractor
  and correlation-breaker construction remains separate.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Use $dk\,2^e\le2^T$ in the sparse normalized loss bound. The selected
  dimensions supply the degree and source capacity hypotheses, and
  $k\le rm$ gives the entropy capacity. Apply the program's flat and
  mixture guarantees with the resulting error bound.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Strong extraction from finite flat sources]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-strong-extractor}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.uniformSeededTestProb,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.FlatStrongSeededExtractor}
  \leanok
  For a finite seed set $S$ and output set $\Omega$, strong extraction
  compares $(Y,E(X,Y))$ with independent uniform seed and output on every
  test $T\subseteq S\times\Omega$. The source $X$ is uniform on any
  nonempty finite support $P$ of size at least $K$. The uniform test
  probability is $|T|/(|S||\Omega|)$, and the permitted discrepancy is
  $\epsilon$. Nonemptiness of $P$ remains explicit when $K=0$.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Forgetting the seed preserves weak extraction]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-strong-extractor-forget}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.FlatStrongSeededExtractor.mono,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.FlatStrongSeededExtractor.flatSeededExtractor}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-strong-extractor,
    def:lowerbounds-algebraic-extractor-sampler}
  Strong extraction is preserved by increasing the support threshold or
  permitted error. For nonempty seed and output sets, it implies the
  existing output-only flat seeded-extractor contract.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Restrict the source quantifier and increase the error bound. For weak
  extraction, apply the strong bound to product tests $S\times U$ and
  clear the positive seed and output cardinalities. The empty support
  satisfies the denominator-free weak inequality directly.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Collision universality]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-universal-hashing}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.UniversalHashFamily}
  \leanok
  A family $H:A\times S\to\Omega$ is universal when every pair of
  distinct inputs collides on at most $|S|/|\Omega|$ seeds. The definition
  uses the integer inequality
  \[
    |\{y:H(x,y)=H(x',y)\}|\,|\Omega|\le |S|.
  \]
  It does not require pairwise independence or uniform output at each
  fixed input; linear hash families all send the zero input to zero.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[The sharp finite retained-seed leftover-hash bound]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-leftover-hashing}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.UniversalHashFamily.comp_injective,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.UniversalHashFamily.seededTestProb_sub_uniform_sq_le,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.UniversalHashFamily.flat_test_sq_le,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.UniversalHashFamily.flatStrongSeededExtractor}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-universal-hashing,
    def:lowerbounds-algebraic-strong-extractor}
  Suppose the seed and output sets are nonempty. For a universal family,
  every joint test on a nonempty flat support of cardinality $N$ has
  squared discrepancy at most $M/(4N)$, where $M=|\Omega|$. Consequently,
  $K>0$, $\epsilon\ge0$, and $M\le4\epsilon^2K$ imply strong extraction
  with threshold $K$ and error $\epsilon$. Restricting the input along an
  injection preserves universality. This is the standard leftover-hash
  argument, in the finite test form used here; see Guruswami--Umans--Vadhan,
  Lemma 5.1, and Barak et al., \emph{Leftover Hash Lemma, Revisited}, Lemma 1.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Double-count squared output-fiber sizes over ordered pairs of source
  points. Separate the diagonal pairs and use universality on distinct
  pairs. Center the fiber counts and apply Cauchy--Schwarz to a centered
  test indicator, whose variance is at most $1/4$. Normalize by the exact
  source and seed cardinalities.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Strong extraction after a conditional flat mixture]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-strong-composition}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.seededTestProb_comp_of_flat_mixture,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.FlatStrongSeededExtractor.comp_of_flat_mixture}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-strong-extractor,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-explicit-condenser-lossless}
  Let $K>0$. Suppose the retained-seed output of $C$ is within $\delta$
  of a supplied finite mixture with nonnegative normalized weights.
  Conditional on the component and condenser seed, its ideal output is
  uniform on the image of an injection from a set of size at least $K$.
  If $H$ is a strong $(K,\epsilon)$ extractor, then
  \[
    E(x,(y,a))=H(C(x,y),a)
  \]
  has error at most $\delta+\epsilon$, retaining both independent seeds.
  The witness supports may depend on the component and on $y$; the actual
  map $C$ need not be injective. The subset-mixture wrapper matches the
  lossless condenser's witnesses on every larger flat support. This is a
  finite formulation of the composition in Guruswami--Umans--Vadhan,
  Proposition 4.5 and Remark 5.15, and Chattopadhyay--Goodman--Liao,
  Lemma 4.9.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Fix the fresh seed $a$ and pull each composed test back to a joint
  condenser test; averaging preserves its error $\delta$. Next fix the
  condenser seed and apply strong extraction to each ideal image support.
  Average the component errors using the normalized weights, then apply
  the triangle inequality. Every probability counts source occurrences
  before dividing, so repeated actual outputs retain their multiplicities.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Multiplication followed by an additive projection]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-field-hashing}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.fieldHash}
  \leanok
  For a field $F$, additive group $\Omega$, and additive map
  $\pi:F\to\Omega$, set $H(x,a)=\pi(ax)$. The multiplier $a$ is the
  hash seed. Coefficient truncation gives the standard field hash of
  Guruswami--Umans--Vadhan, Lemma 5.1.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Universal additive field hashing]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-field-hashing}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.fieldHash_add,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.fieldHash_zero,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.fieldHash_collision_iff,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.fieldHash_collision_count,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.fieldHash_universal}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-field-hashing,
    def:lowerbounds-algebraic-universal-hashing}
  Every fixed-seed map is additive and sends zero to zero. If $F$ and
  $\Omega$ are finite and $\pi$ is surjective, distinct inputs collide
  on exactly $|F|/|\Omega|$ seeds; hence the family is universal.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Collisions are exactly the seeds satisfying $\pi(a(x-x'))=0$.
  For distinct inputs, multiplication by $x-x'$ permutes $F$.
  Surjectivity gives equal-size fibers of $\pi$, so its kernel has the
  stated cardinality.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[The encoded binary multiply-and-prefix hash]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-binary-hashing}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.binaryCoefficientPrefix,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.binaryFieldHash,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.binaryHashBits,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.binaryHashEval,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.decodedBinaryHash}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-field-hashing}
  Over the explicit field $F_s=\mathbf F_2[X]/(M_s)$ of bit width
  $b=2\cdot3^s$, project to the first $\ell$ coefficients.
  The runtime hash multiplies two supplied bit words, reduces modulo the
  generated binary trinomial, and takes the requested prefix. Its paired
  evaluator receives source, seed, modulus half-degree, and output count
  as runtime words. The decoded map uses a canonically encoded field seed.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Correct and uniformly polynomial-time binary hashing]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-binary-hashing}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.binaryCoefficientPrefix_surjective,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.binaryFieldHash_collision_count,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.binaryFieldHash_universal,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.binaryField_decode_inj_of_length,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.binaryHashBits_correct,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.decodedBinaryHash_eq,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.decodedBinaryHash_addBits,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.decodedBinaryHash_universal,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.decodedBinaryHash_flatStrongSeededExtractor,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.binaryHashBits_length,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.binaryHashEval_pair,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.binaryHashEval_length_le,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.binaryHashBits_mem_FP,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.binaryHashEval_mem_FP}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-binary-hashing,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-field-hashing,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-leftover-hashing}
  If $\ell\le b$, the coefficient projection is surjective and distinct
  field inputs collide on exactly a $2^{-\ell}$ fraction of seeds.
  Decoding is injective on source words of every fixed length $n\le b$,
  so the actual decoded hash strongly extracts from such flat sources
  whenever $2^\ell\le4\epsilon^2K$, $K>0$, and $\epsilon\ge0$.
  With a valid half-degree word, the runtime output length is
  $\min(\ell,b)$ and its coefficient vector is the semantic field hash.
  Padded source XOR is preserved for each fixed seed, including unequal
  source lengths. The total paired evaluator is in $\mathrm{FP}$ uniformly
  in all four runtime words and has output length bounded by input length.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Represent any prefix by its polynomial below degree $b$, then use field
  hashing and the fixed-length coefficient codec. Correctness of carryless
  multiplication, runtime remainder, and prefix truncation identifies the
  computed map. Compose their registered polynomial-time certificates.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Serialization of condenser coordinates]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-condenser-serialization}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.encodeCondenserOutput,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.decodeCondenserOutput,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.condenserOutputEquiv}
  \leanok
  A vector of $m$ elements of $F_s$ is encoded by concatenating its $m$
  canonical blocks of width $b=2\cdot3^s$. Decoding reads those blocks
  in the same order. The resulting equivalence is between coordinate
  vectors and bit words of length exactly $mb$, including high zero bits.
  These semantic maps describe the existing runtime output layout.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Serialization preserves coordinates and the actual output]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-condenser-serialization}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.encodeCondenserOutput_length,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.coefficientBlock_encodeCondenserOutput,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.decode_encodeCondenserOutput,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.encode_decodeCondenserOutput,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.encodeCondenserOutput_injective,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.decodeCondenserOutput_addBits,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.encodeCondenserOutput_add,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.encodeCondenserOutput_decodedCondenser,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.encodeCondenserOutput_decodedExplicitCondenser}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-condenser-serialization,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-explicit-condenser-program}
  Both codec round trips are exact at the prescribed width; serialization
  is injective and sends coordinate addition to bitwise XOR. Re-encoding
  the decoded condenser returns its exact original runtime output.
  The latter identity also holds for the scheduled program.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Identify each consecutive block with its field encoding and apply the
  field codec's inverse and addition laws coordinate by coordinate.
  Exact output length and the condenser's checked coordinate formula
  identify the full runtime word, including its zero padding.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[One-shot extraction parameters]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-one-shot-parameters}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.oneShotCondenserExponent,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.oneShotCondenserOutputBits,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.oneShotHashExponent}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-explicit-condenser-parameters}
  For source length $n$, output length $\ell$, and inverse-error exponent
  $e$, set $k=\ell+2e$. Condense with rate parameter $u=1$ and error
  exponent $e+1$. Write $b$ for its field width and $N$ for its output
  width. Choose the hashing field width $B=2\cdot3^{s_H}$ by sparse
  rounding above $\max(N,\ell)$.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Finite one-shot capacity, seed, and error bounds]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-one-shot-parameters}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.oneShotCondenserOutputBits_le,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.oneShotHashBits_source_capacity,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.oneShotHashBits_output_capacity,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.oneShotHashBits_le,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.oneShotSeedBits_le,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.oneShot_leftover_budget_eq,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.oneShot_leftover_budget,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.oneShot_half_errors,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.oneShotCondenserExponent_unaryFn,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.oneShotCondenserOutputBits_unaryFn,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.oneShotHashExponent_unaryFn,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.oneShotCondenserHalfDegree_unaryFn,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.oneShotHashHalfDegree_unaryFn}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-one-shot-parameters,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-explicit-condenser-parameters}
  The selected dimensions satisfy
  \[
    N\le2(\ell+2e)+b,\qquad N,\ell\le B\le6(N+\ell+1),
  \]
  \[
    b+B\le7b+12(\ell+2e)+6\ell+6.
  \]
  The exact sharp leftover-hash budget is
  $2^\ell=4(2^{-(e+1)})^2\,2^{\ell+2e}$, and the two half-errors
  sum to $2^{-e}$. All statements include zero parameters. All three
  parameter functions and both selected field half-degrees have uniform
  unary polynomial-time certificates; no general exponentiation rule is
  asserted.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Apply the condenser's rate bound at $u=1$ and the sparse rounding bounds
  to $\max(N,\ell)$. Handle the zero rounding target by the minimum
  two-bit field. The error identities follow from power arithmetic.
  The existing bounded power-rounding certificates compose with unary
  arithmetic to compute the actual half-degrees.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[The actual condense-then-hash program]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-one-shot-program}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.oneShotExtractorBits,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.oneShotExtractorEval,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.decodedOneShotExtractor}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-one-shot-parameters,
    def:lowerbounds-algebraic-binary-hashing,
    def:lowerbounds-algebraic-explicit-condenser-program}
  Run the scheduled condenser at $k=\ell+2e$, error exponent $e+1$,
  and $u=1$, then hash its complete output with an independent field seed.
  The paired evaluator takes source, both seeds, and unary words for
  $n,\ell,e$. It emits the output bits; the decoded statistical map
  retains both seeds externally for joint tests.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[One uniform linear extractor program]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-one-shot-program}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.oneShotExtractorBits_length,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.oneShotExtractorEval_pair,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.oneShotExtractorEval_length_le,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.oneShotExtractorBits_mem_FP,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.oneShotExtractorEval_mem_FP,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.decodedOneShotExtractor_eq_composition,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.decodedOneShotExtractor_addBits}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-one-shot-program,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-one-shot-parameters,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-condenser-serialization,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-binary-hashing}
  For all source and seed words the total program emits exactly $\ell$
  bits, and the paired evaluator belongs to $\mathrm{FP}$. Parameter
  generation is part of this single evaluator. At canonical field seeds,
  its decoded map is exactly condensation, serialization, and field hashing,
  and it preserves padded source XOR for every fixed pair of seeds.
  The latter identity does not require equal source lengths.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Hash-field capacity gives exact output width. The serialization identity
  connects the actual condenser word to the coordinate map used by hashing.
  Compose the two additivity laws and the registered polynomial-time
  certificates, including the unary parameter generators.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[The one-shot program strongly extracts from flat sources]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-one-shot-extraction}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.decodedOneShotExtractor_flat}
  \leanok
  \uses{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-one-shot-program,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-one-shot-parameters,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-strong-composition,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-leftover-hashing,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-explicit-condenser-lossless}
  For every finite support $P$ of $n$-bit words with
  $|P|\ge2^{\ell+2e}$, the actual decoded program produces $\ell$ bits
  with discrepancy at most $2^{-e}$ on every joint test retaining both
  independent uniform field seeds. The parameters and program are fixed
  before $P$ and the test are chosen. No capacity or numerical error
  assumption is supplied by the caller. This implements the finite
  condense-then-hash mechanism of Chattopadhyay--Goodman--Liao, Lemma 4.9,
  with the explicit sparse field schedule used here.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  The scheduled condenser is within $2^{-(e+1)}$ of its conditional
  $2^{\ell+2e}$-flat mixture. Serialization and fixed-width hashing-field
  decoding preserve each witness injection. Universality and the exact
  leftover budget give another $2^{-(e+1)}$ error. Apply mixture-aware
  strong composition and identify it with the actual program.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Finite probability weights and statistical distance]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-probability-weights}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.IsProbabilityWeight,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.CappedWeight,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.weightDist,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.weightTestProb,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.mapWeight,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.firstWeight,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.uniformWeight,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.flatWeight}
  \leanok
  A probability weight on a finite type is a nonnegative function $p$
  with $\sum_xp(x)=1$. Its threshold-$K$ cap is $Kp(x)\le1$ for every $x$.
  Statistical distance is $\frac12\sum_x|p(x)-q(x)|$; deterministic images
  sum the weights of all preimages. Marginals, uniform weights, and uniform
  weights supported on a finite subset use the same finite-sum conventions.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Exact decomposition of capped weights]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-capped-flat-mixture}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.exists_flat_mixture_of_capped_weights}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-probability-weights}
  If $K>0$ and $p$ is a probability weight with $Kp(x)\le1$, there are
  nonnegative weights $w_S$, indexed by the $K$-element subsets of the
  ambient type, such that $\sum_Sw_S=1$ and
  \[
    p(x)=\sum_S w_S\frac{\mathbf1_{x\in S}}{K}.
  \]
  No extra bound on the ambient cardinality is required.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Complete the capped probability vector to a doubly stochastic matrix.
  Apply Mathlib's Birkhoff--von Neumann theorem, formalized by Bhavik Mehta,
  and group the resulting permutation weights by their selected $K$-subset.
  At full ambient cardinality the source is already uniform. This is an
  exact finite existence argument for real weights, with no runtime claim.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Finite statistical-distance laws]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-weight-distance}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.weightDist_triangle,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.weightDist_le_one,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.weightDist_map_le,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.weightDist_le_iff_tests,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.weightDist_map_map_le,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.CappedWeight.eq_uniform}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-probability-weights}
  Statistical distance satisfies the triangle inequality and contracts
  under deterministic maps. Probability weights have distance at most one.
  For weights of equal total mass, distance at most $\varepsilon$ is
  equivalent to discrepancy at most $\varepsilon$ on every finite test.
  Two deterministic observations of one probability source have distance
  at most their disagreement probability. A normalized source capped at
  the full ambient cardinality is exactly uniform.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Use finite sums of absolute values, grouping fibers for deterministic
  images. Equal total mass identifies the positive discrepancy with half
  the absolute discrepancy. For uniformity, all deficits from uniform
  weights are nonnegative and sum to zero.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Weighted strong extraction and condensation]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-weighted-strong}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.weightedSeededTestProb,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.WeightedStrongSeededExtractor,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.weightedSeededOutput,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.seedFamilyWeight,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.WeightedStrongSeededCondenser}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-probability-weights,
    def:lowerbounds-algebraic-strong-extractor}
  A weighted strong extractor satisfies the retained-seed test bound for
  every normalized threshold-$K$ capped source. A weighted strong condenser
  at thresholds $K_{\rm in},K_{\rm out}$ sends each such input source within
  distance $\varepsilon$ of a family $q_y$ of normalized threshold-$K_{\rm out}$
  capped output weights, indexed by the seed. Both joint laws have a uniform
  seed marginal; the actual seed is independent of the input source.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Flat and weighted strong extraction agree]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-weighted-strong}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.weightedStrongSeededExtractor_iff_flat,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.weightedSeededTestProb_mixture}
  \leanok
  \uses{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-capped-flat-mixture,
    def:lowerbounds-algebraic-weighted-strong}
  For positive $K$, the flat and weighted strong-extractor contracts are
  equivalent, with identical error and seed parameters.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Seeded-test probabilities are linear in the source weights. Decompose a
  capped source into its exact-size flat components and average their
  errors. Conversely, the uniform weighting on a nonempty support is a
  normalized capped weighting.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Strong extraction under seed and output equivalences]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-weighted-strong-equiv}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.WeightedStrongSeededExtractor.equiv}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-weighted-strong}
  Replacing the seed and output alphabets by finite equivalent types
  preserves weighted strong extraction, with the same input threshold and
  error. The seed equivalence reindexes the uniform seed, and the output
  equivalence reindexes the ideal uniform output law.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Pull each retained-seed/output test back through the two equivalences.
  Bijections preserve finite uniform weights and the actual test sum.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Weighted strong condensation from flat witnesses]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-weighted-condenser}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.weightedStrongSeededCondenser_of_flat_injections,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.WeightedStrongSeededExtractor.weightedStrongSeededCondenser}
  \leanok
  \uses{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-capped-flat-mixture,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-weight-distance,
    def:lowerbounds-algebraic-weighted-strong}
  Suppose $K>0$ and every $K$-element input support has seedwise injective
  output witnesses within retained-seed test error $\varepsilon$. Then the
  same map is a weighted strong condenser at input and output threshold $K$,
  with the same error. A weighted strong extractor is a weighted strong
  condenser whose output threshold is the full output cardinality.
  Seeds are nonempty; the latter statement also assumes a nonempty output type.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Mix the injective images of the exact-size flat components at each seed.
  These conditional laws are normalized and capped. Average the test
  bounds and use the test characterization of statistical distance. For
  an extractor, take the uniform output law at every seed.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[The actual programs on arbitrary capped sources]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-weighted-programs}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.decodedExplicitCondenser_weighted,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.decodedExplicitCondenser_weighted_ofFn,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.decodedOneShotExtractor_weightedStrongSeededExtractor,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.decodedOneShotExtractor_ofFn_weighted}
  \leanok
  \uses{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-weighted-condenser,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-weighted-strong,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-explicit-condenser-lossless,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-one-shot-extraction}
  For every positive rate parameter $u$, on arbitrary distributions of
  $n$-bit inputs the scheduled condenser
  preserves point-mass cap $2^{-k}$ in its seedwise ideal output, with
  joint error $2^{-e}$. The one-shot program strongly extracts $\ell$ bits
  with error $2^{-e}$ from every source capped by $2^{-(\ell+2e)}$.
  The statements also permit any injective encoding into words of length
  $n$. These are the same actual programs with their previously proved
  uniform polynomial-time evaluators.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Transport each finite flat support through the input encoding and apply
  the checked flat guarantee. Then use the exact mixture transfers. For
  Boolean vectors, the encoding is \texttt{List.ofFn}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Injective output transport for strong condensers]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-condenser-output-transport}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.WeightedStrongSeededCondenser.map_output_injective}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-weighted-strong,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-weight-distance}
  An injective encoding of the output, allowed to depend on the retained
  seed, preserves both entropy thresholds and the error of a weighted
  strong condenser. The statement includes the empty seed type under
  the zero-mass convention.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Push each conditional witness through its injective encoding. Its cap
  is preserved, and deterministic contraction bounds its distance from
  the correspondingly encoded actual output. Average over the seed.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Two equal halves of the scheduled condenser output]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-explicit-pair-condenser}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.explicitCondenserHalfWidth,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Extractor.explicitCondenserPair}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-explicit-condenser-program}
  If the selected field width is $b=2\cdot3^s$ and the condenser has
  $c$ field coordinates, set $H=c3^s$. The paired map runs the actual
  scheduled bit program on the input and encoded field seed, then reads
  its first and second $H$ bits as two Boolean vectors.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[The actual condenser in pair-output form]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-explicit-pair-condenser}
