\begin{definition}[Truncated distance kernel]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-gaussian-kernel}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Gaussian.kernel,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Gaussian.unitKernel,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Gaussian.sphere,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Gaussian.ball}
  \leanok
  For a finite simple graph, a decay rate $q$, and a radius $R$, the kernel
  row of a vertex $v$ is $a_v(z)=q^{\operatorname{dist}(v,z)}$ when
  $\operatorname{dist}(v,z)\le R$ and $0$ otherwise. Its normalization is
  $\hat a_v=a_v/\lVert a_v\rVert$; the norm is at least one because
  $a_v(v)=1$.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Kernel correlation of adjacent vertices]
  \label{lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-gaussian-correlation}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Gaussian.card_sphere_le,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Gaussian.card_ball_le,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Gaussian.sum_kernel_mul_ge,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Gaussian.sum_unitKernel_mul_ge}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-gaussian-kernel}
  In a graph of maximum degree three, spheres of radius $d$ have at most
  $3\cdot 2^d$ vertices. For adjacent vertices $u,v$ and $q\ge 0$,
  \[
    \langle\hat a_u,\hat a_v\rangle\ \ge\ \frac{2q}{1+q^2}-3(2q^2)^R .
  \]
  No girth or expansion hypothesis is needed.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  The two distances to any vertex differ by at most one. If both kernel
  values are nonzero, their product is at least $\kappa=2q/(1+q^2)$ times
  the mean of their squares, with equality when the distances differ. If
  exactly one is nonzero, it lies on the truncation sphere of radius $R$.
  Summing, the defect is at most $(\kappa/2)q^{2R}$ per sphere vertex.
  Normalize using the arithmetic–geometric mean inequality and norms at
  least one.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Gaussian threshold crossings]
  \label{lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-gaussian-crossing}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Gaussian.gaussPi,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Gaussian.gaussPi_between_le,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Gaussian.gaussPi_window_le,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Gaussian.gaussPi_tail_le}
  \leanok
  Let $\omega$ have independent standard Gaussian coordinates and let
  $\alpha,\beta$ have equal norms with $\alpha+\beta\ne 0$. For every
  threshold $t$, the probability that $t$ separates
  $\langle\alpha,\omega\rangle$ and $\langle\beta,\omega\rangle$ is at most
  $(2/\pi)\lVert\beta-\alpha\rVert/\lVert\alpha+\beta\rVert$. A unit form lies
  in a window of width $\delta$ with probability at most
  $\delta/\sqrt{2\pi}$ and beyond $\pm T$ with probability at most $1/T^2$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  The forms $U=\langle\alpha+\beta,\omega\rangle$ and
  $V=\langle\beta-\alpha,\omega\rangle$ are uncorrelated jointly Gaussian,
  hence independent. A separated threshold forces $|U-2t|\le|V|$. Condition
  on $V$, bound the density of $U$ by its maximum, and use
  $\mathbb E|N(0,1)|=\sqrt{2/\pi}$. The tail is Chebyshev's inequality.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Second moment for local events]
  \label{lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-gaussian-second-moment}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Gaussian.pi_variance_sum_indicator_le,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Gaussian.pi_deviation_le}
  \leanok
  Under a product of probability measures, let each event of a finite
  family depend only on a finite set of coordinates, each set meeting at
  most $D$ of the sets. The number of occurring events has variance at most
  $|F|D$, and deviates from its mean by at least $c>0$ with probability at
  most $|F|D/c^2$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Indicators of events on disjoint coordinate sets are independent, so
  their covariance vanishes; every other covariance is at most one. Apply
  Chebyshev's inequality.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Gaussian cubic cutwidth]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-gaussian-cutwidth}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Gaussian.cutwidthCoefficient,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Gaussian.exists_cutwidthBound}
  \leanok
  \uses{lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-gaussian-correlation,
    lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-gaussian-crossing,
    lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-gaussian-second-moment}
  For every $\xi>0$, every sufficiently large simple cubic graph on $h$
  vertices has a vertex ordering all of whose prefix cuts have at most
  $\bigl(\tfrac{3}{\pi}(3-2\sqrt2)+\xi\bigr)h$ edges. The coefficient is
  about $0.16384$, below the $1/6$ of the Monien–Preis and Fomin–Høie
  bounds.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Order the vertices by Gaussian scores $\langle\hat a_v,\omega\rangle$.
  Fix thresholds $-T+i\delta$ with $M\delta=2T$. Every prefix lies between
  two consecutive threshold sets or inside a tail, so its cut is at most a
  threshold cut plus three times a window count, or three times a tail
  count. The expected threshold cut is at most
  $\tfrac32 h\cdot\tfrac2\pi\sqrt{(1-\rho_0)/(1+\rho_0)}$ with $\rho_0$ the
  correlation bound. All events are local, so each of the $2M+2$ counts
  deviates by $\varepsilon h$ with probability $O(1/h)$, and one sample
  keeps them all close to their means. As $q\uparrow 1/\sqrt2$ and
  $R\to\infty$, $\rho_0\to 2\sqrt2/3$, where
  $\sqrt{(1-\rho_0)/(1+\rho_0)}=3-2\sqrt2$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Sheppard's crossing bound]
  \label{lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-gaussian-sheppard}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Gaussian.gaussPi_abs_le_mul_abs,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Gaussian.gaussPi_between_le_arctan,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Gaussian.tanHalf,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Gaussian.two_mul_arctan_tanHalf,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Gaussian.gaussPi_between_le_arccos}
  \leanok
  \uses{lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-gaussian-crossing}
  For independent standard Gaussians, $|Z_1|\le k|Z_2|$ has probability
  $(2/\pi)\arctan k$. Consequently, for unit vectors $\alpha,\beta$ with
  $\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle=\rho>-1$, every threshold separates
  $\langle\alpha,\omega\rangle$ and $\langle\beta,\omega\rangle$ with probability at most
  $\arccos(\rho)/\pi$, with equality at threshold zero.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  The sum and difference forms are independent. A separated threshold forces the sum to
  lie within the absolute difference of twice the threshold; a symmetric unimodal density
  gives the interval centred at zero the most mass. The planar angle law and
  $2\arctan\tan(\theta/2)=\theta$ finish the computation.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Crossings away from the median]
  \label{lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-gaussian-decay}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Gaussian.prod_gaussianReal_abs_sub_le_abs_exp,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Gaussian.gaussPi_between_le_arctan_exp,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Gaussian.gaussPi_between_le_exp_arccos}
  \leanok
  \uses{lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-gaussian-sheppard}
  For unit vectors $\alpha,\beta$ with $\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle=\rho>-1$ and every
  threshold $t$, the threshold separates $\langle\alpha,\omega\rangle$ and
  $\langle\beta,\omega\rangle$ with probability at most
  $e^{-t^2/2}\arccos(\rho)/\pi$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Write the sum and difference forms in polar coordinates $(R,\psi)$ after rescaling. At
  radius $R$ a separating threshold forces $R\ge|t|$ and puts $|\psi|$ within half the angle
  $\arccos\rho$ of $\arccos(t/R)$, because the cosine is decreasing on $[0,\pi]$. So each
  circle contributes angle at most $2\arccos\rho$, and the radial tail
  $\int_{|t|}^\infty Re^{-R^2/2}\,dR=e^{-t^2/2}$ supplies the factor.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Angles in a star]
  \label{lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-gaussian-arccos-star}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Gaussian.sum_arccos_le,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Gaussian.sum_arccos_star_le}
  \leanok
  \uses{lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-gaussian-star}
  The function $\arccos$ is concave on $[0,1]$. For three unit vectors with inner products
  at least $\kappa\ge7/8$ with a unit vector, the sum of their pairwise angles over ordered
  pairs is at most $6\arccos\bigl((3\kappa^2-1)/2\bigr)$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Apply Jensen's inequality to the six ordered-pair correlations, whose average is at least
  $(3\kappa^2-1)/2$ by the star inequality.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Inner products in a star]
  \label{lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-gaussian-star}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Gaussian.le_inner_of_le_inner,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Gaussian.sum_inner_ordered_pairs_ge}
  \leanok
  If unit vectors $y_1,\dots,y_n$ satisfy $\langle y_j,x\rangle\ge\kappa\ge0$ for a
  unit vector $x$, then $\sum_{j\ne j'}\langle y_j,y_{j'}\rangle\ge n^2\kappa^2-n$ and
  each $\langle y_j,y_{j'}\rangle\ge4\kappa-3$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Expand $\lVert\sum_j y_j\rVert^2\ge\langle\sum_j y_j,x\rangle^2$, and sum the
  squares $(y-2x+y')^2\ge0$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Edge-score path decompositions]
  \label{lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-gaussian-edge-order}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Gaussian.EdgeStraddles,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Gaussian.indicator_edgeStraddles_le,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Gaussian.exists_pathDecomposition_of_edgeScore}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-ordering-hypotheses}
  List the edges of a simple cubic graph by a real score, and keep each vertex in
  the bags between its first and last incident edge. A vertex in the bag of an
  edge of score $t$ has incident edges scoring at most and at least $t$. With
  thresholds $a+i\delta$, if both tails hold few edges and every threshold has few
  vertices whose edge scores straddle it and few edges in the following window, every
  bag is small. A straddling vertex has at least four separated ordered pairs of
  incident edges.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Sort the edges with a stable merge sort. A bag vertex lies in a tail, straddles
  the threshold below the bag's score, or is an endpoint of an edge in the window.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Jumping the median band]
  \label{lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-gaussian-band-order}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Gaussian.exists_pathDecomposition_subset_edgeScore,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Gaussian.card_filter_edgeScore_le_window,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Gaussian.card_filter_edgeScore_le_of_grid,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Gaussian.card_filter_edgeScore_le_of_grids,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Gaussian.exists_pathDecomposition_of_bandJump}
  \leanok
  \uses{lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-gaussian-edge-order}
  Score the edges of a simple cubic graph and fix $c>0$. Call an edge low, band or high
  as its score lies below $-c$, in $[-c,c)$, or at least $c$, and split the band edges into
  blocks so that two band edges at a common vertex share a block. List the low edges by
  score, then the blocks one at a time in increasing order of the number of their vertices
  with a high edge minus the number with a low edge, then the high edges by score. Every bag
  of the resulting path decomposition has at most
  $\max(S(-c),S(c))+|V_j|$ vertices for some block $j$, or at most the number of vertices
  with incident scores on both sides of a threshold outside $[-c,c)$. Here $S(t)$ counts
  the vertices whose edge scores straddle $t$ and $V_j$ the vertices of block $j$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Give each block a common score inside $(-c,c)$, increasing with its imbalance, and use the
  edge-score decomposition of the modified scores. Between blocks a bag holds the band-free
  vertices with low and high edges, the vertices of finished blocks with a high edge, and
  those of unstarted blocks with a low edge. Every vertex lies in at most one block, so
  $S(-c)$ and $S(c)$ split into these sets, and processing blocks in order of imbalance keeps
  the middle count below $\max(S(-c),S(c))$ by an exchange argument.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Gaussian cubic pathwidth]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-gaussian-pathwidth}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Gaussian.frontierCoefficient,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Gaussian.exists_frontier_pathwidthBound,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Gaussian.frontierCoefficient_pos,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Gaussian.two_mul_frontierCoefficient_le,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Gaussian.one_add_inv_two_mul_frontierCoefficient}
  \leanok
  \uses{lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-gaussian-correlation,
    lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-gaussian-crossing,
    lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-gaussian-second-moment,
    lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-gaussian-sheppard,
    lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-gaussian-arccos-star,
    lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-gaussian-edge-order}
  For every $\xi>0$, every sufficiently large simple cubic graph on $h$ vertices
  has a path decomposition with bags of at most
  $\bigl(\tfrac{3}{2\pi}\arccos\tfrac{1+2\sqrt2}{4}+\xi\bigr)h+1$ vertices. The
  coefficient is about $0.14035$, well below the $1/6$ of the Fomin--H{\o}ie bound;
  twice it is at most $9/32$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Give each edge $uv$ the unit vector $y_{uv}=(\hat a_u+\hat a_v)/\lVert\hat a_u+\hat a_v\rVert$
  and the score $\langle y_{uv},\omega\rangle$, and decompose by edge scores. If
  adjacent kernel rows have correlation at least $\rho_0$, the edge vectors at a
  vertex have correlation at least $\kappa=\sqrt{(1+\rho_0)/2}$ with its row. A
  threshold separates two edge scores with probability at most $\arccos/\pi$ of their
  correlation, so by the angle lemma a vertex straddles a threshold with probability at
  most $\tfrac{3}{2\pi}\arccos\bigl((1+3\rho_0)/4\bigr)$.
  The grid, locality, and second-moment argument of
  Theorem~\ref{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-gaussian-cutwidth} control every bag of one
  sample, and $\rho_0\to2\sqrt2/3$.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Subcritical band clusters]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-gaussian-band-subcritical}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Gaussian.bandGraph,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Gaussian.bigBandEvent,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Gaussian.BandSubcritical}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-gaussian-kernel}
  Give each edge $uv$ of a finite simple graph the Gaussian edge score
  $\langle y_{uv},\omega\rangle$ with
  $y_{uv}=(\hat a_u+\hat a_v)/\lVert\hat a_u+\hat a_v\rVert$, built from the kernel with decay
  rate $q$ and radius $R$. For $c>0$ the band graph keeps the edges scoring in $[-c,c)$, and
  the band cluster of a vertex is its connected component in the band graph. The band
  hypothesis $\mathrm{BandSubcritical}(q,R,c)$ says that for every $\varepsilon>0$ some $K$
  makes the expected number of vertices whose band cluster has more than $K$ vertices at most
  $\varepsilon h$ on every simple cubic graph with $h$ vertices. This is an open percolation
  hypothesis, not a theorem of the library: it asserts that the band clusters of the
  edge-score field are subcritical uniformly over cubic graphs. Numerically it holds on the
  infinite cubic tree for $c<0.1826$, which appears to be the worst case.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Band-jump cubic pathwidth, conditional on the band hypothesis]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-gaussian-band-pathwidth}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Gaussian.exists_band_pathwidthBound,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Gaussian.two_mul_exp_mul_frontierCoefficient_le}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-gaussian-band-subcritical,
    lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-gaussian-band-order,
    lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-gaussian-decay,
    lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-gaussian-second-moment,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-gaussian-pathwidth}
  This result is conditional on the open band hypothesis of
  Definition~\ref{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-gaussian-band-subcritical}. Let $c>0$ and assume
  $\mathrm{BandSubcritical}(q,R,c)$ for every decay rate $0\le q<1/\sqrt2$ and every radius
  $R$. Then for every $\xi>0$, every sufficiently large simple cubic graph on $h$ vertices has
  a path decomposition with bags of at most $\bigl(e^{-c^2/2}p+\xi\bigr)h+1$ vertices, where
  $p=\tfrac{3}{2\pi}\arccos\tfrac{1+2\sqrt2}{4}$. Unconditionally, $2e^{-c^2/2}p\le5/18$ at
  $c=4/25$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Use the band-jump order of Lemma~\ref{lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-gaussian-band-order} for the
  edge scores of one Gaussian sample, with the band clusters as blocks. Every threshold $t$ of
  a low grid on $[-T,-c]$ or a high grid on $[c,T]$ has $|t|\ge c$, so by
  Lemma~\ref{lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-gaussian-decay} and the angle bound of
  Theorem~\ref{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-gaussian-pathwidth} a vertex straddles it with
  probability at most $e^{-c^2/2}\tfrac{3}{2\pi}\arccos\bigl((1+3\rho_0)/4\bigr)$; the
  second-moment bound of Lemma~\ref{lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-gaussian-second-moment} controls
  the grids and tails of one sample. Inside the band a bag also holds the vertices of one
  block. They lie in one band cluster, so there are at most $K$ of them, or they all lie in
  clusters of more than $K$ vertices; by the hypothesis and Markov's inequality the latter
  number less than $2\eta h$ with probability at least $1/2$. The cluster event is measurable
  because it depends only on the finitely many band indicators. Taking $K\le\varepsilon h$
  and $\rho_0\to2\sqrt2/3$ gives the coefficient. At $c=4/25$ use $2p\le9/32$ and
  $e^{-8/625}\le80/81$, which follows from $e^{x}\ge1+x$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Circuit coefficient from Gaussian layouts]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-gaussian-coefficient}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Multigraph.exists_orderingBound_gaussian,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Multigraph.exists_orderingBound_twenty_div_sixtyOne,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Gaussian.two_mul_cutwidthCoefficient_le,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Gaussian.one_add_inv_two_mul_cutwidthCoefficient,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Multigraph.exists_orderingBound_frontier,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Multigraph.exists_orderingBound_nine_div_thirtyTwo,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.eventually_lt_size_of_rectangleFree_gaussian,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.nondet_eventually_lt_size_of_rectangleFree_gaussian}
  \leanok
  \uses{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-gaussian-cutwidth,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-gaussian-pathwidth,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-ordering-bound,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-cutwidth-coefficient}
  For every $\eta>0$ some $C$ satisfies
  $\mathrm{OrderingBound}\bigl(\tfrac{6}{\pi}(3-2\sqrt2),\eta,C\bigr)$, from the
  Gaussian cutwidth layout, and some $C$ satisfies
  $\mathrm{OrderingBound}(2p,\eta,C)$ with
  $p=\tfrac{3}{2\pi}\arccos\tfrac{1+2\sqrt2}{4}$, from the Gaussian pathwidth
  decomposition; these coefficients are about $0.32768\le20/61$ and
  $0.28070\le9/32$. Every family as in
  Theorem~\ref{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-cutwidth-subexponential} therefore
  needs more than $\bigl(1+\pi/(3\arccos\tfrac{1+2\sqrt2}{4})-\varepsilon\bigr)n$ gates,
  about $(4.5625-\varepsilon)n$, for deterministic circuits and for
  nondeterministic circuits with arbitrarily many witness inputs.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Apply the cutwidth-to-ordering and pathwidth-to-ordering transports to
  Theorems~\ref{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-gaussian-cutwidth}
  and~\ref{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-gaussian-pathwidth}, and the general
  coefficient theorem with the pathwidth coefficient $p$, for which $1+1/(2p)$ equals
  $1+\pi/(3\arccos\tfrac{1+2\sqrt2}{4})$. The comparison with $20/61$ needs rational
  bounds on $\sqrt2$ and $\pi$; the comparison with $9/32$ also uses the fourth-order
  Taylor bound for the cosine at $3\pi/32$.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Superconcentrator]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-superconcentrator}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Multigraph.DirAdj,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Multigraph.IsDirWalk,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Multigraph.inDegree,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Multigraph.Superconcentrator}
  \leanok
  Direct every edge of a multigraph from its first to its second endpoint. A directed walk
  is a list of vertices in which each vertex is joined to the next by an edge directed towards
  it. A multigraph with distinct vertices $i_1,\dots,i_N$ (inputs) and $o_1,\dots,o_N$
  (outputs), $2N$ in all, is an $N$-superconcentrator when for every $k$, all $k$-sets $X$ of
  inputs and $Y$ of outputs are joined by $k$ vertex-disjoint directed walks from $X$ to $Y$.
  Every path is a walk, so this includes superconcentrators defined with paths.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Superconcentrator cut lemma]
  \label{lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-superconcentrator-cut}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Multigraph.Superconcentrator.exists_le_card_cut}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-superconcentrator}
  Every linear order of the vertices of an $N$-superconcentrator has a lower set whose cut has
  at least $N$ edges.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Take a lower set $S$ containing exactly $N$ of the $2N$ terminals, say $a$ inputs and $N-a$
  outputs; its complement contains $N-a$ inputs and $a$ outputs. The superconcentrator joins
  the $a$ inputs in $S$ to the $a$ outputs outside $S$ by vertex-disjoint directed walks, each
  of which leaves $S$ along an edge directed out of $S$, and the $N-a$ inputs outside $S$ to
  the $N-a$ outputs in $S$, each entering along an edge directed into $S$. Walks of one family
  are vertex-disjoint, so their crossing edges are distinct, and an edge directed out of $S$
  is not directed into it. No Menger theorem is needed.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Superconcentrators need $(5.5625-o(1))N$ edges]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-superconcentrator}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Multigraph.Superconcentrator.le_of_orderingBound,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Multigraph.Superconcentrator.le_of_orderingBound_of_connected,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Multigraph.Superconcentrator.eventually_le_card_edges_of_orderingBound,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Multigraph.Superconcentrator.eventually_le_card_edges,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Multigraph.Superconcentrator.eventually_fifty_div_nine_sub_mul_le_card_edges,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Multigraph.Superconcentrator.eventually_five_sub_mul_le_card_edges}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-superconcentrator,
    lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-superconcentrator-cut,
    def:lowerbounds-algebraic-ordering-hypotheses,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-ordering-bound,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-gaussian-coefficient}
  Assume $\mathrm{OrderingBound}(A,\eta,C)$ with $A+\eta\ge0$. Every $N$-superconcentrator
  with $N>0$ and $M$ edges satisfies
  $N\le(A+\eta)\max\{M-2N,0\}+3\log_2(2M)+C$, and a connected one with $V$ vertices satisfies
  $N\le(A+\eta)\max\{M-V,0\}+3\log_2(2M)+C$. Hence if $A>0$ and every $\eta>0$ admits such a
  $C$, then for every $\varepsilon>0$ and all large $N$ every $N$-superconcentrator has at
  least $(2+1/A-\varepsilon)N$ edges. With the edge-score coefficient
  $A=2p$, $p=\tfrac{3}{2\pi}\arccos\tfrac{1+2\sqrt2}{4}$, this is
  $\bigl(2+\pi/(3\arccos\tfrac{1+2\sqrt2}{4})-\varepsilon\bigr)N\approx(5.5625-\varepsilon)N$
  edges, and at least $(50/9-\varepsilon)N$ since $2p\le9/32$. The coefficient $A=1/3$ of the
  Fomin--H{\o}ie bound gives $(5-\varepsilon)N$, the bound of Lev and Valiant
  (\emph{Size bounds for superconcentrators}, Theoretical Computer Science, 1983).
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Every input reaches every output, so all terminals lie in one undirected component. Replace
  each vertex of that component by a directed path with one vertex per end of an edge with
  its tail in the component, the ends of entering edges first. Each path vertex meets at most
  two path edges and one original edge, no edge is a loop, the result is connected, and its
  edges minus vertices are the component's. A directed walk enters a vertex at an entering
  end, from which its path reaches every leaving end, so vertex-disjoint walks lift to
  vertex-disjoint walks; with each input at the first vertex of its path and each output at
  the last, the result is a superconcentrator. Its ordering has prefix cuts of at most
  $(A+\eta)\max\{M'-V',0\}+3\log_2V'+C$ edges, and one has at least $N$ by
  Lemma~\ref{lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-superconcentrator-cut}. Here $M'-V'\le M-2N$, since
  the component contains the $2N$ terminals, and $V'\le2M$. If $M<(2+1/A-\varepsilon)N$, then
  at slack $\eta=A^2\varepsilon/2$ the right side is at most
  $(1-A\varepsilon/2)N+O(\log N)$, which is less than $N$ for large $N$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Superconcentrators of in-degree two]
  \label{cor:lowerbounds-algebraic-superconcentrator-in-degree}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Multigraph.Superconcentrator.le_of_orderingBound_of_inDegree,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Multigraph.Superconcentrator.eventually_le_card_vertices_of_orderingBound,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Multigraph.Superconcentrator.eventually_le_card_vertices}
  \leanok
  \uses{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-superconcentrator}
  Let the inputs have in-degree zero and every vertex in-degree at most two. Under the
  hypotheses of Theorem~\ref{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-superconcentrator}, for every
  $\varepsilon>0$ and all large $N$ every such $N$-superconcentrator has at least
  $(1+1/A-\varepsilon)N$ vertices that are not inputs. This is $(4-\varepsilon)N$ for
  $A=1/3$, the corresponding bound of Lev and Valiant, and
  $\bigl(1+\pi/(3\arccos\tfrac{1+2\sqrt2}{4})-\varepsilon\bigr)N\approx(4.5625-\varepsilon)N$
  for the edge-score coefficient.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Count in the component of the terminals. Its edges enter non-input vertices, at most two
  each; if $S$ is the set of non-input vertices met by an edge of the component, the
  component has at most $2|S|$ edges and at least $N+|S|$ vertices. Its edges minus vertices
  is therefore at most $|S|-N\le(V-N)-N$, and it has at most $2(V-N)$ edges, so the argument
  of Theorem~\ref{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-superconcentrator} applies with $(V-N)-N$ in place
  of $M-2N$.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Concentrator]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-concentrator}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Multigraph.Concentrator}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-superconcentrator}
  A multigraph with distinct vertices $i_1,\dots,i_n$ (inputs) and $o_1,\dots,o_m$ (outputs),
  $n+m$ in all, is an $(n,m)$-concentrator when every set $X$ of at most $m$ inputs is joined
  to some $|X|$ outputs by $|X|$ vertex-disjoint directed walks, one from each input of $X$; the
  outputs are not prescribed, and the walks may pass through other inputs and outputs. Every
  path is a walk, so this includes the concentrators of Pinsker (\emph{On the complexity of a
  concentrator}, 7th International Teletraffic Congress, 1973), defined with paths. Parallel
  edges are counted separately.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Concentrator cut lemma]
  \label{lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-concentrator-cut}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Multigraph.Concentrator.exists_le_card_cut}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-concentrator}
  Every linear order of the vertices of an $(n,m)$-concentrator has a lower set whose cut has
  at least $\min(m,n-m)$ edges.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Let $h=\min(m,n-m)$, take a lower set $S$ containing exactly $h$ inputs, and let $b$ outputs
  lie in $S$. As $h\le m$, the $h$ inputs in $S$ are joined to distinct outputs by
  vertex-disjoint walks; at most $b$ of them end in $S$, and each of the others leaves $S$ along
  an edge directed out of $S$. As $n-h\ge m$, some $m$ inputs outside $S$ are joined to $m$
  distinct outputs, that is, to all of them; each of the $b$ walks ending in $S$ enters it along
  an edge directed into $S$. Walks of one family are vertex-disjoint, so their crossing edges are
  distinct, and the cut of $S$ has at least $(h-b)+b=h$ edges.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Concentrators need $n+m+(3.5625-o(1))\min(m,n-m)$ edges]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-concentrator}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Multigraph.Concentrator.add_le_card_edges_add_one,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Multigraph.Concentrator.le_of_orderingBound,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Multigraph.Concentrator.eventually_le_card_edges_of_orderingBound,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Multigraph.Concentrator.eventually_le_card_edges_half_of_orderingBound,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Multigraph.Concentrator.eventually_le_card_edges,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Multigraph.Concentrator.eventually_le_card_edges_half,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Multigraph.Concentrator.eventually_fiftyNine_div_eighteen_sub_mul_le_card_edges,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Multigraph.Concentrator.eventually_add_three_mul_le_card_edges,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Multigraph.Concentrator.eventually_three_sub_mul_le_card_edges}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-concentrator,
    lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-concentrator-cut,
    def:lowerbounds-algebraic-ordering-hypotheses,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-ordering-bound,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-gaussian-coefficient,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-superconcentrator}
  Let $1\le m<n$. Every $(n,m)$-concentrator with $M$ edges has $M\ge n+m-1$, and under
  $\mathrm{OrderingBound}(A,\eta,C)$ with $A+\eta\ge0$ it satisfies
  $\min(m,n-m)\le(A+\eta)\max\{M-n-m,0\}+3\log_2(2M)+C$. Hence if $A>0$ and every $\eta>0$
  admits such a $C$, then for every $\varepsilon>0$ and all large $n$, uniformly in $m$, every
  $(n,m)$-concentrator has at least $n+m+\min(m,n-m)/A-\varepsilon n$ edges. With the
  edge-score coefficient $A=2p$ this is
  $n+m+\kappa\min(m,n-m)-\varepsilon n$ edges, where
  $\kappa=\pi/(3\arccos\tfrac{1+2\sqrt2}{4})\approx3.5625$; with $\lfloor n/2\rfloor$ outputs
  it is $(3/2+\kappa/2-\varepsilon)n\approx(3.28-\varepsilon)n$ edges, at least
  $(59/18-\varepsilon)n$. The coefficient $A=1/3$ gives $n+m+3\min(m,n-m)-\varepsilon n$, and
  $(3-\varepsilon)n$ with $\lfloor n/2\rfloor$ outputs. Pinsker (1973) proved that every
  $(n,m)$-concentrator with $m\ge2$ has at least $2n-2$ edges; the bound here is larger, up to
  $\varepsilon n$, whenever $m\ge n/(1+\kappa)\approx0.22n$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  A set of vertices closed under adjacency that holds $a$ inputs holds at least $\min(a,m)$
  outputs, since the walks from its inputs stay in it. If neither the undirected component of
  the first input nor its complement held all $m$ outputs, each would hold no more inputs than
  outputs, giving $n\le m$; so the component holds every output, and then its complement holds
  no input. Every input reaches an output and every output is reached, so each terminal has an
  edge of the component at it. As in
  Theorem~\ref{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-superconcentrator}, splitting each vertex of the
  component into a directed path of edge ends gives a connected loopless concentrator of
  maximum degree three whose edges minus vertices are those of the component, at most $M-n-m$,
  with at most $2M$ vertices; vertex-disjoint walk families lift. Its ordering has prefix cuts
  of at most $(A+\eta)\max\{M-n-m,0\}+3\log_2(2M)+C$ edges, and one has at least $\min(m,n-m)$
  by Lemma~\ref{lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-concentrator-cut}. The split graph is connected, so it
  has at least its number of vertices minus one edges, which gives $M\ge n+m-1$. If
  $M<n+m+\min(m,n-m)/A-\varepsilon n$, then at slack $\eta=A^2\varepsilon/2$ either
  $\min(m,n-m)/A\ge\varepsilon n$ and the ordering bound fails by $(A\varepsilon/2)n-O(\log n)$,
  or $\min(m,n-m)>A(\varepsilon n-1)$ exceeds the logarithmic term, or $M<n+m-1$.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Acyclic multigraphs]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-acyclic}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Multigraph.Acyclic,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Multigraph.acyclic_iff_forall_not_transGen}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-superconcentrator}
  A multigraph is acyclic when some numbering of its vertices increases strictly along every
  edge, from its first endpoint to its second. A finite multigraph is acyclic exactly when no
  vertex reaches itself along a directed walk of positive length.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Eliminating an input of an acyclic concentrator]
  \label{lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-concentrator-elimination}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Multigraph.Concentrator.exists_eliminate}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-concentrator, def:lowerbounds-algebraic-acyclic}
  Let $G$ be an acyclic $(n+1,m)$-concentrator whose inputs have in-degree zero, with
  $2\le m\le n$. Removing at least two edges of $G$ and replacing its inputs leaves an acyclic
  $(n,m)$-concentrator on the same vertices and outputs whose inputs have in-degree zero.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Walks from inputs visit a vertex of in-degree zero only at their start. Every input has an
  edge leaving it. If some input has two, remove its edges and drop it. Otherwise every input
  $x$ has one edge, to its head, and distinct inputs have distinct heads, as two inputs with a
  common head cannot be routed together. If the head $u$ of some input $x$ is entered only by
  the edge from $x$, then $u$ is neither an input nor an output (every output is reached from
  $m$ inputs other than $x$); remove that edge and make $u$ an input in place of $x$, whose walks
  continue from $u$. Otherwise let $w$ be a first non-input vertex at an edge in a topological
  order. Edges entering $w$ come from inputs whose head is $w$, so there is at most one, and then
  $w$ would be a head entered only by it; so no edge enters $w$, $w$ is no output, and its edges
  can be removed. The last two steps keep the number of inputs and remove an edge, so induction
  on the number of edges concludes.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Acyclic concentrators need $2n+(2.5625-o(1))\min(m,n-m)$ edges]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-acyclic-concentrator}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Multigraph.Concentrator.le_of_orderingBound_of_acyclic,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Multigraph.Concentrator.two_mul_le_card_edges_add_min,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Multigraph.Concentrator.eventually_le_card_edges_of_acyclic_of_orderingBound,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Multigraph.Concentrator.eventually_le_card_edges_of_acyclic,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Multigraph.Concentrator.eventually_two_mul_add_two_mul_le_card_edges_of_acyclic}
  \leanok
  \uses{lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-concentrator-elimination,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-concentrator}
  Let $G$ be an acyclic $(n,m)$-concentrator with $M$ edges whose inputs have in-degree zero,
  with $2\le m<n$, and $h=\min(m,n-m)$. Then $M\ge2n-h-1$, and under
  $\mathrm{OrderingBound}(A,\eta,C)$ with $A+\eta\ge0$,
  $h\le(A+\eta)\max\{M-(2n-h),0\}+3\log_2(2M)+C$. Hence if $A>0$ and every $\eta>0$ admits such
  a $C$, then for every $\varepsilon>0$ and all large $n$, uniformly in $m$, every such $G$ has
  at least $2n+(1/A-1)h-\varepsilon n$ edges: with the edge-score coefficient
  $2n+(\kappa-1)h-\varepsilon n$, $\kappa=\pi/(3\arccos\tfrac{1+2\sqrt2}{4})\approx3.5625$, and
  with $A=1/3$, $2n+2h-\varepsilon n$. For $m\ge n/2$ this is
  Theorem~\ref{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-concentrator}; for $m<n/2$ it is larger, about $2.64n$
  rather than $2.14n$ at $m=n/4$, and it exceeds Pinsker's $2n-2$ whenever $h$ grows linearly.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  For $m\ge n/2$ apply Theorem~\ref{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-concentrator}, as
  $n+m=2n-h$. For $m<n/2$, eliminate $n-2m$ inputs by
  Lemma~\ref{lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-concentrator-elimination}, removing at least $2(n-2m)$
  edges, and apply Theorem~\ref{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-concentrator} to the resulting
  $(2m,m)$-concentrator with $M'\le M-2(n-2m)$ edges: $m\le(A+\eta)\max\{M'-3m,0\}+3\log_2(2M')+C$
  with $M'-3m\le M-(2n-m)$, and $M'\ge3m-1$ gives $M\ge2n-m-1$. The asymptotic form follows as
  for Theorem~\ref{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-concentrator}.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Hyperconcentrator]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-hyperconcentrator}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Multigraph.Hyperconcentrator,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Multigraph.Superconcentrator.hyperconcentrator}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-superconcentrator}
  A multigraph with distinct vertices $i_1,\dots,i_N$ (inputs) and $o_1,\dots,o_N$ (outputs),
  $2N$ in all, is an $N$-hyperconcentrator when for every $k$, every set of $k$ inputs is joined
  to the first $k$ outputs $o_1,\dots,o_k$ by $k$ vertex-disjoint directed walks. The walks may
  pass through other inputs and outputs, and every path is a walk, so this includes
  hyperconcentrators defined with paths. Every superconcentrator is a hyperconcentrator.
  Parallel edges are counted separately.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Hyperconcentrator cut lemma]
  \label{lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-hyperconcentrator-cut}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Multigraph.Hyperconcentrator.exists_le_card_cut}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-hyperconcentrator}
  Every linear order of the vertices of an $N$-hyperconcentrator has a lower set whose cut has
  at least $\lfloor N/2\rfloor$ edges.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Let $h=\lfloor N/2\rfloor$, take a lower set $S$ containing exactly $h$ inputs, and let $c$ of
  $o_1,\dots,o_h$ lie in $S$. The $h$ inputs in $S$ are joined to $o_1,\dots,o_h$ by
  vertex-disjoint walks, and the $h-c$ of them ending outside $S$ leave it along distinct edges
  directed out of $S$. The $N-h\ge h$ inputs outside $S$ are joined to $o_1,\dots,o_{N-h}$, among
  them $o_1,\dots,o_h$, and the $c$ walks ending in $S$ enter it along distinct edges directed
  into $S$. So the cut of $S$ has at least $h$ edges.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Hyperconcentrators need $(3.78-o(1))N$ edges]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-hyperconcentrator}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Multigraph.Hyperconcentrator.le_of_orderingBound,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Multigraph.Hyperconcentrator.le_of_orderingBound_of_inDegree,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Multigraph.Hyperconcentrator.eventually_le_card_edges_of_orderingBound,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Multigraph.Hyperconcentrator.eventually_le_card_vertices_of_orderingBound,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Multigraph.Hyperconcentrator.eventually_le_card_edges,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Multigraph.Hyperconcentrator.eventually_thirtyFour_div_nine_sub_mul_le_card_edges,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Multigraph.Hyperconcentrator.eventually_le_card_vertices,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Multigraph.Hyperconcentrator.eventually_twentyFive_div_nine_sub_mul_le_card_vertices,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Multigraph.Hyperconcentrator.eventually_seven_div_two_sub_mul_le_card_edges}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-hyperconcentrator,
    lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-hyperconcentrator-cut,
    def:lowerbounds-algebraic-ordering-hypotheses,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-ordering-bound,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-gaussian-coefficient,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-superconcentrator,
    cor:lowerbounds-algebraic-superconcentrator-in-degree}
  Assume $\mathrm{OrderingBound}(A,\eta,C)$ with $A+\eta\ge0$. Every $N$-hyperconcentrator with
  $N>0$ and $M$ edges satisfies $\lfloor N/2\rfloor\le(A+\eta)\max\{M-2N,0\}+3\log_2(2M)+C$;
  if its inputs have in-degree zero and its vertices in-degree at most two, then with $V$
  vertices also $\lfloor N/2\rfloor\le(A+\eta)\max\{(V-N)-N,0\}+3\log_2(4(V-N))+C$. Hence if
  $A>0$ and every $\eta>0$ admits such a $C$, then for every $\varepsilon>0$ and all large $N$
  every $N$-hyperconcentrator has at least $(2+1/(2A)-\varepsilon)N$ edges, and at least
  $(1+1/(2A)-\varepsilon)N$ non-input vertices under the degree conditions. With the edge-score
  coefficient $A=2p$ these are $(2+\kappa/2-\varepsilon)N\approx(3.78-\varepsilon)N$ edges, at
  least $(34/9-\varepsilon)N$, and $(1+\kappa/2-\varepsilon)N\approx(2.78-\varepsilon)N$
  non-input vertices, at least $(25/9-\varepsilon)N$, where
  $\kappa=\pi/(3\arccos\tfrac{1+2\sqrt2}{4})$. The coefficient $A=1/3$ gives
  $(7/2-\varepsilon)N$ edges.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Every input reaches $o_1$ and every output is reached, so all terminals lie in one undirected
  component and each has an edge of the component at it. Splitting the component as in
  Theorem~\ref{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-superconcentrator} gives a connected loopless
  hyperconcentrator of maximum degree three whose edges minus vertices are at most $M-2N$, with
  at most $2M$ vertices; its ordering has a prefix cut with at least $\lfloor N/2\rfloor$ edges
  by Lemma~\ref{lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-hyperconcentrator-cut}. Under the degree conditions
  the count of Corollary~\ref{cor:lowerbounds-algebraic-superconcentrator-in-degree} bounds the
  edges minus vertices by $(V-N)-N$. The connected split graph gives $M\ge2N-1$, and
  $\lfloor N/2\rfloor/A\ge N/(2A)-1/(2A)$; the asymptotic bound follows as for concentrators.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[The concrete family under a general ordering coefficient]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-explicit-coefficient}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.sourceReductionHardFamily_eventually_lt_size_of_orderingBound}
  \leanok
  \uses{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-uniform-hard-family,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-cutwidth-coefficient}
  Let $A>0$, and suppose that for every $\eta>0$ some $C$ satisfies
  $\mathrm{OrderingBound}(A,\eta,C)$. For every $\varepsilon>0$ and all
  sufficiently large $n$, every circuit computing
  $\mathrm{sourceReductionHardFamily}(n)$ has more than
  $(1+1/A-\varepsilon)n$ gates, with $n$ the full input length. The family
  is the one of Theorem~\ref{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-explicit-four-n},
  which is the case $A=1/3$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Apply the general-coefficient padded theorem at fixed error $35/72$,
  then shift its eventual statement from the unpadded index to the full
  input length.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[The concrete family needs more than $(41/9-\varepsilon)n$ gates]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-explicit-gaussian}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.sourceReductionHardFamily_eventually_lt_size_gaussian,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.sourceReductionHardFamily_eventually_lt_size_fortyOne_div_nine}
  \leanok
  \uses{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-explicit-coefficient,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-gaussian-coefficient}
  For every $\varepsilon>0$ and all sufficiently large $n$, every circuit
  computing $\mathrm{sourceReductionHardFamily}(n)$ over the full binary
  basis has more than $\bigl(1+\pi/(3\arccos\tfrac{1+2\sqrt2}{4})-\varepsilon\bigr)n$
  gates, about $(4.5625-\varepsilon)n$, with $n$ the full input length. In
  particular it has more than $(41/9-\varepsilon)n$ gates. The family, its
  polynomial-time evaluator, and the basis are those of
  Theorem~\ref{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-explicit-four-n}; no hypothesis
  remains.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Apply Theorem~\ref{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-explicit-coefficient} with
  the Gaussian ordering coefficient
  $A=\tfrac{3}{\pi}\arccos\tfrac{1+2\sqrt2}{4}$, or with its rational weakening
  $A=9/32$, for which $1+1/A=41/9$.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Finite commutative aggregate gates and their budget]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-aggregate-budget}
  \lean{Algebraic.Aggregate.Op, Algebraic.Aggregate.signature,
    Algebraic.Aggregate.interpretation, Algebraic.Aggregate.budget,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.AggregateNetwork}
  \leanok
  A mixed circuit contains arbitrary binary Boolean gates and special gates of the form
  $h_j(\prod_i \phi_{ji}(z_i))$, where $M_j$ is a finite commutative monoid,
  $\phi_{ji}:\{0,1\}\to M_j$ is a slot-dependent contribution, and
  $h_j:M_j\to\{0,1\}$ is arbitrary. Fan-in, fanout, depth, repeated slots, and
  special-to-special connections are unrestricted. For the actual $q$ special
  occurrences, define
  \[
    D=q+\sum_{j=1}^q\lceil\log_2 |M_j|\rceil.
  \]
  This counts one output-guess bit and one aggregate register per occurrence.
  An aggregate network adds local monoid contributions and a final predicate of
  their product to the Boolean wire constraints of a graph network.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Outgoing-transition counting for aggregate networks]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-aggregate-count}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.AggregateNetwork.card_accepting_le,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.AggregateNetwork.card_accepting_le_of_read_eq_univ}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-aggregate-budget}
  Let a degree-at-most-three aggregate network with vertex set $V$, finite
  commutative monoid $M$, and cutwidth at most $w$ compute a $K$-rectangle-free
  function on $n$ inputs, where $K>1$. If its input ports read $n'$ coordinates,
  its number of accepting inputs is at most
  \[
    |M|(K-1)2^{n-n'}+|V|2^{w+3}|M|(K-1)^2.
  \]
  If all coordinates are read, this is at most
  $(|V|2^{w+3}+1)|M|(K-1)^2$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Charge the first large past set to the previous frontier values, previous
  aggregate, and incident edge bits. This outgoing key determines the next
  aggregate without inverting multiplication. Rectangle-freeness bounds the
  two input fibres. Terminal states with small past sets contribute the first
  term. Thus neither cancellation nor inverse operations are needed.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[A large component and a dense accepting slice]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-aggregate-component}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Aggregate.exists_large_component,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Aggregate.card_restriction_le,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Aggregate.sliceBound_of_circuit}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-aggregate-budget,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-aggregate-count}
  Suppose the mixed circuit's output is $K$-rectangle-free and accepts at
  least $2^{n-2}$ inputs. Set $r=D+\lceil\log_2 K\rceil+3$ and assume $3r\le n$.
  After guessing special outputs and erasing special incoming slots, some
  connected component contains more than $n-r$ input coordinates.
  Fixing the other coordinates preserves rectangle-freeness and can preserve
  the accepting density. The resulting $m$-input restriction satisfies
  \[
    2^{m-2}\le
    \left((n+3g)2^{(A+\eta)\max(g-m,0)+3\log_2(n+3g)+C+3}+1\right)
       2^{D+1}K^2,
  \]
  whenever $\mathrm{OrderingBound}(A,\eta,C)$ holds, $A+\eta\ge0$, and $C\ge0$;
  here $g$ counts all circuit gates.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Every union of components has a mixing key consisting of the guessed
  outputs and its partial aggregates, with at most $2^D$ values. A balanced
  union would contradict rectangle-freeness and density. A greedy union
  argument therefore gives the large component. Its observed signals compile
  to a subcubic network without extra edges, including observations of sinks.
  One additional two-state register checks the designated output. Apply the
  aggregate count to each guessed computation and sum over the guesses.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[The explicit lower bound survives sublinear aggregate budgets]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-aggregate-explicit}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Aggregate.sourceReductionHardFamily_eventually_lt_size_of_orderingBound,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Aggregate.sourceReductionHardFamily_eventually_lt_size_gaussian,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Aggregate.sourceReductionHardFamily_eventually_lt_size_fortyOne_div_nine}
  \leanok
  \uses{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-aggregate-component,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-uniform-hard-family,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-gaussian-coefficient}
  Fix a budget bound $D(n)=o(n)$. For every $\varepsilon>0$ and all sufficiently
  large $n$, every mixed circuit of budget at most $D(n)$ computing the same
  fixed polynomial-time family $\mathrm{sourceReductionHardFamily}(n)$ has
  more than
  \[
    \left(1+\frac{\pi}{3\arccos((1+2\sqrt2)/4)}-\varepsilon\right)n
    \approx(4.5625-\varepsilon)n
  \]
  total gates. In particular it has more than $(41/9-\varepsilon)n$ gates.
  More generally the coefficient is $1+1/A$ for every $A>0$ with the ordering
  bound at all positive slacks. No invertibility or unproved graph assumption
  remains in the Gaussian specialization.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  The fixed balanced family has a rectangle threshold with
  $\log_2 K(n)=o(n)$. Taking logarithms of the dense-slice bound leaves an
  $O(D+\log K+\log n)$ remainder, absorbed by any positive linear slack.
  Apply the proved Gaussian ordering coefficient.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Joint factorization of aggregate registers]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-aggregate-compressed}
  \lean{Algebraic.Aggregate.Compressed.Factorization,
    Algebraic.Aggregate.Compressed.budget}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-aggregate-budget}
  Let $R$ be the product of all special-gate registers. A joint factorization
  consists of a finite commutative monoid $M$, a homomorphism $d:M\to R$, and
  one contribution $a(w,b)\in M$ for each circuit wire $w$ and Boolean value $b$,
  such that $d(a(w,b))$ is exactly the vector of that wire's contributions to
  all special gates, counting repeated slots. Its budget is
  $D_M=2\lceil\log_2|M|\rceil$.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[The full coefficient survives joint aggregate compression]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-aggregate-compressed}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Aggregate.Compressed.sourceReductionHardFamily_eventually_lt_size_gaussian,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Aggregate.Compressed.sourceReductionHardFamily_eventually_lt_size_generated}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-aggregate-compressed,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-aggregate-explicit}
  Fix $D(n)=o(n)$. For every $\varepsilon>0$, all sufficiently large $n$, and
  every mixed circuit computing the same fixed explicit family, a joint
  factorization with $D_M\le D(n)$ implies more than $(L-\varepsilon)n$ total
  gates, where $L=1+\pi/(3\arccos((1+2\sqrt2)/4))$.
  There is no bound on the number of special gates or their dependencies.
  The submonoid of $R$ generated by all local contributions gives a canonical
  factorization; its cardinality is still charged.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Guess one joint final state to determine every special output. Accumulate
  all local contributions in a second copy of $M$ and check equality with
  the guess. Decoding verifies every special equation; induction along the
  circuit recovers its true evaluation, including special-to-special edges.
  These two states also give the component-mixing key. Apply the aggregate
  network count and the existing graph-ordering asymptotics.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Modular contribution rank supplies the compressed state]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-aggregate-modular-rank}
  \lean{Algebraic.Aggregate.Compressed.Modular.factorization,
    Algebraic.Aggregate.Compressed.Modular.card_compressedState,
    Algebraic.Aggregate.Compressed.Modular.budget_compressedState_le,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Aggregate.Compressed.sourceReductionHardFamily_eventually_lt_size_modular_rankBudget}
  \leanok
  \uses{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-aggregate-compressed}
  For a common prime modulus $p$, form the matrix whose columns are the joint
  contribution vectors of every pair $(w,b)$ and whose rows are special gates.
  If its rank is $r$, its column space supplies a factorization with $p^r$
  states and budget $2\lceil\log_2(p^r)\rceil\le2r\lceil\log_2p\rceil$.
  A sublinear bound on the latter budget therefore preserves coefficient $L$.
  The threshold is uniform over prime moduli and circuits; no separate
  factorization certificate is required. Arbitrary modular readouts are allowed.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Every local column belongs to its span, and inclusion decodes it. A rank-$r$
  vector space over $\mathbf F_p$ has $p^r$ elements. Use the compressed-state
  theorem with its constructed factorization and the logarithmic budget bound.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Exact capacity of a mixed aggregate circuit]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-aggregate-capacity}
  \lean{Algebraic.Aggregate.Capacity.capacity,
    Algebraic.Aggregate.Capacity.realCapacity,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Aggregate.OneWaySummary}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-aggregate-budget}
  If a mixed circuit has $s$ binary gates and special registers $M_j$, its exact
  capacity is $B=s+\sum_j\log_2|M_j|$. The integral version rounds each logarithm
  upward. These capacities have no guessed-output term. A one-way summary for a
  coordinate split depends only on the sending coordinates and determines the
  output for every assignment to the remaining coordinates.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[One-way capacity bound without a sparse-gate hypothesis]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-aggregate-capacity-finite}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Aggregate.card_accepting_le_of_oneWay,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Aggregate.input_le_capacity_of_oneWay,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Aggregate.input_le_capacity_of_circuit,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Aggregate.input_le_logCard_of_circuit,
    Algebraic.Aggregate.Capacity.logb_card_key_eq_realCapacity}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-aggregate-capacity}
  Every mixed aggregate circuit computing a $K$-rectangle-free function with at
  least $2^{n-2}$ accepting inputs has
  \[
    n\le B+2\lceil\log_2 K\rceil+7.
  \]
  No bound is imposed on the number, placement, or fan-in of special gates.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Give the receiving party $\lceil\log_2K\rceil+3$ input coordinates. The sender
  supplies each special gate's aggregate over its own primary-input slots and at
  most one bit per binary gate. The receiver reconstructs all internal gates
  topologically. A further bit handles a directly designated primary output.
  Small row classes have fewer than $K$ rows, while large row classes accept fewer
  than $K$ receiving assignments. Counting both classes and taking logarithms gives
  the bound. This is a classical communication-complexity argument, in the tradition
  of Roychowdhury--Orlitsky--Siu (1994).
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Whole-basis lower bounds for the fixed explicit family]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-aggregate-capacity-explicit}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Aggregate.sourceReductionHardFamily_eventually_lt_realCapacity,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Aggregate.sourceReductionHardFamily_eventually_lt_capacity,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Aggregate.sourceReductionHardFamily_eventually_lt_size_of_register_card,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Aggregate.sourceReductionHardFamily_eventually_lt_size_two_state}
  \leanok
  \uses{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-aggregate-capacity-finite,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-uniform-hard-family}
  For every $\varepsilon>0$, all sufficiently large mixed circuits computing the
  same fixed polynomial-time family have $B>(1-\varepsilon)n$. If all special
  registers have at most $r\ge2$ states, they have more than
  $(1/\log_2r-\varepsilon)n$ gates. In particular arbitrary numbers of two-state
  aggregates, together with the full binary basis, require more than
  $(1-\varepsilon)n$ gates. Depth, fanout, repeated slots, and special-to-special
  wiring remain unrestricted.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  The rectangle threshold has sublinear logarithm. Absorb the finite remainder
  in positive linear slack, and bound exact capacity by $g\log_2r$ for $g$ gates.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Two-sided hardness and a monochromatic coset obstruction]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-aggregate-capacity-polarity}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.RectangleFree.coordinateEquiv,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.RectangleFree.not_balancePad,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.FlatSumsetDisperser.card_lt_of_monochromatic_coset,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Aggregate.family_eventually_sumsetDisperser,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Aggregate.family_eventually_rectangleFree_both}
  \leanok
  \uses{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-uniform-hard-family}
  The fixed balanced family eventually has both output colors rectangle-free at
  the same threshold, and is a flat sumset disperser at that threshold. If $V$ is
  XOR-closed and $f$ is constant on a translate $a+V$, then $|V|<K$ for every
  $K$-flat sumset disperser $f$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Flipping the balancing bit negates the output and preserves rectangles. For the
  coset obstruction, apply sumset dispersion to $a+V$ and $V$: all their XOR sums
  lie in the same monochromatic translate.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Signed unbounded AND/OR/XOR circuits]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-geometry-model}
  \lean{Algebraic.Aggregate.Geometry.Op,
    Algebraic.Aggregate.Geometry.signature,
    Algebraic.Aggregate.Geometry.interpretation,
    Algebraic.Aggregate.Geometry.multiPrimary,
    Algebraic.Aggregate.Geometry.multiCount,
    Algebraic.Aggregate.Geometry.size_binaryCircuit,
    Algebraic.Aggregate.Geometry.eval_binaryCircuit}
  \leanok
  A gate is either an affine Boolean function
  $b\oplus\bigoplus_i a_i z_i$, or an optionally negated conjunction
  $b\oplus\bigwedge_i[z_i=p_i]$, with arbitrary finite arity and Boolean
  parameters. Every gate costs one. The existing acyclic circuit model allows
  arbitrary depth, fanout, and repeated wires, including opposite literals of
  the same wire. This basis contains unbounded AND, OR, and XOR, constants,
  and every binary Boolean operation; binary normalization preserves gate count
  and outputs. For a circuit with $g$ gates, let $h_2$ count conjunction-form
  gates whose slots directly read at least two distinct primary-input variables.
  This is a syntactic count and requires no independence of the values read.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Affine restrictions with paired gate charges]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-geometry-pairing}
  \lean{Algebraic.Aggregate.Geometry.exists_affine_restriction,
    Algebraic.Aggregate.Geometry.exists_monochromatic_affine,
    Algebraic.Aggregate.Geometry.two_mul_input_le_size_add_multi}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-geometry-model,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-aggregate-capacity-polarity}
  Every signed unbounded AND/OR/XOR circuit on $n$ inputs has a nonempty
  monochromatic affine subspace $S$ of codimension $d$ satisfying
  \[
    2^d|S|=2^n,\qquad 2d\le g+h_2+2.
  \]
  Consequently, if its output is a $K$-flat sumset disperser, then
  \[
    2n\le g+h_2+2\lceil\log_2K\rceil.
  \]
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Process gates topologically, keeping every processed output affine on the
  current subspace. For a non-affine conjunction with a nonconstant internal
  predecessor, one affine equation fixes that predecessor to a controlling
  value and makes both gates permanently constant. If every internal predecessor
  is constant, a non-affine conjunction must have at least two distinct primary
  inputs; charge one equation to this gate and its $h_2$ mark. Thus each equation
  is paid for by two newly constant gates or by one such gate and one mark.
  A final equation fixes the designated output if necessary. The coset
  obstruction gives $|S|<K$, yielding the displayed gate inequality.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[The pairing and entropy coefficient]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-geometry-coefficient}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Aggregate.Geometry.Entropy.bitSaving,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Aggregate.Geometry.Entropy.bitSaving_eq,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Aggregate.Geometry.pairRetention,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Aggregate.Geometry.gateCoefficient}
  \leanok
  Write $H_2(p)=-p\log_2p-(1-p)\log_2(1-p)$ and define
  \[
    c=1-H_2(1/4),\qquad
    \alpha(n,k)=\frac{(n-k-1)(n-k-2)}{n(n-1)},\qquad
    \gamma=\frac{1+2c}{1+c}\approx1.15876032857.
  \]
  When $k+3\le n$, the factor $\alpha(n,k)$ is the probability that two designated
  distinct primary variables both lie on a uniformly chosen sending side of
  size $n-k-1$. The constant $c$ measures the information saving from a Boolean
  message whose probability of being true is at most one quarter.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[The finite pairing and entropy inequality]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-geometry-finite}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Aggregate.Geometry.input_le_size_of_entropy,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Aggregate.Geometry.size_lowerBound_of_sumsetDisperser}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-geometry-model,
    def:lowerbounds-algebraic-geometry-coefficient,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-geometry-pairing}
  Let a signed unbounded AND/OR/XOR circuit with $g$ gates compute a
  $K$-flat sumset disperser on $n$ inputs. Assume $K>0$, set
  $k=\lceil\log_2K\rceil$, and suppose $k+3\le n$. With
  $\delta=c\alpha(n,k)$, its gate count satisfies
  \[
    (1+2\delta)n-2\delta k-k-2-\log_2K\le(1+\delta)g.
  \]
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  The sender supplies one partial parity or signed-conjunction bit per gate,
  with one extra bit for a possible primary output; the receiver reconstructs
  the circuit. Giving the receiver $k+1$ coordinates makes every message fibre
  smaller than $K$ by two-sided rectangle-freeness. Designate a pair of distinct
  primary variables at each of the $h_2$ gates. Averaging retains at least
  $\alpha(n,k)h_2$ pairs on the sending side, each giving a quarter-biased message.
  Weighted counting, without assuming independence of message coordinates, gives
  $g\ge n-k-2-\log_2K+\delta h_2$. Combine this with
  $g+h_2\ge2n-2k$ from affine pairing.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[The explicit family needs more than $(\gamma-\varepsilon)n$ signed gates]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-geometry-explicit}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Aggregate.Geometry.sourceReductionHardFamily_eventually_lt_size,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Aggregate.Geometry.one_lt_gateCoefficient,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Aggregate.Geometry.gateCoefficient_lt_two}
  \leanok
  \uses{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-geometry-finite,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-aggregate-capacity-polarity,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-uniform-hard-family}
  For every $\varepsilon>0$ and all sufficiently large $n$, every signed
  unbounded AND/OR/XOR circuit computing the fixed polynomial-time family
  $\mathrm{sourceReductionHardFamily}(n)$ has more than
  $(\gamma-\varepsilon)n$ gates, where $1<\gamma<2$ is the exact coefficient
  above. There is no sparsity, depth, fanout, or fan-in hypothesis. This theorem
  concerns the signed affine/conjunction basis, not all finite-monoid gates.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  The family's sumset threshold has $\log_2K(n)=o(n)$, hence $k=o(n)$ and
  $\alpha(n,k)\to1$. Divide the finite inequality by $(1+\delta)n$ and absorb
  its sublinear remainder in $\varepsilon$. The argument combines affine
  restrictions in the tradition of Demenkov--Kulikov with communication
  accounting in the tradition of Roychowdhury--Orlitsky--Siu. Formal verification
  establishes the stated model and bound; historical priority and the strongest
  previously published coefficient for this exact basis remain unresolved.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[The joint conjunction-message coefficient]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-geometry-joint-coefficient}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Aggregate.Geometry.Joint.quarterEntropy,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Aggregate.Geometry.Joint.overlapPenalty,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Aggregate.Geometry.Joint.gateSaving,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Aggregate.Geometry.Joint.gateCoefficient}
  \leanok
  With binary entropy measured in bits, set
  \[
    h=H_2(1/4),\qquad \beta=h-\tfrac34,\qquad \rho=h-\tfrac12,
    \qquad C_* = \frac{h+3/4}{h+1/2}=1.19065368005\ldots.
  \]
  Here $\beta>0$ charges distinct primary variables, while $\rho>0$ is the
  gate saving in the joint message bound.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[An exact overlap cap for actual Boolean features]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-geometry-actual-pair-entropy}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Aggregate.Geometry.Entropy.pairEntropyCost,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Aggregate.Geometry.Entropy.SignedEdge.dominatedPairWeightBound,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Aggregate.Geometry.Entropy.pairEntropySaving_pos}
  \leanok
  Let $A_1,A_2$ be signed conjunctions on two distinct primary coordinates
  each, with intersecting supports, under uniform independent primary bits.
  Suppose Boolean features $Y_i$ satisfy $Y_i\le A_i$ pointwise. The features
  may otherwise depend arbitrarily on all primary coordinates. There is a
  normalized finite weight certificate for $(Y_1,Y_2)$ of natural-log cost
  \[
    J=3\log 2-\tfrac58\log 5
      =H(5/8,1/8,1/8,1/8).
  \]
  The saving $2H(1/4,3/4)-J$ is strictly positive. Constant false features
  are allowed, so this is not a positive lower bound on mutual information.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  For compatible literal patterns, use weights $(5/8,1/8,1/8,1/8)$ in
  the order $(00,01,10,11)$. For incompatible patterns, use
  $(1/2,1/4,1/4,0)$, whose cost is at most $J$. Exact two- and three-bit
  truth tables cover equal supports and one-coordinate intersections.
  Both weights increase when either Boolean coordinate changes from true
  to false. Replacing $A_i$ by any dominated $Y_i$ therefore preserves the
  average logarithmic weight bound, even when the extra predicates depend
  on every primary bit. The strict saving reduces to $3^{12}<2^8 5^5$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[The finite joint-message inequality]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-geometry-joint-finite}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Aggregate.Geometry.Entropy.Graph.input_le_cost_of_fibres,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Aggregate.Geometry.Joint.input_le_size_of_entropy,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Aggregate.Geometry.Joint.size_lowerBound_of_sumsetDisperser}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-geometry-joint-coefficient,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-geometry-pairing,
    def:lowerbounds-algebraic-geometry-coefficient}
  Let a signed unbounded AND/OR/XOR circuit compute a $K$-flat sumset disperser,
  where $K>0$, $k=\lceil\log_2K\rceil$, and $k+3\le n$.
  With $d=\rho\alpha(n,k)$, its gate count satisfies
  \[
    (1-\beta+2d)n-2dk-(1-\beta)(k+1)-1-\log_2K\le(1+d)g.
  \]
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  For $m$ signed two-variable conjunctions using $v$ primary variables,
  a normalized joint message weight has average coding cost at most
  $m(3/2-h)+\beta v$ bits. Insert the support-graph edges in any order:
  an edge with zero, one, or two previously used endpoints costs at most
  $h$, $3/4$, or $3/2-h$ bits respectively. Conditional tables on at most
  four independent bits establish all signed cases, including parallel edges
  and triangles. Contradictory conjunctions have constant summaries; summaries
  using at least three variables cost at most $H_2(1/8)\le3/2-h$.
  Thus the actual sending-side message costs at most
  $g+1-\rho m+\beta(n-k-1)$ bits. Pair averaging retains
  $m\ge\alpha(n,k)h_2$, while rectangle freeness bounds every message fibre.
  Combine the resulting inequality with $g+h_2\ge2n-2k$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[The explicit family attains the stronger joint-message coefficient]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-geometry-joint-explicit}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Aggregate.Geometry.Joint.sourceReductionHardFamily_eventually_lt_size,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Aggregate.Geometry.Joint.old_gateCoefficient_lt}
  \leanok
  \uses{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-geometry-joint-finite,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-uniform-hard-family}
  For every $\varepsilon>0$, all sufficiently large input lengths require
  more than $(C_*-\varepsilon)n$ signed unbounded AND/OR/XOR gates to compute
  the same fixed polynomial-time Boolean family. The exact constant $C_*$ is
  strictly larger than the previous separate-coordinate coefficient $\gamma$.
  There are no depth, fanout, fan-in, placement, or sparsity restrictions.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  The existing family's logarithmic threshold is sublinear, so $k=o(n)$ and
  $\alpha(n,k)\to1$. Normalize the finite inequality and absorb the vanishing
  remainder. The limiting ratio is
  $(1-\beta+2\rho)/(1+\rho)=C_*$. The strict improvement is equivalent to
  $h(h-3/4)>0$, established by exact logarithmic inequalities.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Shared primary controls improve the affine charge]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-geometry-shared-controls}
  \lean{Algebraic.Aggregate.Geometry.Shared.exists_pairing_two_mul_le,
    Algebraic.Aggregate.Geometry.Shared.PrimaryPairing.exists_affine_restriction,
    Algebraic.Aggregate.Geometry.Shared.seven_mul_input_add_two_mul_exactTwo_le}
  \leanok
  \uses{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-geometry-pairing}
  Let $t$ count conjunction gates with exactly two distinct direct primary
  inputs, and $h_2$ those with at least two. Every circuit computing a
  $K$-flat sumset disperser satisfies
  \[
    7n+2t\le4g+4h_2+8\lceil\log_2K\rceil.
  \]
  No gate placement, fan-in, fanout, or depth restriction is imposed.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Choose a maximal gate-disjoint pairing of intersecting two-primary supports.
  If it contains $p$ pairs, the remaining supports are disjoint, so
  $2t\le n+4p$. Fix one common primary coordinate per pair when necessary.
  Each independent equation makes a distinct gate constant, and both primary
  products in the pair become affine. Run the affine restriction procedure
  from this initial flat, subtracting the initially constant gates from its
  charge. It yields $2n+p\le g+h_2+2\lceil\log_2K\rceil$.
  Eliminate $p$ with the matching inequality.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[The shared-control coefficient]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-geometry-shared-coefficient}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Aggregate.Geometry.Shared.wideEntropy,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Aggregate.Geometry.Shared.wideSaving,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Aggregate.Geometry.Shared.gateCoefficient}
  \leanok
  Write $h=H_2(1/4)$ and $r=1-H_2(1/8)$. The shared-control coefficient is
  $C_{\mathrm s}=(3-h+3r)/(2+2r)$.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[The explicit family attains the shared-control coefficient]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-geometry-shared-explicit}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Aggregate.Geometry.Shared.size_lowerBound_of_sumsetDisperser,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Aggregate.Geometry.Shared.old_gateCoefficient_lt,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Aggregate.Geometry.Shared.sourceReductionHardFamily_eventually_lt_size}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-geometry-shared-coefficient,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-geometry-shared-controls,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-geometry-joint-explicit}
  Put $h=H_2(1/4)$ and $r=1-H_2(1/8)$. The same fixed explicit Boolean family
  requires more than $(C_{\mathrm s}-\varepsilon)n$ gates eventually, for every
  $\varepsilon>0$, over the same whole signed unbounded AND/OR/XOR basis, where
  \[
    C_{\mathrm s}=\frac{3-h+3r}{2+2r}=1.22148505965\ldots.
  \]
  This coefficient strictly exceeds the preceding joint-message coefficient.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Keep separate information costs for exact-two-primary gates and gates using at
  least three primary variables. The latter cost at most $H_2(1/8)$ bits each.
  Averaging a chosen triple for each gate retains a common fraction tending to one
  when Bob receives $\lceil\log_2K\rceil+1=o(n)$ coordinates. Combine this finite
  information inequality with both the original affine count and the shared-control
  count. Exact logarithmic comparisons give positive multipliers eliminating both
  gate classes. Normalize by $n$; the threshold losses vanish.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Exact size of a simultaneous majority event]
  \label{lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-geometry-majority-count}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Aggregate.Geometry.Fiber.card_majorityInputs,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Aggregate.Geometry.Fiber.two_mul_card_le}
  \leanok
  Let $s$ signed two-variable conjunctions have pairwise disjoint endpoints
  among $a$ Boolean coordinates. The number of inputs on which every conjunction
  is false equals
  \[
    3^s2^{a-2s},\qquad 2s\le a.
  \]
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Each pair has exactly three false assignments. Disjoint endpoints identify
  the majority event with the product of these three-element choices and the
  unrestricted assignments on the unused coordinates.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Residual-message counting on the majority event]
  \label{lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-geometry-majority-message}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Aggregate.Geometry.Fiber.log_card_le_of_fibers,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Aggregate.Geometry.Fiber.log_card_le_of_boolean_fibers,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Aggregate.Geometry.Fiber.card_residual_fiber_le}
  \leanok
  \uses{lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-geometry-majority-count}
  Let a finite message map from these $a$ input coordinates to an alphabet $Y$
  have fibres of size at most $K>0$ on the majority event. Then
  \[
    a+(\log_2 3-2)s\le\log_2|Y|+\log_2K.
  \]
  In particular, an $r$-bit residual message has $\log_2|Y|=r$.
  Removing message coordinates that are fixed on the event preserves the
  original full-message fibre bound.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Partition the majority event by its residual messages and compare its exact
  cardinality with $|Y|K$. Take base-two logarithms. The residual messages need
  not be independent.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Majority-fiber interpolation constants]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-geometry-fiber-coefficients}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Aggregate.Geometry.Fiber.messageLoss,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Aggregate.Geometry.Fiber.fiberWeight,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Aggregate.Geometry.Fiber.separateWeight,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Aggregate.Geometry.Fiber.jointWeight,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Aggregate.Geometry.Fiber.scalarNumerator,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Aggregate.Geometry.Fiber.scalarDenominator,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Aggregate.Geometry.Fiber.gateCoefficient,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Aggregate.Geometry.Fiber.inversionNumerator,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Aggregate.Geometry.Fiber.inversionDenominator,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Aggregate.Geometry.Fiber.inversionCoefficient,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Aggregate.Geometry.Fiber.inversionPenalty}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-geometry-joint-coefficient,
    def:lowerbounds-algebraic-geometry-shared-coefficient}
  Put $c=1-H_2(1/4)$, $r=1-H_2(1/8)$, $\rho=H_2(1/4)-1/2$, and
  $\beta=H_2(1/4)-3/4$. Define
  \[
    \gamma_{\mathrm F}=\log_2(3/2),\quad
    \ell=\frac{r}{2\gamma_{\mathrm F}},\quad
    \mu=\frac{\rho-r/2}{\rho-c},\quad
    \nu=\frac{r/2-c}{\rho-c}.
  \]
  The scalar and inversion constants are the exact ratios
  \[
  \begin{aligned}
    N_{\mathrm s}&=1+c/2+7r/4+\ell,&D_{\mathrm s}&=1+r+\ell,
      &C_{\mathrm F}&=N_{\mathrm s}/D_{\mathrm s},\\
    N_{\mathrm i}&=3+c/2+7r/4+3\ell,&D_{\mathrm i}&=2+r+2\ell,
      &C_{\mathrm I}&=N_{\mathrm i}/D_{\mathrm i},
  \end{aligned}
  \]
  with inversion penalty $P_{\mathrm I}=4r/D_{\mathrm i}$. Their decimal
  approximations are $C_{\mathrm F}\approx1.2364849888$,
  $C_{\mathrm I}\approx1.5644077959$, and
  $P_{\mathrm I}\approx0.5640721944$.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Positive interpolation and exact cancellation]
  \label{lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-geometry-fiber-weights}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Aggregate.Geometry.Fiber.messageLoss_pos,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Aggregate.Geometry.Fiber.fiberWeight_pos,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Aggregate.Geometry.Fiber.separateWeight_pos,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Aggregate.Geometry.Fiber.jointWeight_pos,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Aggregate.Geometry.Fiber.separateWeight_add_jointWeight,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Aggregate.Geometry.Fiber.weighted_saving,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Aggregate.Geometry.Fiber.jointWeight_mul_overlapPenalty,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Aggregate.Geometry.Fiber.fiberWeight_mul_messageLoss}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-geometry-fiber-coefficients}
  The four constants $\gamma_{\mathrm F},\ell,\mu,\nu$ are positive, and
  \[
    \mu+\nu=1,\qquad \mu c+\nu\rho=r/2,\qquad
    \nu\beta=(r/2-c)/2,\qquad \ell\gamma_{\mathrm F}=r/2.
  \]
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Exact logarithmic comparisons give $c<r/2<\rho$; the first follows from
  $3^{12}<7^7$. The identities are field arithmetic with positive denominators.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[The explicit family attains the majority-fiber coefficient]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-geometry-fiber-explicit}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Aggregate.Geometry.Fiber.sourceReductionHardFamily_eventually_lt_size}
  \leanok
  \uses{lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-geometry-majority-message,
    lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-geometry-fiber-weights,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-geometry-shared-controls,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-uniform-hard-family}
  For every $\varepsilon>0$, every sufficiently large member of the same fixed
  polynomial-time Boolean family requires more than
  $(C_{\mathrm F}-\varepsilon)n$ gates over the whole signed unbounded
  AND/OR/XOR basis. Depth, fan-in, fanout, repetitions, and gate placement are
  unrestricted. The earlier scalar coefficients remain separate proved results.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Use one maximal primary-support pairing in all inequalities. The unpaired
  exact-two supports are disjoint. Their majority event saves
  $\gamma_{\mathrm F}$ bits per retained support, while each coordinate given
  to the receiver destroys at most one such support. Combine marginal entropy,
  joint entropy, majority counting, and shared-control affine geometry with
  weights $\mu,\nu,\ell,r$, retaining the finite cut-retention factors. The
  gate-class counts cancel. Normalize by $n$ and use $\log_2K(n)=o(n)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Multioutput affine restrictions and output counting]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-geometry-multioutput}
  \lean{Algebraic.Aggregate.Geometry.input_add_multiCount_le_size,
    Algebraic.Aggregate.Geometry.three_mul_input_le_two_mul_size}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-geometry-model,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-geometry-pairing}
  Let an $n$-input, $n$-output signed unbounded AND/OR/XOR circuit compute
  a permutation whose coordinates are not primary projections. Suppose every
  affine flat on which all output coordinates are affine has size at most
  $2^r$. Then its gate count satisfies
  \[
    3n\le 2g+2r.
  \]
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Bijectivity makes the output coordinates distinct and balanced. A conjunction
  gate reading two distinct primary inputs has one output fibre of size at most
  $2^{n-2}$, so it cannot be a balanced output. Thus the $n$ distinct output
  gates lie outside the $h_2$ gates, giving $g\ge n+h_2$. The affine restriction
  invariant before fixing an output gives $g+h_2\ge2n-2r$. Add the inequalities.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Independent nonaffine output components]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-geometry-nonlinear-rank}
  \lean{Algebraic.Aggregate.Geometry.affineFunctions,
    Algebraic.Aggregate.Geometry.NonaffineComponents,
    Algebraic.Aggregate.Geometry.conjunctionCount}
  \leanok
  The affine functions of the primary input bits form a submodule of all
  $\mathbf F_2$-valued functions. A Boolean map has independent nonaffine
  components when every nonzero linear combination of its output coordinates
  lies outside this submodule. Write $q$ for the number of conjunction operations
  in a signed unbounded AND/OR/XOR circuit.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Nonlinear generators bound output rank]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-geometry-nonlinear-rank}
  \lean{Algebraic.Aggregate.Geometry.NonaffineComponents.output_le_conjunctionCount}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-geometry-nonlinear-rank,
    def:lowerbounds-algebraic-geometry-model}
  A circuit with $m$ independent nonaffine output components contains at least
  $m$ conjunction gates. Gate arity, depth, fanout, and reuse of nonlinear
  intermediate functions are unrestricted.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Pass to the vector space of functions modulo the affine primary functions.
  Every affine gate is a linear combination of earlier gate values in this
  quotient. Induction on the actual acyclic program therefore places all output
  classes in the span of the $q$ conjunction classes. Independence gives $m\le q$.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Binary-field inversion in linear coordinates]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-geometry-inversion}
  \lean{Algebraic.Aggregate.Geometry.Inversion.encode,
    Algebraic.Aggregate.Geometry.Inversion.inverseFunction}
  \leanok
  Let $K$ be a binary field and fix a linear isomorphism
  $e:\mathbf F_2^n\longrightarrow K$. The Boolean map $I_e$ encodes its input
  using $e$, takes the multiplicative inverse in $K$ with $0^{-1}=0$, and
  decodes all $n$ output bits in the same basis.
\end{definition}
