\begin{theorem}[A three-halves bound for binary-field inversion]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-geometry-inversion}
  \lean{Algebraic.Aggregate.Geometry.Inversion.three_mul_input_le_two_mul_size}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-geometry-inversion,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-geometry-multioutput}
  For every $n\ge3$ and every linear coordinate basis, any signed unbounded
  AND/OR/XOR circuit computing all $n$ output bits of $I_e$ satisfies
  \[
    3n\le2g+4,
    \qquad\text{equivalently}\qquad g\ge\tfrac32n-2.
  \]
  Depth, fan-in, and fanout are unrestricted, and each scalar Boolean gate
  costs one. There is no supplied hardness hypothesis.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Inversion is a permutation. The reciprocal ternary identity in characteristic
  two shows that every simultaneous affine restriction has at most four points.
  A nonzero additive defect, scaled by arbitrary field elements, shows that
  no nonzero additive component of inversion can be additive when $|K|>4$;
  in particular no output is a primary projection. Apply the preceding
  theorem with $r=2$. The reciprocal identity is a special case of the
  nonvanishing affine-subspace sums studied by Claude Carlet,
  \emph{On the vector subspaces of $\mathbf F_{2^n}$ over which the
  multiplicative inverse function sums to zero} (2024),
  \href{https://doi.org/10.1007/s10623-024-01531-6}{doi:10.1007/s10623-024-01531-6}.
  The circuit deduction is formalized here; historical priority is not asserted.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Output rank and entropy strengthen binary-field inversion]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-geometry-inversion-entropy}
  \lean{Algebraic.Aggregate.Geometry.entropy_mul_size_lower_bound,
    Algebraic.Aggregate.Geometry.Inversion.inverseFunction_nonaffineComponents,
    Algebraic.Aggregate.Geometry.Inversion.entropy_mul_size_lower_bound,
    Algebraic.Aggregate.Geometry.Inversion.gateCoefficient_mul_sub_constantPenalty_le_size,
    Algebraic.Aggregate.Geometry.Inversion.three_halves_lt_gateCoefficient}
  \leanok
  \uses{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-geometry-nonlinear-rank,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-geometry-inversion}
  Put $c=1-H_2(1/4)$. Under the same assumptions as the inversion theorem,
  \[
    (2+c)g\ge(3+2c)n-4c,
    \qquad
    g\ge\frac{3+2c}{2+c}n-\frac{4c}{2+c}.
  \]
  The leading coefficient is $1.54311234736\ldots>3/2$; the additive penalty is
  $0.34489877887\ldots$. The field, basis, and circuit assumptions are unchanged.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  The scaling-defect argument excludes every affine nonzero output component,
  so the nonlinear-generator count is at least $n$. Let $o$ count conjunction
  output gates. Distinct outputs imply $g\ge2n-o$. A balanced conjunction with a
  primary literal must equal a signed primary literal; thus all $o$ conjunction
  outputs have constant primary summaries. The full primary-message vector is
  injective, and the $h_2$ marked summaries are quarter-biased, giving
  $n\le g-o-ch_2$. Hence $2g\ge3n+ch_2$. Combine with
  $g+h_2\ge2n-4$ and divide by $2+c>0$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Majority fibers strengthen binary-field inversion]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-geometry-inversion-fiber}
  \lean{Algebraic.Aggregate.Geometry.Inversion.fiberCoefficient_mul_sub_penalty_le_size}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-geometry-fiber-coefficients,
    lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-geometry-majority-message,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-geometry-inversion-entropy}
  Let $K$ be a finite field over $\mathbf F_2$ with a supplied linear
  isomorphism $e:\mathbf F_2^n\longrightarrow K$, where $n\ge3$.
  Every signed unbounded AND/OR/XOR circuit computing all $n$ coordinates
  of inversion $I_e$, with $0^{-1}=0$, satisfies
  \[
    g\ge C_{\mathrm I}n-P_{\mathrm I}.
  \]
  Here $C_{\mathrm I}\approx1.5644077959$ and
  $P_{\mathrm I}\approx0.5640721944$; the exact constants are the logarithmic
  ratios defined above. Depth,
  fan-in, fanout, repeated literals, and nonlinear reuse are unrestricted.
  All target hardness properties are proved; no uniform field/basis
  construction or evaluator complexity is asserted by this theorem.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  In a nonliteral permutation, conjunction output summaries are constant true
  and the full primary message is injective. Remove these output coordinates
  and the constant false coordinates of the disjoint majority event. With $o$
  conjunction outputs and $t-2p$ unmatched gates, this gives
  $n+\gamma_{\mathrm F}(t-2p)\le g-o$. Independent nonaffine output components
  and distinct designated outputs give $2n\le g+o$. Combine these with both
  entropy bounds and the shared affine restriction. Inversion has at most
  four points in every simultaneous affine-output flat, giving the exact
  finite penalty $4r/D_{\mathrm i}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Residual entropy conditioned on majority fibers]
  \label{lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-geometry-conditional-entropy}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Aggregate.Geometry.Fiber.Conditional.three_pow_mul_card_evalLiterals_le,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Aggregate.Geometry.Fiber.Conditional.evalLiteralsPairWeightBound,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Aggregate.Geometry.Fiber.Conditional.evalLiteralsTripleWeightBound,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Aggregate.Geometry.Fiber.Conditional.log_card_le_of_weight}
  \leanok
  \uses{lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-geometry-majority-count,
    lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-geometry-majority-message}
  On the uniform distribution on a product of disjoint three-point majority
  fibers and unrestricted remaining coordinates, an assignment to any $d$
  distinct coordinates has probability at most $(2/3)^d$. Consequently a
  conjunction of two or at least three distinct prescribed coordinates has
  entropy at most $H_2(4/9)$ or $H_2(8/27)$, respectively. For a message with
  fibers of size at most $K$, the logarithm of the domain size is at most its
  entropy bound plus $\log_2 K$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Count the compatible choices on each disjoint pair: prescribing one
  coordinate retains at most two of its three choices, and prescribing both
  retains at most one. Multiply these bounds over the product. Binary entropy
  is increasing up to $1/2$; entropy subadditivity bounds the whole message
  without assuming independent summaries.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Conditional-majority coefficients]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-geometry-conditional-coefficients}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Aggregate.Geometry.Fiber.Conditional.pairSaving,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Aggregate.Geometry.Fiber.Conditional.tripleSaving,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Aggregate.Geometry.Fiber.Conditional.fiberWeight,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Aggregate.Geometry.Fiber.Conditional.wideWeight,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Aggregate.Geometry.Fiber.Conditional.gateCoefficient,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Aggregate.Geometry.Fiber.Conditional.inversionCoefficient,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Aggregate.Geometry.Fiber.Conditional.inversionPenalty}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-geometry-fiber-coefficients}
  Set $\eta_2=1-H_2(4/9)$, $\eta_3=1-H_2(8/27)$,
  $\gamma=\log_2(3/2)$, $c=1-H_2(1/4)$, and $r=1-H_2(1/8)$. Put
  \[
    \ell=\frac{r}{2(\gamma-\eta_2)-\eta_3},\qquad
    R=2(\gamma-\eta_2)\ell.
  \]
  The new exact coefficients and finite penalty are
  \[
  \begin{aligned}
    C&=\frac{1+c/2+\ell(1+7\gamma/2-3\eta_2)}{1+\ell+R}
       \approx1.2453914029,\\
    I&=\frac{3+c/2+\ell(3+7\gamma/2-3\eta_2)}{2+2\ell+R}
       \approx1.5659486596,\qquad
    P=\frac{4R}{2+2\ell+R}\approx0.6016050397.
  \end{aligned}
  \]
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Conditional fibers strengthen both unrestricted signed-gate bounds]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-geometry-conditional}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Aggregate.Geometry.Fiber.Conditional.sourceReductionHardFamily_eventually_lt_size,
    Algebraic.Aggregate.Geometry.Inversion.conditionalFiberCoefficient_mul_sub_penalty_le_size}
  \leanok
  \uses{lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-geometry-conditional-entropy,
    def:lowerbounds-algebraic-geometry-conditional-coefficients,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-geometry-fiber-explicit,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-geometry-inversion-fiber}
  The same fixed polynomial-time scalar family requires more than
  $(C-\varepsilon)n$ signed unbounded AND/OR/XOR gates eventually, for every
  $\varepsilon>0$. All coordinates of binary-field inversion in every supplied
  linear basis require at least $In-P$ gates when $n\ge3$. The depth, fan-in,
  fanout, and gate-placement assumptions are unchanged. The earlier inversion
  inequality remains useful because its additive penalty is smaller.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Use the same maximal pairing for the affine restriction and the majority
  event. If $t$ counts narrow summaries, $w$ counts wide summaries, and $u$
  counts unpaired narrow summaries, the new scalar leading inequality is
  $n+(\gamma-\eta_2)u+\eta_2t+\eta_3w\le g+o(n)$.
  The finite theorem retains receiver-cut losses explicitly. Combine it with
  the marginal and joint entropy inequalities and the shared affine geometry;
  the positive interpolation weights cancel all three gate-class counts.
  For inversion, output-component independence replaces the leading $n,g$
  by $3n,2g$, and the four-point affine obstruction supplies the penalty $P$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Inversion's actual nonlinear-feature budget]
  \label{lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-geometry-inversion-features}
  \lean{Algebraic.Aggregate.Geometry.output_affine_conjunction_representation,
    Algebraic.Aggregate.Geometry.Inversion.input_add_feature_bias_le_conjunctionCount}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-geometry-nonlinear-rank,
    def:lowerbounds-algebraic-geometry-inversion}
  Every output of a signed unbounded AND/OR/XOR circuit is an affine function
  of the primary bits plus a linear combination of its actual conjunction
  gate outputs. Suppose the circuit computes all coordinates of inversion
  in any supplied binary-field basis. Let $N$ count conjunction gates and
  let $m$ count those reading at least two distinct primary coordinates.
  With $c=1-H_2(1/4)$,
  \[
    N\ge n+cm-\log_2 5.
  \]
  No independence, fan-in, fanout, depth, or nonlinear-reuse restriction is imposed.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Apply the gate-span induction to the sum of the affine subspace and the
  range of the linear-combination map on actual conjunction functions.
  Assemble the output representations in the field basis to obtain
  $I(x)+Ax+a=D(h(x))$. Inversion's classical differential-uniformity bound
  gives at most $q+4(q-1)$ ordered collisions for this affine residual,
  with $q=2^n$. Equality of feature vectors implies equality of residuals,
  so their average fibre size is at most five. The weighted logarithmic
  collision inequality charges every quarter-biased feature. Each of the
  $m$ marked actual conjunction outputs has a rare Boolean value occurring
  on at most one quarter of the cube, even with repeated or internal wires.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Nonlinear features strengthen binary-field inversion]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-geometry-inversion-feature-bound}
  \lean{Algebraic.Aggregate.Geometry.Inversion.feature_entropy_mul_size_lower_bound,
    Algebraic.Aggregate.Geometry.Inversion.featureCoefficient_mul_sub_penalty_le_size}
  \leanok
  \uses{lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-geometry-inversion-features,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-geometry-inversion-entropy}
  Let $K$ be a finite field over $\mathbf F_2$ with a supplied linear
  isomorphism $e:\mathbf F_2^n\longrightarrow K$, where $n\ge3$.
  Every signed unbounded AND/OR/XOR circuit computing all inversion
  coordinates satisfies
  \[
    (2+2c)g\ge(3+4c)n-\log_2 5-8c,
    \qquad c=1-H_2(1/4).
  \]
  Equivalently, its gate count is at least
  $1.5793801642\ldots n-1.6116903280\ldots$.
  This gives a larger leading coefficient than the majority-fibre bound;
  both finite inequalities remain available with their different penalties.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Let $o$ count conjunction output gates. Distinct designated outputs give
  $n+N\le g+o$, while their constant primary summaries and the biased
  internal summaries give $n\le g-o-cm$. Hence
  $2g\ge2n+N+cm\ge3n+2cm-\log_2 5$.
  The affine-restriction inequality $2n\le g+m+4$ eliminates $m$.
  Divide by $2+2c>0$ for the normalized gate bound.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Full Hamming residue modulo three]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-modthree-residue}
  \lean{Algebraic.BooleanCube.ModThree.bit,
    Algebraic.BooleanCube.ModThree.residue}
  \leanok
  For $x\in\mathbf F_2^n$, let $w_3(x)\in\mathbf F_3$ be its Hamming weight
  modulo three, interpreting each binary coordinate as zero or one.
  This is the full three-valued residue, not a Boolean residue predicate.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Constant-residue affine flats have at most half dimension]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-modthree-half-dimension}
  \lean{Algebraic.BooleanCube.ModThree.two_mul_finrank_le_of_constant,
    Algebraic.BooleanCube.ModThree.two_mul_finrank_submodule_le,
    Algebraic.BooleanCube.ModThree.exists_kernel_residue_ne,
    Algebraic.BooleanCube.ModThree.exists_supported_kernel_residue_ne}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-modthree-residue}
  If $w_3$ is constant on an affine flat $a+V\subseteq\mathbf F_2^n$, then
  $2\dim V\le n$. Consequently, for any $q$ binary linear equations and any
  coordinate block $B$ with $|B|>2q$, there is a homogeneous solution supported
  in $B$ that changes the residue at any prescribed base point.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Write the affine coordinates as $a_i+\ell_i(u)$. Over $\mathbf F_3$, their
  sign characters are linearly independent when the linear forms are distinct.
  Constant full residue forces the signed multiplicity of each nonzero form
  to vanish, so every such form occurs at least twice. Injectivity requires at
  least $\dim V$ distinct nonzero forms. For the supported conclusion, restrict
  the equations to $B$ and apply rank--nullity.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[A signed MOD3 cube in every binary affine solution coset]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-modthree-cube}
  \lean{Algebraic.BooleanCube.ModThree.exists_affineCube_of_disjoint_blocks,
    Algebraic.BooleanCube.ModThree.exists_signed_cube}
  \leanok
  \uses{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-modthree-half-dimension}
  For every linear map $R:\mathbf F_2^n\to\mathbf F_2^q$ and every base point
  $a$, put $r=\lfloor n/(2q+1)\rfloor$. There are an injective linear map
  $L:\mathbf F_2^r\to\mathbf F_2^n$ and signs $\delta_j\in\{1,-1\}$ such that
  $RL=0$ and, for every $z\in\mathbf F_2^r$,
  \[
    w_3(a+Lz)=w_3(a)+\sum_{j=1}^r\delta_j z_j\quad\text{in }\mathbf F_3,
  \]
  with each $z_j$ interpreted as zero or one.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Partition $r(2q+1)$ coordinates into $r$ disjoint blocks. The preceding
  theorem supplies a residue-changing kernel direction in every block.
  Their disjoint supports give injectivity and additive residue changes.
  Every nonzero element of $\mathbf F_3$ is $1$ or $-1$.
\end{proof}

The geometric statements above are checked ingredients for a restricted-circuit
research direction. Circuit freezing, probabilistic polynomial approximation,
and the Razborov--Smolensky degree lower bound are not formalized by these nodes;
no superlinear unrestricted circuit theorem follows from them alone.

\begin{theorem}[Conditional: the concrete family needs more than $(23/5-\varepsilon)n$ gates]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-explicit-band}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.Multigraph.exists_orderingBound_band,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.Multigraph.exists_orderingBound_five_div_eighteen_of_bandSubcritical,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.sourceReductionHardFamily_eventually_lt_size_of_bandSubcritical,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.sourceReductionHardFamily_eventually_lt_size_twentyThree_div_five_of_bandSubcritical}
  \leanok
  \uses{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-gaussian-band-pathwidth,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-ordering-bound,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-explicit-coefficient}
  This result is conditional on the open band hypothesis of
  Definition~\ref{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-gaussian-band-subcritical}. Let $c>0$ and assume
  $\mathrm{BandSubcritical}(q,R,c)$ for every decay rate $0\le q<1/\sqrt2$ and every radius
  $R$, and let $p=\tfrac{3}{2\pi}\arccos\tfrac{1+2\sqrt2}{4}$. Then for every $\eta>0$ some $C$
  satisfies $\mathrm{OrderingBound}(2e^{-c^2/2}p,\eta,C)$, and for every $\varepsilon>0$ and
  all sufficiently large $n$, every circuit computing $\mathrm{sourceReductionHardFamily}(n)$
  over the full binary basis has more than $\bigl(1+1/(2e^{-c^2/2}p)-\varepsilon\bigr)n$ gates,
  with $n$ the full input length. If the hypothesis holds at $c=4/25$, the ordering
  coefficient is at most $5/18$ and every such circuit has more than $(23/5-\varepsilon)n$
  gates.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Apply the pathwidth-to-ordering transport of
  Theorem~\ref{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-ordering-bound} to
  Theorem~\ref{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-gaussian-band-pathwidth}, then
  Theorem~\ref{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-explicit-coefficient} with $A=2e^{-c^2/2}p$, or with
  its rational weakening $A=5/18$ at $c=4/25$, for which $1+1/A=23/5$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Average-case cutwidth bound for balanced families]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-cutwidth-average}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.eventually_card_agree_le_of_pathwidthBound,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.eventually_card_agree_le_of_balanced,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.eventually_card_agree_le_of_flatSumsetExtractor}
  \leanok
  \uses{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-cutwidth}
  Call $f$ $(K, \nu)$-balanced when on every rectangle with both sides of
  size at least $K$, under every split of the coordinates, the fraction of
  accepting inputs lies within $\nu$ of one half (a sumset extractor with
  polynomial threshold $K$ and error $\nu$ is balanced at that threshold).
  For every $\nu\ge0$, every family $f_n$ that is $(K_n, \nu)$-balanced
  with $K_n \le n^c$, every
  $\varepsilon > 0$, and all large $n$, every circuit with at most
  $(4 - \varepsilon)n$ binary gates agrees with $f_n$ on at most
  $(1/2 + 3\nu)\,2^n + 2^{(1 - \varepsilon/24)n}$ inputs.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  The worst-case count gives nothing here, since an input whose past set is
  still small at a vertex lies in a thin rectangle on which a balanced
  function is unconstrained. Instead the direction of the wiring graph is
  used: at a cut, the forward-crossing bits are determined by the inputs read
  so far together with the backward-crossing bits, and symmetrically for the
  future. This caps the number of cut assignments a fixed past or future can
  see, so the thin-rectangle mass at a prefix $L$ is at most
  $(K - 1)(2^{n - a(L)} + 2^{n - c(L)})$ with $a(L)$ the inputs read minus
  the forward cut and $c(L)$ the inputs unread minus the backward cut. Along
  the vertex order $a$ grows by at most four per step and $a + c$ is $n$
  minus the cut, so a prefix with both $a$ and $c$ linear exists whenever
  the cutwidth is below $(1 - \Omega(\varepsilon))n$. Circuits reading few
  inputs are constant on subcubes, which balance handles directly.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Signals across a single cut]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-single-cut}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.SingleCut.inputsIn,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.SingleCut.forward,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.SingleCut.backward,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.SingleCut.boundaryKey,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.SingleCut.mix,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.SingleCut.leftParts,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.SingleCut.rightParts}
  \leanok
  Split the wires of a straight-line program, its inputs and its gates, into
  a set $S$ and its complement $\bar S$, and let $i$ be the number of inputs
  in $S$. The forward signals $A$ are the wires in $S$ read by some gate in
  $\bar S$, and the backward signals $B$ are the wires in $\bar S$ read by
  some gate in $S$. A signal is the value of one wire, counted once however
  many gates read it. The boundary key of an input $x$ is the pair $(a, b)$
  of the values of the signals in $A$ and in $B$. Mixing $x$ and $x'$ takes
  the coordinates of $x$ whose input wires lie in $S$ and the remaining
  coordinates of $x'$. For a set $Z$ of inputs and a key $\sigma$, the left
  and right parts $L_\sigma$ and $R_\sigma$ are the restrictions of the
  members of $Z$ with key $\sigma$ to the inputs in $S$ and in $\bar S$.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Product fibres of a single cut]
  \label{lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-single-cut-fibres}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.SingleCut.trace_mix,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.SingleCut.trace_eq_of_agree_backward,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.SingleCut.trace_eq_of_agree_forward,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.SingleCut.boundaryKey_eq_of_leftPart_eq,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.SingleCut.boundaryKey_eq_of_rightPart_eq,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.SingleCut.card_filter_boundaryKey_eq,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.SingleCut.card_image_boundaryKey_le,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.SingleCut.sum_card_leftParts_le,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.SingleCut.sum_card_rightParts_le}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-single-cut}
  Fix a program over any signature, with values in any type $U$, and any
  number of outputs. If $x$ and $x'$ have the same boundary key, the mixed
  input takes the values of $x$ on every wire of $S$ and those of $x'$ on
  every wire of $\bar S$. The inputs in $S$ and the values on $B$ determine
  the values on all of $S$; symmetrically, the inputs in $\bar S$ and the
  values on $A$ determine the values on all of $\bar S$. Consequently, if $Z$
  is closed under mixing inputs with equal keys, then the members of $Z$ with
  key $\sigma$ correspond bijectively to $L_\sigma \times R_\sigma$; for a
  fixed $b$ the sets $L_{(a, b)}$ are pairwise disjoint, so
  $\sum_\sigma |L_\sigma| \le |U|^{i + |B|}$, and symmetrically
  $\sum_\sigma |R_\sigma| \le |U|^{n - i + |A|}$. There are at most
  $|U|^{|A| + |B|}$ keys.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Induct over the wires in program order. A gate in $S$ reads wires of $S$,
  where the values agree by induction, and backward signals, whose values are
  part of the key; a gate in $\bar S$ is symmetric. Restricting to the inputs
  in $S$ and in $\bar S$ is injective, and mixing shows that every pair of a
  left part and a right part of key $\sigma$ is realized. Two inputs with the
  same left part and the same $b$ have the same $a$ by determination, which
  gives the disjointness.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Single-cut criterion]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-single-cut}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.SingleCut.card_accepting_le_of_trace,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.SingleCut.card_accepting_le,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.SingleCut.card_accepting_lt}
  \leanok
  \uses{lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-single-cut-fibres}
  Let a single-output circuit over any basis with Boolean values compute a
  $K$-rectangle-free function $f$ on $n$ inputs (as in
  Theorem~\ref{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-nechiporuk}). Then for every split
  of its wires into $S$ and $\bar S$,
  \[
    |f^{-1}(1)| \le (K - 1)\left(2^{i + |B|} + 2^{n - i + |A|}\right)
      < K\left(2^{i + |B|} + 2^{n - i + |A|}\right).
  \]
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  The accepted inputs are closed under mixing inputs with equal keys, because
  the output wire lies on one side and the mixed input copies that side. So
  the accepted inputs with key $\sigma$ form the one-rectangle
  $L_\sigma \times R_\sigma$, one of whose sides has fewer than $K$ elements;
  hence it has at most $(K - 1)(|L_\sigma| + |R_\sigma|)$ points. Summing
  over $\sigma$ and applying the partition bounds of
  Lemma~\ref{lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-single-cut-fibres} gives the bound.
  A rectangle-free threshold is positive, which gives the strict form.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Single-cut bound for dense rectangle-free functions]
  \label{cor:lowerbounds-algebraic-single-cut}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.SingleCut.le_max_add_clog,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.SingleCut.sub_logb_lt_max,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.SingleCut.exists_prefix_le}
  \leanok
  \uses{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-single-cut}
  If moreover $f$ has at least $2^{n - 2}$ accepting inputs, then every split
  satisfies $n \le \max(i + |B|, n - i + |A|) + \lceil \log_2 K \rceil + 2$,
  and hence $\max(i + |B|, n - i + |A|) > n - \log_2 K - 3$. In particular,
  for every injective ranking of the wires some prefix, the wires ranked
  below a threshold, is crossed by at least
  $\lfloor n/2 \rfloor - \lceil \log_2 K \rceil - 2$ forward and backward
  signals.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Bound $K \le 2^{\lceil \log_2 K \rceil}$ and both powers by
  $2^{\max(i + |B|, n - i + |A|)}$ and compare exponents. Adding the wires in
  rank order changes the number of inputs in the prefix by at most one at a
  time, so some prefix contains exactly $\lfloor n/2 \rfloor$ inputs; for it
  $\max(i + |B|, n - i + |A|) \le \lceil n/2 \rceil + |A| + |B|$.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Components and multi-output splits]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-multi-output}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.Linked,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.component,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.gatesIn,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.prefixUpTo,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.prefixBelow,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.outputsIn,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.leftFibre,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.rightFibre,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.blockRank}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-single-cut}
  Two wires of a straight-line program are linked when one is a gate reading
  the other; the components of the resulting undirected wire graph are its
  connected components. For a ranking of the wires, the prefix up to a wire
  $w$ consists of the wires ranked at most as high as $w$. For designated
  output wires and a split $S$ of the wires with complement $T$, let $X_S$
  and $X_T$ be the inputs placed in $S$ and in $T$, and $Y_S$ and $Y_T$ the
  outputs whose wires lie in $S$ and in $T$. For a function $f$ and an input
  $x$, the left fibre of $x$ is the set of inputs that agree with $x$ on
  $X_T$ and on which $f$ agrees with $f(x)$ on $Y_T$; the right fibre is
  defined symmetrically. For a matrix $M$, $M[Y, X]$ is its block with rows
  $Y$ and columns $X$.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Transferring graph orderings to multi-output programs]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-multi-output-ordering}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.exists_rank,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.exists_rank_of_connected,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.component_closed,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.forward_union_of_closed,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.backward_union_of_closed,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.orderingBound_nonneg}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-multi-output,
    def:lowerbounds-algebraic-ordering-hypotheses}
  Let a straight-line program over any signature, with any value type and
  any designated outputs, have $n$ inputs, $s$ gates, and fan-in at most
  two, and suppose $\mathrm{OrderingBound}(A,\eta,C)$ with $A+\eta\ge0$.
  Then some injective ranking of the wires, with ranks below $n+s$, has the
  following property: for every wire $w$, the prefix up to $w$ is crossed
  by at most
  \[
    (A+\eta)\max\{g-i,0\} + 3\log_2(n+3s) + C
  \]
  forward and backward signals, where $g$ and $i$ are the numbers of gates
  and inputs in the component of $w$. If the wire graph is connected, every
  prefix is crossed by at most $(A+\eta)\max\{s-n,0\}+3\log_2(n+3s)+C$
  signals.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  For each component, build the subcubic wire graph: a vertex per wire, and
  $f-1$ copy vertices routing a signal read by $f\ge2$ argument slots, with
  the slots and the copy edges as edges. It is loopless, of maximum degree
  three, and connected, and its edge excess is at most $g-i$; apply the
  ordering hypothesis and order the wires of the component by their
  vertices. The edges carrying one signal form a tree joining its wire to
  every gate reading it, so a signal crossing a split of the wires crosses
  the corresponding cut with an edge of its own, and distinct signals use
  distinct edges. Lay out the components one after another. A component is
  closed under reading, so earlier components contribute no crossing
  signals, and the prefix up to $w$ is charged to a lower-set cut of the
  wire graph of the component of $w$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Rank-cut bound]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-rank-cut}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.card_pow_le_of_fibres,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.card_pow_le_of_fibres_of_trace,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.card_leftFibre_le,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.card_rightFibre_le,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.blockRank_add_blockRank_le,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.blockRank_add_blockRank_le_of_trace}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-multi-output,
    lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-single-cut-fibres}
  Let a circuit over any signature with values in a finite set $U$ compute
  $f\colon U^n\to U^m$. If, for a split $S$ with forward signals $A$ and
  backward signals $B$, every left fibre has at most $D_T$ elements and
  every right fibre at most $D_S$, then $|U|^n\le|U|^{|A|+|B|}D_TD_S$. In
  particular, if a circuit with values in a finite field computes the linear
  map $x\mapsto Mx$, with arbitrary gate functions, then every split
  satisfies
  \[
    \operatorname{rank} M[Y_T, X_S] + \operatorname{rank} M[Y_S, X_T]
      \le |A| + |B|.
  \]
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  The inputs with a fixed boundary key form a product $L\times R$ of left
  and right parts (Lemma~\ref{lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-single-cut-fibres}).
  Mixing a member of the fibre into a fixed member $x_0$ on $X_T$ keeps the
  outputs in $Y_T$, so $L$ embeds into the left fibre of $x_0$, and
  symmetrically $R$ embeds into its right fibre; sum over the at most
  $|U|^{|A|+|B|}$ keys. For a linear map, a left fibre minus its base point
  lies in the kernel of $M[Y_T,X_S]$, which has $|F|^{|X_S|-r_1}$ elements;
  symmetrically for right fibres, and comparing exponents gives
  $r_1+r_2\le|A|+|B|$.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Totally regular and Cauchy matrices]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-totally-regular}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.TotallyRegular,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.cauchy,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.cauchyZMod,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.cauchyCharZero}
  \leanok
  A matrix over a field is totally regular when every square submatrix is
  nonsingular. The Cauchy matrix of nodes $x_i$ and $y_j$ has entries
  $1/(x_i-y_j)$; over $\mathbb{Z}/q$, the explicit family takes $x_i=i$ and
  $y_j=N+j$ for $0\le i,j<N$.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Cauchy matrices are totally regular]
  \label{lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-cauchy}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.TotallyRegular.min_card_le_blockRank,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.det_cauchy_ne_zero,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.totallyRegular_cauchy,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.totallyRegular_cauchyZMod}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-totally-regular,
    def:lowerbounds-algebraic-multi-output}
  Every block of a totally regular matrix has rank at least the smaller of
  its dimensions. A Cauchy matrix with distinct $x_i$, distinct $y_j$, and
  $x_i\ne y_j$ for all $i$ and $j$ is totally regular. In particular the
  explicit family over $\mathbb{Z}/q$ is totally regular for every prime
  $q\ge2N$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  A block contains a square block of the smaller dimension, which is
  nonsingular. Every square submatrix of a Cauchy matrix is a Cauchy
  matrix, so it suffices that a square Cauchy matrix has trivial kernel.
  If $\sum_j v_j/(x_i-y_j)=0$ for all $i$, the Lagrange interpolant through
  the points $(y_j, v_j/w_j)$, with $w_j=\prod_{l\ne j}(y_j-y_l)^{-1}$ the
  barycentric weights, has the first barycentric form
  $\prod_l(x-y_l)\sum_j v_j/(x-y_j)$ away from the nodes, so it vanishes at
  every $x_i$. Its degree is below the number of nodes, so it is zero, and
  then every $v_j$ is zero. For the explicit family the nodes $0,\dots,2N-1$
  are distinct modulo $q$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Totally regular maps need $(1+1/(2p)-\varepsilon)N$ gates]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-totally-regular}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.sub_one_le_of_totallyRegular,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.eventually_lt_size_of_totallyRegular_of_orderingBound,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.eventually_lt_size_of_totallyRegular,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.eventually_lt_size_of_totallyRegular_fortyOne_div_nine,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.eventually_lt_size_cauchyZMod}
  \leanok
  \uses{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-multi-output-ordering,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-rank-cut,
    lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-cauchy,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-gaussian-coefficient}
  Let a circuit over a finite field $F$, over any signature with fan-in at
  most two (so with arbitrary functions $F\times F\to F$, unary functions,
  and constants as gates), compute the linear map of a totally regular
  $N\times N$ matrix with $s$ gates. Under $\mathrm{OrderingBound}(A,\eta,C)$
  with $A+\eta\ge0$,
  \[
    N - 1 \le (A+\eta)\max\{s-N,0\} + 3\log_2(N+3s) + C .
  \]
  Consequently, if the ordering hypothesis holds with coefficient $A>0$ at
  every positive slack, then for every $\varepsilon>0$ and all large $N$,
  independently of the field and the signature, every such circuit has more
  than $(1+1/A-\varepsilon)N$ gates. With the Gaussian pathwidth
  coefficient $p=\tfrac{3}{2\pi}\arccos\tfrac{1+2\sqrt2}{4}$ of
  Theorem~\ref{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-gaussian-coefficient} this is
  $(1+1/(2p)-\varepsilon)N\approx(4.5625-\varepsilon)N$, and as $2p\le9/32$
  it is more than $(41/9-\varepsilon)N$; in particular for the explicit
  Cauchy family over $\mathbb{Z}/q$ with $q\ge2N$ prime.

  The graph of a circuit computing a totally regular map is a
  superconcentrator whatever its gate functions (Valiant), so the same bound
  also follows from
  Corollary~\ref{cor:lowerbounds-algebraic-superconcentrator-in-degree}. The
  proof here goes through the rank-cut bound instead, which applies to linear
  maps that are not totally regular as well.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Distinct outputs lie on distinct wires, since two outputs on one wire would
  give equal rows and a singular $2\times2$ block. The component of an output
  is closed, so it is crossed by no signal, and the rank-cut bound forces
  every input and every output into it. Along the ranking of
  Theorem~\ref{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-multi-output-ordering}, the number
  of inputs plus outputs in a prefix grows by at most two per wire, so some
  prefix ending at an input or output contains $N$ or $N+1$ of them; an
  output carried by an input wire counts twice. For that prefix the two
  blocks of the rank-cut bound have full rank by
  Lemma~\ref{lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-cauchy}, with ranks summing to at
  least $N-1$, and the prefix is charged to the component containing all
  $N$ inputs and at most $s$ gates. For the asymptotic form, assume at most
  $(1+1/A-\varepsilon)N$ gates, choose $\eta=A^2\varepsilon/2$, and compare:
  the cycle term is at most $(1-A\varepsilon/2)N$, and the logarithmic term
  is $O(\log N)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Local linear realizations and polynomial gates]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-polynomial-realization}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.Linear.Realization,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.Polynomial.IsPolynomial,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.Polynomial.signature,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.Polynomial.interpretation}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-multi-output}
  A local linear realization assigns a row $v_w\in F^n$ to every original
  circuit wire. Input $j$ carries $e_j$, and each gate row lies in the span
  of its argument rows. A polynomial interpretation assigns each operation a
  polynomial in its argument slots, with unrestricted degree and coefficients.
  The full polynomial signature contains every such finite-arity polynomial.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Local realizations bound ranks at every cut]
  \label{lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-linear-realization-cut}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.Linear.Realization.blockRank_le_forward,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.Linear.Realization.blockRank_le_backward,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.Linear.Realization.blockRank_add_blockRank_le}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-polynomial-realization}
  Over any field, suppose the output rows of a local realization form $M$.
  For any wire cut $S$, its two crossing-signal sets $A,B$ satisfy
  \[
    \operatorname{rank}M[Y_T,X_S]\le |A|,\qquad
    \operatorname{rank}M[Y_S,X_T]\le |B|.
  \]
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Restrict each exterior wire's row to the interior input coordinates.
  Induction along the program puts it in the span of the forward signal rows:
  exterior inputs restrict to zero, and an interior predecessor of an exterior
  gate is a forward signal. This span has dimension at most $|A|$.
  Apply the same argument to the complementary cut.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[The discrete Fourier transform matrix]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-dft}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.dft}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-multi-output}
  For an element $\omega$ of a field and $N\in\mathbb{N}$, the Fourier matrix is
  $F_N=(\omega^{rc})_{0\le r,c<N}$, with rows $r$ the outputs and columns $c$ the inputs. When
  $\omega$ is a primitive $N$-th root of unity, $x\mapsto F_Nx$ is the $N$-point discrete Fourier
  transform: over $\mathbb{C}$ with $\omega=e^{2\pi i/N}$ the classical DFT, for $N=2^k$ the
  map computed by the fast Fourier transform, and over $\mathbb{Z}/q$ with $N\mid q-1$ a
  number-theoretic transform.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Half cuts of Vandermonde and Fourier matrices]
  \label{lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-dft-half-cut}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.le_blockRank_add_blockRank_vandermonde,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.le_blockRank_add_blockRank_dft}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-dft, def:lowerbounds-algebraic-multi-output}
  Let $M=(x_c^{\,r})_{0\le r,c<N}$ for distinct nodes $x_c$ of a field. For every set $X$ of $h$
  columns with $2h\le N$ and every set $Y$ of rows,
  \[
    h\le\operatorname{rank}M[Y^c,X]+\operatorname{rank}M[Y,X^c].
  \]
  In particular, for a primitive $N$-th root of unity $\omega$ the Fourier matrix $F_N$, with
  nodes $\omega^c$, satisfies this for $h=\lfloor N/2\rfloor$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  On a set $C$ of columns, the rows $r<|C|$ of $M$ are linearly independent: a vanishing
  combination $\sum_rg_rx_c^{\,r}$ is a polynomial of degree below $|C|$ with the $|C|$ distinct
  roots $x_c$, $c\in C$. So $M[Y^c,X]$ has rank at least the number of rows $r<h$ outside $Y$,
  and $M[Y,X^c]$, with $N-h\ge h$ columns, has rank at least the number of rows $r<N-h$ in $Y$,
  hence at least the number of rows $r<h$ in $Y$. The two counts add up to $h$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[The Fourier transform needs $(2.78-o(1))N$ gates]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-dft}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.half_le_of_dft,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.Linear.Realization.half_le_of_dft,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.Linear.eventually_lt_size_dft_of_orderingBound,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.Linear.eventually_lt_size_dft,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.Linear.eventually_lt_size_dft_twentyFive_div_nine,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.Linear.eventually_lt_size_complexDFT,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.Linear.eventually_lt_size_fft,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.eventually_lt_size_dft_of_orderingBound,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.eventually_lt_size_dft,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.eventually_lt_size_dft_twentyFive_div_nine,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.exists_isPrimitiveRoot_zmod,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.eventually_lt_size_ntt}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-dft,
    lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-dft-half-cut,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-multi-output-ordering,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-rank-cut,
    lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-linear-realization-cut,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-gaussian-coefficient}
  Let $\omega$ be a primitive $N$-th root of unity in a field $F$, for any $N$. Consider a
  fan-in-two circuit with $s$ gates computing $x\mapsto F_Nx$ which is either linear over $F$
  (it has a local linear realization: each gate computes a linear combination of its arguments,
  with arbitrary coefficients) or, when $F$ is finite, has arbitrary gates $F\times F\to F$.
  Under $\mathrm{OrderingBound}(A,\eta,C)$ with $A+\eta\ge0$,
  \[
    \lfloor N/2\rfloor\le(A+\eta)\max\{s-N,0\}+3\log_2(N+3s)+C .
  \]
  Consequently, if the ordering hypothesis holds with coefficient $A>0$ at every positive slack,
  then for every $\varepsilon>0$ and all large $N$, independently of the field, the root of
  unity, and the signature, every such circuit has more than $(1+1/(2A)-\varepsilon)N$ gates.
  With the edge-score coefficient $A=2p$ this is
  $(1+\kappa/2-\varepsilon)N\approx(2.78-\varepsilon)N$, with
  $\kappa=\pi/(3\arccos\tfrac{1+2\sqrt2}{4})$, and more than $(25/9-\varepsilon)N$. This applies
  to the complex DFT with $\omega=e^{2\pi i/N}$, including $N=2^k$, and to number-theoretic
  transforms over $\mathbb{Z}/q$ for a prime $q$ with $N\mid q-1$, where a primitive $N$-th root
  of unity exists.

  Valiant (1975) observed that a linear circuit computing the Fourier transform is a
  hyperconcentrator (Definition~\ref{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-hyperconcentrator}). For prime
  $N$ every minor of the complex Fourier matrix is nonsingular (Chebotar\"ev), so such circuits
  are superconcentrators, and Lev and Valiant (1983) derived $4N-o(N)$ additions. For general $N$
  Lokam (\emph{Complexity lower bounds using linear algebra}, 2009) and Ailon (arXiv:1403.1307)
  record no superlinear lower bound for unrestricted linear circuits; the bound here is linear.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  The Fourier matrix is not totally regular (for $N=4$, rows and columns $\{0,2\}$ give a
  singular block), so Theorem~\ref{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-totally-regular} does not apply.
  The rank-cut inequality holds at every split, by
  Theorem~\ref{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-rank-cut} for finite fields and by
  Lemma~\ref{lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-linear-realization-cut} for linear circuits. The
  component of the wire of output $0$, whose row has no zero entry, is closed and so crossed by
  no signal; the rank-cut inequality then puts every input into it. Along the ranking of
  Theorem~\ref{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-multi-output-ordering}, the number of inputs in a
  prefix grows by at most one per wire, so the prefix ending at some input holds exactly
  $\lfloor N/2\rfloor$ inputs. By Lemma~\ref{lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-dft-half-cut} it is
  crossed by at least $\lfloor N/2\rfloor$ signals, and it is charged to the component holding
  all $N$ inputs and at most $s$ gates. For the asymptotic form, assume at most
  $(1+1/(2A)-\varepsilon)N$ gates and choose $\eta=A^2\varepsilon$: the cycle term is at most
  $(1/2-A\varepsilon/2)N$, and the logarithmic term is $O(\log N)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Formal differentiation preserves the circuit graph]
  \label{lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-polynomial-linearization}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.Polynomial.realization,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.Polynomial.realization_output}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-polynomial-realization}
  A polynomial interpretation gives a local linear realization on the same
  program. Over an infinite field, if the circuit computes $x\mapsto Mx$ as
  a function, its realization's output rows are exactly the rows of $M$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Take the vector of formal partial derivatives at zero of every wire's
  polynomial. Polynomial induction and the product rule put each gate vector
  in the span of its argument vectors, including nonzero constant terms and
  in every characteristic. Over an infinite field, equality of evaluated
  output functions identifies their formal polynomials and linear rows.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[The Gaussian bound for polynomial gates over every field]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-polynomial-totally-regular}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.Polynomial.blockRank_add_blockRank_le,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.Polynomial.sub_one_le_of_totallyRegular,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.Polynomial.eventually_lt_size_of_totallyRegular,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.Polynomial.eventually_lt_size}
  \leanok
  \uses{lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-linear-realization-cut,
    lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-polynomial-linearization,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-totally-regular}
  For every $\varepsilon>0$, all sufficiently large $n$, every field $F$,
  and every totally regular $n\times n$ matrix $M$, every circuit computing
  all coordinates of $Mx$ with polynomial gates of at most two input slots
  has more than
  \[
    \left(1+\frac{\pi}{3\arccos((1+2\sqrt2)/4)}-\varepsilon\right)n
  \]
  gates. The threshold is uniform over fields, matrices, signatures, and
  gate polynomials. Degrees, coefficients, depth, and fanout are unrestricted.
  Coefficients are included in gate descriptions; explicit nullary gate
  occurrences count in this theorem's size.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  On infinite fields use the formal realization and its rank-cut bound.
  On finite fields use the existing cardinality rank-cut theorem. Both feed
  the same graph-layout proof of
  $n-1\le(A+\eta)(s-n)^++3\log_2(n+3s)+C$ and its Gaussian asymptotics.
  No separate construction of vertex-disjoint paths is needed.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[One rational Cauchy family over characteristic-zero fields]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-polynomial-cauchy}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.totallyRegular_cauchyCharZero,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.cauchyCharZero_eq_map_rat,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.Polynomial.eventually_lt_size_cauchyCharZero,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.Polynomial.eventually_lt_size_cauchyCharZero_full}
  \leanok
  \uses{lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-cauchy,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-polynomial-totally-regular}
  Let $M_n(i,j)=1/(i-(n+j))$ for $0\le i,j<n$. This is one rational matrix
  family, interpreted over any characteristic-zero field. For every
  $\varepsilon>0$, all sufficiently large $n$, and every such field, computing
  $M_nx$ requires more than $(L-\varepsilon)n$ polynomial gates of fan-in at
  most two, with $L=1+\pi/(3\arccos((1+2\sqrt2)/4))$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  The nodes $0,\ldots,n-1$ and $n,\ldots,2n-1$ remain distinct in every
  characteristic-zero field, so all square minors are nonsingular. Apply
  the polynomial-gate theorem, uniformly over the field and gate basis.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Arithmetic lower bounds with free constants]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-arithmetic-free-constants}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.Polynomial.exists_polynomial_circuit_of_nonconstant,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.Polynomial.eventually_lt_arithmeticCost_of_totallyRegular,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.Polynomial.eventually_lt_arithmeticCost_cauchyCharZero}
  \leanok
  \uses{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-polynomial-totally-regular,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-polynomial-cauchy}
  The same eventual lower bound $(L-\varepsilon)n$ holds for the number of
  additions and multiplications in an arithmetic circuit, with arbitrary
  constant gates free, computing a totally regular linear map over any field.
  In particular it holds for the rational Cauchy family over every
  characteristic-zero field.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Track an original wire as either a literal constant or a retained signal.
  A constant gate emits no node. Each addition or multiplication emits one
  binary polynomial gate with its literal constant arguments substituted
  into the polynomial. Ignored argument slots use an existing input; this
  requires only $n>0$. Every target output is nonconstant, as its matrix row
  has a nonzero entry. Thus each output is a retained signal, with no final
  constant gate needed. The compiled size equals the original arithmetic
  cost exactly; apply the polynomial theorem.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Quadratic forms and a Hankel Cauchy matrix]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-quadratic-form}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.quadForm,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.hankelCauchyZMod}
  \leanok
  The quadratic form of a square matrix $M$ is $x\mapsto x^{\mathsf T}Mx$.
  Over $\mathbb{Z}/q$, the $N\times N$ Hankel Cauchy matrix has entries
  $1/(i+j+2)$ for $0\le i,j<N$.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Rank-cut bound for quadratic forms]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-quadratic-rank-cut}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.trace_add_trace_eq_trace_mix_add_trace_mix,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.quadForm_add_quadForm_eq,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.card_mul_card_le_of_dotProduct_mulVec_eq_zero,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.blockRank_add_transpose_le_of_trace,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.blockRank_add_transpose_le,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.blockRank_add_transpose_le_of_sum_of_trace,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.blockRank_add_transpose_le_of_sum}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-quadratic-form,
    def:lowerbounds-algebraic-multi-output,
    lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-single-cut-fibres}
  Let a circuit over a finite field $F$, over any signature, compute the
  quadratic form of an $N\times N$ matrix $M$, and let $H=M+M^{\mathsf T}$.
  For every split $S$ of its wires, with complement $T$, forward signals $A$,
  backward signals $B$, and inputs $X_S$ and $X_T$ on the two sides,
  \[
    \operatorname{rank} H[X_S, X_T] \le |A| + |B| .
  \]
  The same holds for a circuit with several outputs $f_o$ when, for some
  coefficients $c_o\in F$, the combination $x\mapsto\sum_o c_of_o(x)$ is
  the quadratic form of $M$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  The inputs with a fixed boundary key form a product $P_S\times P_T$ of
  left and right parts
  (Lemma~\ref{lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-single-cut-fibres}). Mixing two
  members of a class only exchanges the values of a wire on one side of the
  split, so the mixed second difference
  $f(x_S,x_T)-f(x'_S,x_T)-f(x_S,x'_T)+f(x'_S,x'_T)$ of the output vanishes
  on the class, wherever the output lies. For a quadratic form the pure
  terms cancel and this difference is the cross term
  $(x_S-x'_S)^{\mathsf T}H[X_S,X_T](x_T-x'_T)$. So for $l,l'\in P_S$ and
  $r,r'\in P_T$, $(l-l')^{\mathsf T}H[X_S,X_T](r-r')=0$. Let $V$ have the
  columns $r-r_0$ for a fixed $r_0\in P_T$; then $P_T$ embeds in the column
  space of $V$, the differences $l-l_0$ lie in the kernel of
  $(H[X_S,X_T]V)^{\mathsf T}$, and by Sylvester's rank inequality
  $|P_S|\,|P_T|\le|F|^{|X_S|-\operatorname{rank}(H[X_S,X_T]V)+\operatorname{rank}V}
  \le|F|^{N-\operatorname{rank}H[X_S,X_T]}$. Summing over the at most
  $|F|^{|A|+|B|}$ classes gives $|F|^N\le|F|^{|A|+|B|}\,
  |F|^{N-\operatorname{rank}H[X_S,X_T]}$. Only the vanishing of the mixed
  second difference on the classes is used; for a combination of outputs it
  vanishes for each output wire, hence for the combination.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[A symmetric totally regular Cauchy matrix]
  \label{lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-hankel-cauchy}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.totallyRegular_add_transpose,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.hankelCauchyZMod_eq_cauchy,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.totallyRegular_hankelCauchyZMod,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.totallyRegular_hankelCauchyZMod_add_transpose}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-quadratic-form,
    lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-cauchy}
  If $M$ is symmetric and totally regular and $2\ne0$ in the field, then
  $M+M^{\mathsf T}$ is totally regular. For a prime $q>2N$ the Hankel Cauchy
  matrix over $\mathbb{Z}/q$ is the Cauchy matrix with nodes $x_i=i+1$ and
  $y_j=-(j+1)$, so it and its symmetrization are totally regular.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  For symmetric $M$, $M+M^{\mathsf T}=2M$, and a square block of $2M$ has
  determinant $2^k$ times that of $M$. The nodes $x_i$ are distinct, the
  $y_j$ are distinct, and $x_i-y_j=i+j+2$ is nonzero modulo $q$ since
  $0<i+j+2\le2N<q$; as $q$ is odd, $2\ne0$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Quadratic forms need $(1+1/(4p)-\varepsilon)N$ gates]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-quadratic}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.input_mem_component_of_quadForm,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.half_le_of_quadForm,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.eventually_lt_size_of_quadForm_of_orderingBound,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.eventually_lt_size_of_quadForm,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.eventually_lt_size_of_quadForm_twentyFive_div_nine,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.eventually_lt_size_hankelCauchyZMod}
  \leanok
  \uses{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-multi-output-ordering,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-quadratic-rank-cut,
    lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-cauchy,
    lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-hankel-cauchy,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-gaussian-coefficient}
  Let a circuit over a finite field $F$, over any signature with fan-in at
  most two (so with arbitrary functions $F\times F\to F$, unary functions,
  and constants as gates, which covers arithmetic circuits over $F$), compute
  with $s$ gates the quadratic form of an $N\times N$ matrix $M$ such that
  $M+M^{\mathsf T}$ is totally regular. Under
  $\mathrm{OrderingBound}(A,\eta,C)$ with $A+\eta\ge0$,
  \[
    \lfloor N/2\rfloor \le (A+\eta)\max\{s-N,0\} + 3\log_2(N+3s) + C .
  \]
  Consequently, if the ordering hypothesis holds with coefficient $A>0$ at
  every positive slack, then for every $\varepsilon>0$ and all large $N$,
  independently of the field, the matrix, and the signature, every such
  circuit has more than $(1+1/(2A)-\varepsilon)N$ gates. With the Gaussian
  pathwidth coefficient $p$ of
  Theorem~\ref{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-gaussian-coefficient} and $A=2p$
  this is $(1+1/(4p)-\varepsilon)N\approx(2.7812-\varepsilon)N$, and as
  $2p\le9/32$ it is more than $(25/9-\varepsilon)N$; in particular for the
  quadratic form of the Hankel Cauchy matrix over $\mathbb{Z}/q$ with $q>2N$
  prime.

  For context, no superlinear lower bound is known for general arithmetic
  circuits computing an explicit polynomial of constant degree; Ramya and
  Shastri (arXiv:2509.11322, 2025) prove $\Omega(n\log n)$ for planar
  circuits computing totally regular bilinear forms.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  The component of an input wire is closed, so it is crossed by no signal,
  and by the rank-cut bound
  (Theorem~\ref{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-quadratic-rank-cut}) the block of
  $M+M^{\mathsf T}$ between its inputs and the other inputs has rank zero;
  by total regularity the component contains every input. Along the ranking
  of Theorem~\ref{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-multi-output-ordering}, the
  number of inputs in a prefix grows by at most one per wire, so the prefix
  ending at some input holds exactly $\lfloor N/2\rfloor$ inputs. Its block
  has rank at least $\min\{\lfloor N/2\rfloor,\lceil N/2\rceil\}=\lfloor
  N/2\rfloor$ (Lemma~\ref{lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-cauchy}), and the prefix
  is charged to the component containing all $N$ inputs and at most $s$
  gates. For the asymptotic form, assume at most $(1+1/(2A)-\varepsilon)N$
  gates, choose $\eta=A^2\varepsilon$, and compare with
  $\lfloor N/2\rfloor\ge(N-1)/2$: the cycle term is at most
  $(1/2-A\varepsilon/2)N$, and the logarithmic term is $O(\log N)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Restricted rank-cut bound]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-restricted-rank-cut}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.card_le_of_fibres_of_mix_of_trace,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.card_piFinset_le_of_fibres,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.supportedKernel,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.card_supportedKernel_le,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.card_pow_le_supportedKernel,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.card_pow_le_supportedKernel_of_trace,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.apply_eq_zero_of_closed_of_trace}
  \leanok
  \uses{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-rank-cut,
    lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-single-cut-fibres}
  Let a circuit over any signature with values in a finite set $U$ compute
  $f\colon U^n\to U^m$, and let $S$ be a split of its wires with forward
  signals $A$ and backward signals $B$. If $D=D_0\times\dots\times D_{n-1}$
  is a coordinatewise product domain, every left fibre taken within $D$ has
  at most $D_T$ elements, and every right fibre within $D$ at most $D_S$,
  then $|D|\le|U|^{|A|+|B|}D_TD_S$; the same holds for every set of inputs
  closed under mixing.

  Now let $U$ be a finite abelian group, $J$ a set of free coordinates and
  $z_0$ a base point, and suppose that $f(z')-f(z)=g(z'-z)$ whenever $z$ and
  $z'$ agree with $z_0$ outside $J$. For sets $P$ of coordinates and $Q$ of
  outputs, let $\ker_g(P,Q)$ be the set of vectors supported on $P$ whose
  image under $g$ vanishes on $Q$. With $X_S$ and $X_T$ the free inputs in
  $S$ and outside $S$, and $Y_S$ and $Y_T$ the outputs carried in $S$ and
  outside $S$,
  \[
    |U|^{|J|} \le |U|^{|A|+|B|}\,|\ker_g(X_S,Y_T)|\,|\ker_g(X_T,Y_S)| .
  \]
  For a linear $g$ this is the rank-cut bound of
  Theorem~\ref{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-rank-cut} restricted to the free
  inputs. If $S$ is crossed by no signal, both kernels are full: $g$ maps
  vectors supported on $X_S$ to vectors vanishing on $Y_T$, and vectors
  supported on $X_T$ to vectors vanishing on $Y_S$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  The proof of the rank-cut bound only mixes two inputs with equal boundary
  keys, and the mixture of two members of a product domain lies in it. So
  the inputs in the domain with a fixed key form a product of left and right
  parts (Lemma~\ref{lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-single-cut-fibres}), the left
  parts embed into the left fibre, within the domain, of any member, and
  summing over the at most $|U|^{|A|+|B|}$ keys bounds the domain. The
  inputs agreeing with $z_0$ outside $J$ form a product domain with
  $|U|^{|J|}$ members, and subtracting the base point maps a left fibre
  injectively into $\ker_g(X_S,Y_T)$, and a right fibre into
  $\ker_g(X_T,Y_S)$. A kernel has at most $|U|^{|P|}$ members, and
  $|X_S|+|X_T|=|J|$; without crossing signals the bound forces both
  kernels to have exactly that many.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Multiplication in coordinates]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-field-mul}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.fieldMul}
  \leanok
  Let $K$ be an algebra over $F$ with a basis $b_0,\dots,b_{n-1}$.
  Multiplication in $K$, written in coordinates, is the map
  $F^{n+n}\to F^n$ sending the coordinates $x$ and $y$ of two elements to
  the coordinates of their product
  $\bigl(\sum_i x_ib_i\bigr)\bigl(\sum_j y_jb_j\bigr)$. The first $n$
  inputs are the coordinates of the first factor and the last $n$ inputs
  those of the second.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Multiplication in a degree-$n$ extension needs $(2+1/(2p)-\varepsilon)n$ gates]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-field-mul}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.fieldMul_append_equivFun,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.mem_component_of_fieldMul,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.le_add_two_of_fieldMul,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.sub_two_le_of_fieldMul,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.eventually_lt_size_of_fieldMul_of_orderingBound,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.eventually_lt_size_of_fieldMul,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.eventually_lt_size_of_fieldMul_fifty_div_nine,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.nonempty_basis_galoisField,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.eventually_lt_size_galoisField,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.eventually_lt_size_galoisField_fifty_div_nine}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-field-mul,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-restricted-rank-cut,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-multi-output-ordering,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-gaussian-coefficient}
  Let $K$ be a finite field of degree $n$ over a finite field $F$, with any
  basis, and let a circuit over $F$, over any signature with fan-in at most
  two (so with arbitrary functions $F\times F\to F$, unary functions, and
  constants as gates), compute multiplication in $K$ in coordinates with $s$
  gates. Every split of its wires holding $a$ inputs of the first factor and
  $n-a$ outputs is crossed by at least $n-2$ signals, and all inputs and
  outputs lie in one component of the wire graph. Under
  $\mathrm{OrderingBound}(A,\eta,C)$ with $A+\eta\ge0$,
  \[
    n - 2 \le (A+\eta)\max\{s-2n,0\} + 3\log_2(2n+3s) + C .
  \]
  Consequently, if the ordering hypothesis holds with coefficient $A>0$ at
  every positive slack, then for every $\varepsilon>0$ and all large $n$,
  independently of the fields, the basis, and the signature, every such
  circuit has more than $(2+1/A-\varepsilon)n$ gates. With the Gaussian
  pathwidth coefficient $p$ of
  Theorem~\ref{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-gaussian-coefficient} this is
  $(2+1/(2p)-\varepsilon)n\approx(5.5625-\varepsilon)n$, and as $2p\le9/32$
  it is more than $(50/9-\varepsilon)n$. In particular, multiplication in
  $\mathrm{GF}(2^n)$ over $\mathrm{GF}(2)$, in any basis, needs more than
  $(2+1/(2p)-\varepsilon)n$ gates over the full binary basis.

  Earlier lower bounds for this multiplication count only the
  multiplications of bilinear or quadratic algorithms; we know of no earlier
  bound on the number of gates over the full binary basis. The coefficient
  is not claimed to be a record for explicit functions with $2n$ inputs and
  $n$ outputs in general.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Fixing the second factor $y$ makes the map linear in $x$: it is
  multiplication by $y$. By
  Theorem~\ref{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-restricted-rank-cut} on the inputs
  with second factor $y$, a split $S$ satisfies
  $|F|^n\le|F|^{|A|+|B|}k_1(y)k_2(y)$, where $k_1(y)$ and $k_2(y)$ are the
  sizes of the kernels of the blocks of multiplication by $y$ with columns
  $X_S$ and rows $Y_T$, and with columns $X_T$ and rows $Y_S$. Sum a kernel
  with columns $P$ and rows $Q$ over all $y$: the zero vector lies in every
  kernel, and for a nonzero $u$ the map $y\mapsto uy$ is a bijection of $K$,
  so exactly $|F|^{n-|Q|}$ values of $y$ put $u$ in the kernel. The sum is
  at most $|F|^n+|F|^{|P|+n-|Q|}\le2|F|^n$ when $|P|\le|Q|$. If $S$ holds
  $a$ inputs of the first factor and $n-a$ outputs, both blocks have
  $|P|=|Q|$, so some $y$ has $k_1(y)+k_2(y)\le4$, hence $k_1(y)k_2(y)\le
  4\le|F|^2$ and $n\le|A|+|B|+2$.

  The component of an output is crossed by no signal, so both kernels are
  full for every $y$. Since $b_j\cdot b_j^{-1}b_i=b_i$, a suitable $y$ makes
  any entry of the block nonzero; hence the component holds every input of
  the first factor and every output, and, fixing $x$ instead of $y$, every
  input of the second factor. The outputs are distinct wires (take $y=1$)
  and none is an input of the first factor (take $y=0$), so along the
  ranking of Theorem~\ref{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-multi-output-ordering}
  the number of first-factor inputs and outputs in a prefix grows by at most
  one per wire, and some prefix ending at one of them holds exactly $n$. It
  is charged to the component holding all $2n$ inputs and at most $s$
  gates. For the asymptotic form, assume at most $(2+1/A-\varepsilon)n$
  gates, choose $\eta=A^2\varepsilon/2$, and compare: the cycle term is at
  most $(1-A\varepsilon/2)n$, and the logarithmic term is $O(\log n)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Matrix multiplication in coordinates]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-mat-mul}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.matMul,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.matMulLeft,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.matMulRight,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.matMulOutput,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.matMulInput,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.matMulTermA,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.matMulTermB,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.matMulTermC}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-multi-output}
  The product $C=AB$ of two $n\times n$ matrices over a semiring $R$ is the
  map $R^{n^2+n^2}\to R^{n^2}$ whose output $C_{ik}$ is
  $\sum_jA_{ij}B_{jk}$: the first $n^2$ inputs hold the entries $A_{ij}$ of
  the first factor and the last $n^2$ inputs the entries $B_{jk}$ of the
  second, index pairs numbered row by row. In a circuit computing it, the
  \emph{terminals} are the wires of the inputs $A_{ij}$ and $B_{jk}$ and the
  output wires carrying $C_{ik}$.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[The terminal graph of matrix multiplication]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-mat-mul-terminal-graph}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.Tripartite.rowSet,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.Tripartite.colSet,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.Tripartite.degI,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.Tripartite.degJ,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.Tripartite.degK,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.Tripartite.minority,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.Tripartite.chargeI,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.Tripartite.chargeJ,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.Tripartite.chargeK,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.Tripartite.heavyI,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.Tripartite.heavyJ,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.Tripartite.heavyK,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.Tripartite.heavyCount,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.Tripartite.mixedPairs,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.Tripartite.lightHeavy,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.Tripartite.place}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-mat-mul}
  The terminals of $n\times n$ matrix multiplication are the edges of the
  complete tripartite graph on three copies $I$, $J$, $K$ of
  $\{0,\dots,n-1\}$: $A_{ij}$ joins $i$ and $j$, $B_{jk}$ joins $j$ and $k$,
  and $C_{ik}$ joins $i$ and $k$, so every vertex has degree $2n$. A set of
  placed terminals (those whose wires lie in a split $S$) places $d(v)$
  terminals at each vertex $v$. The \emph{minority} at $v$ is
  $g(d(v))=\min\{d(v),2n-d(v)\}$, and the \emph{charges} are
  $R_I=\sum_{i\in I}g(d(i))$, $R_J=\sum_{j\in J}g(d(j))$ and
  $R_K=\sum_{k\in K}g(d(k))$. A vertex is \emph{heavy} when $d(v)\ge n$ and
  light otherwise.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Charging the terminal graph]
  \label{lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-mat-mul-charging}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.Tripartite.minority_of_lt,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.Tripartite.minority_of_le,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.Tripartite.sum_card_row,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.Tripartite.sum_card_col,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.Tripartite.lightHeavy_le_charge,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.Tripartite.card_mixedPairs,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.Tripartite.three_mul_sq_le_two_mul_lightHeavy,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.Tripartite.three_mul_sq_le_two_mul_charge,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.Tripartite.heavyCount_le_add_two,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.Tripartite.exists_threshold}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-mat-mul-terminal-graph,
    def:lowerbounds-algebraic-multi-output}
  For every set of placed terminals, the number $P$ of terminals with one
  light and one heavy endpoint satisfies $P\le R_I+R_J+R_K$. If the number
  $h$ of heavy vertices satisfies $3n\le2h\le3n+3$, then
  \[
    3n^2 \le 2P + 3 \le 2(R_I+R_J+R_K) + 3 .
  \]
  Placing one more terminal adds at most two heavy vertices. Consequently,
  if $n\ge1$ and the $3n^2$ terminals of a program lie on distinct wires,
  then along every injective ranking of the wires some prefix of the form
  $\{w : \mathrm{rank}(w)\le\mathrm{rank}(w_0)\}$, with $w_0$ a terminal,
  has $3n\le2h\le3n+3$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Charge a light--heavy terminal to its light endpoint if it is placed and
  to its heavy endpoint otherwise. At a light vertex $g(d)=d$ counts its
  placed terminals, at a heavy vertex $g(d)=2n-d$ counts its unplaced
  ones, so every vertex pays for the terminals charged to it. With $x$, $y$,
  $z$ heavy vertices in $I$, $J$, $K$ and $h=x+y+z$,
  $P=x(n-y)+(n-x)y+y(n-z)+(n-y)z+x(n-z)+(n-x)z=2nh-2(xy+yz+zx)$, and
  $3(xy+yz+zx)\le h^2$, so
  $3(2P+3-3n^2)\ge9-(2h-3n)^2\ge0$. The degrees sum to twice the number of
  placed terminals, so placing one terminal raises the degrees by two in
  total and the heavy count by at most two. Along the ranking the heavy
  count of the prefixes climbs from $0$ to $3n$ in steps of at most two; the
  first prefix with at least $\lceil3n/2\rceil$ heavy vertices has at most
  $\lceil3n/2\rceil+1$, and it ends at a terminal, since the heavy count
  changes there.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[The charges bound the crossing signals]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-mat-mul-charges}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.terminal_injective_of_matMul,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.mem_component_of_matMul,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.charges_le_of_matMul_of_totallyRegular,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.charges_le_add_of_matMul}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-mat-mul-terminal-graph,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-restricted-rank-cut,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-quadratic-rank-cut,
    lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-cauchy,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-multi-output-ordering}
  Let a circuit over a finite field $F$, over any signature, compute
  $n\times n$ matrix multiplication. Its $3n^2$ terminals lie on distinct
  wires, and if $n\ge1$ they all lie in the component of the output
  $C_{00}$ in the wire graph. Let $S$ be a set of its wires with forward
  signals $A$ and backward signals $B$, and let $R_I$, $R_J$, $R_K$ be the
  charges of the terminals placed in $S$. If $F$ has a totally regular
  $n\times n$ matrix, in particular if $F=\mathbb{Z}/q$ for a prime
  $q\ge2n$, then
  \[
    R_I,\ R_J,\ R_K \le |A| + |B| ;
  \]
  over every finite field, $R_I,R_J,R_K\le|A|+|B|+4n$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Fix the second factor to a matrix $B_0$. The product changes by
  $\delta A\mapsto\delta A\,B_0$, which is block diagonal over the rows $i$,
  and Theorem~\ref{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-restricted-rank-cut} on these
  inputs gives $|F|^{n^2}\le|F|^{|A|+|B|}\prod_i k_1(i)k_2(i)$, where
  $k_1(i)$ and $k_2(i)$ count the left kernels of the blocks of $B_0$ with
  rows the placed (respectively unplaced) entries $A_{ij}$ and columns the
  unplaced (respectively placed) outputs $C_{ik}$. With $a$ and $c$ placed
  entries of the two kinds at $i$, a totally regular $B_0$ gives these blocks
  ranks $\min\{a,n-c\}$ and $\min\{n-a,c\}$, which sum to $g(a+c)$; so
  $R_I\le|A|+|B|$. Fixing the first factor gives $R_K$ over the columns
  $k$. For $R_J$, the combination $\sum_{ik}\Lambda_{ik}C_{ik}$ is a
  quadratic form whose Hessian pairs $A_{ij}$ with $B_{jk}$ through
  $\Lambda_{ik}$; by Theorem~\ref{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-quadratic-rank-cut}
  its cross block between the inputs in $S$ and outside $S$ has rank at most
  $|A|+|B|$, and its kernel is block diagonal over $j$, with blocks of
  $\Lambda$ and $\Lambda^{\mathsf T}$ whose ranks again sum to $g(d(j))$.
  Over an arbitrary finite field, choose the matrix after $S$, uniformly at
  random. For a block with columns $P$ and rows $Q$, a nonzero vector
  supported on $P$ lies in the kernel for at most an $|F|^{-|Q|}$ fraction
  of all matrices, so the kernel sizes average at most twice the ideal size
  $|F|^e$, $e=|P|-\min\{|P|,|Q|\}$. A kernel of size $k$ has
  $k<|F|^{d+1}$ with $d=\lfloor\log_{|F|}k\rfloor$, and its excess exponent
  $d+1-e$ is at most $k/|F|^e$, so the excess averages at most $2$ for each
  of the $2n$ blocks of a part; some matrix has total excess at most $4n$,
  which is the loss.
  The component of $C_{00}$ is crossed by no signal, so with a matrix unit
  as the fixed factor the slices separate; the unit vector at $A_{ij}$ moves
  $C_{ik}$ when $B_0=E_{jk}$, and the unit vector at $B_{jk}$ moves $C_{ik}$
  when the first factor is $E_{ij}$, so the component spreads from $C_{00}$
  to every terminal. Distinct terminals differ on a suitable input built
  from matrix units.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Matrix multiplication needs $(2+1/(4p)-\varepsilon)n^2$ gates]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-mat-mul}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.matMul_matMulInput,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.half_sq_le_of_matMul_of_totallyRegular,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.half_sq_le_of_matMul_zmod,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.sq_sub_le_of_matMul,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.eventually_lt_size_of_matMul_of_orderingBound,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.eventually_lt_size_of_matMul,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.eventually_lt_size_of_matMul_thirtyFour_div_nine,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.eventually_lt_size_matMul_zmod_two,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.eventually_lt_size_matMul_zmod_two_thirtyFour_div_nine}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-mat-mul,
    lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-mat-mul-charging,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-mat-mul-charges,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-multi-output-ordering,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-gaussian-coefficient}
  Let a circuit over a finite field $F$, over any signature with fan-in at
  most two (so with arbitrary functions $F\times F\to F$, unary functions,
  and constants as gates), compute $n\times n$ matrix multiplication over
  $F$ with $s$ gates. Under $\mathrm{OrderingBound}(A,\eta,C)$ with
  $A+\eta\ge0$,
  \[
    \frac{n^2-8n-1}{2} \le (A+\eta)\max\{s-2n^2,0\} + 3\log_2(2n^2+3s) + C ,
  \]
  and the left side improves to $(n^2-1)/2$ when $F$ has a totally regular
  $n\times n$ matrix, in particular over $\mathbb{Z}/q$ for a prime
  $q\ge2n$. Consequently, if the ordering hypothesis holds with coefficient
  $A>0$ at every positive slack, then for every $\varepsilon>0$ and all
  large $n$, independently of the field and the signature, every such
  circuit has more than $(2+1/(2A)-\varepsilon)n^2$ gates. With the Gaussian
  pathwidth coefficient $p$ of
  Theorem~\ref{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-gaussian-coefficient} and $A=2p$
  this is $(2+1/(4p)-\varepsilon)n^2\approx(3.7812-\varepsilon)n^2$, and as
  $2p\le9/32$ it is more than $(34/9-\varepsilon)n^2$. In particular, every
  circuit over the full binary basis computing the product of two
  $n\times n$ matrices over $\mathrm{GF}(2)$ has more than
  $(2+1/(4p)-\varepsilon)n^2$ gates.

  Lower bounds for matrix multiplication usually count multiplications in
  arithmetic or bilinear models: Bläser (STACS 2001) proves that
  $\tfrac52n^2-3n$ nonscalar multiplications are needed, and Shpilka
  (SICOMP 2003) $3n^2-o(n^2)$ product gates over $\mathrm{GF}(2)$ for
  bilinear and quadratic circuits. We know of no earlier bound beyond about
  $2n^2$ on the number of gates of circuits with arbitrary fan-in-two gates,
  or over the full binary basis, computing the matrix product. The
  coefficient is not claimed to be a record for explicit functions with
  $2n^2$ inputs and $n^2$ outputs in general.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  By Theorem~\ref{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-mat-mul-charges} the terminals
  lie on distinct wires in one component. Along the ranking of
  Theorem~\ref{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-multi-output-ordering}, take the
  threshold prefix of Lemma~\ref{lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-mat-mul-charging}:
  it ends at a terminal and $3n^2\le2(R_I+R_J+R_K)+3$. Each charge is at most
  the number $w$ of crossing signals plus a loss $L$ ($L=0$ with a totally
  regular matrix, $L=4n$ in general), so $w\ge(n^2-2L-1)/2$, and the prefix
  is charged to the component holding all $2n^2$ inputs and at most $s$
  gates. For the asymptotic form, assume at most
  $(2+1/(2A)-\varepsilon)n^2$ gates and choose $\eta=A^2\varepsilon$: the
  cycle term is at most $(1/2-A\varepsilon/2)n^2$, while the loss $4n$ and
  the logarithmic term $O(\log n)$ are $o(n^2)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Polynomial gates over commutative algebras]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-taylor-formal}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.Taylor.algebraInterpretation,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.Taylor.wirePolynomial,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.Taylor.FormallyComputes,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.Taylor.jacobian,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.Taylor.hessian}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-multi-output,
    def:lowerbounds-algebraic-polynomial-realization}
  Let every operation of a signature carry a polynomial in its argument
  slots with coefficients in a commutative ring $K$, of any degree. Over
  every commutative $K$-algebra $A$ the program is evaluated by substituting
  the arguments of each gate into its polynomial. The wire polynomial
  $f_w\in K[X_0,\dots,X_{n-1}]$ of a wire $w$ is its value over the
  polynomial ring at the variables, and a circuit \emph{formally computes}
  polynomials $f_o$ when its output $o$ carries $f_o$. For a $K$-algebra $L$
  and $a\in L^n$, the Jacobian of a family of polynomials and the Hessian of
  one polynomial at $a$ are the matrices of their formal first and second
  partial derivatives evaluated at $a$.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Formal and functional computation]
  \label{lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-taylor-formal}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.Taylor.trace_eq_aeval_wirePolynomial,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.Taylor.FormallyComputes.computes,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.Taylor.formallyComputes_of_computes}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-taylor-formal}
  Over every commutative $K$-algebra a wire carries its wire polynomial
  evaluated at the input, so a circuit formally computing polynomials
  computes them as functions over every such algebra. Conversely, over an
  infinite field, a circuit whose operations are polynomial functions and
  which computes polynomial functions formally computes those polynomials,
  for the polynomials chosen to represent its operations.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Evaluation at a point is an algebra homomorphism out of the polynomial
  ring, and algebra homomorphisms commute with substitution into the gate
  polynomials, hence with the whole program. Over an infinite field a
  polynomial is determined by its values.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[The Taylor cut lemma]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-taylor-cut}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.Taylor.exists_cut}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-taylor-formal,
    lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-taylor-formal,
    lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-single-cut-fibres,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-quadratic-rank-cut}
  Let a program have polynomial gates with coefficients in a commutative
  ring $K$, let $L$ be a field that is a $K$-algebra, let $a\in L^n$, and let
  $S$ be a split of the wires with complement $T$, forward signals $A$,
  backward signals $B$, and inputs $X_S$ and $X_T$ on the two sides. There
  are subspaces $V$ of vectors supported on $X_S$ and $W$ of vectors
  supported on $X_T$, with $\dim V+\dim W\ge n-|A|-|B|$, such that
  \begin{enumerate}
    \item[(a)] $\nabla f_t(a)\cdot v=0$ for every $v\in V$ and every wire $t$ of $T$;
