    \item[(b)] $\nabla f_s(a)\cdot u=0$ for every $u\in W$ and every wire $s$ of $S$;
    \item[(c)] $v^{\mathsf T}\,\nabla^2 f_w(a)\,u=0$ for all $v\in V$, $u\in W$, and
      every wire $w$.
  \end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Evaluate the program over $L[s,t]=L[s][t]$ with $s^2=t^2=0$. The
  first-order Taylor expansion over dual numbers, applied twice, shows that
  at $a+sv+tu$ every wire carries
  $f(a)+s\,\nabla f(a)\cdot v+t\,\nabla f(a)\cdot u+st\,v^{\mathsf T}\nabla^2
  f(a)\,u$. Let $Z$ be the directions along which no crossing wire moves to
  first order; it is cut out by at most $|A|+|B|$ linear equations. For
  $\delta\in Z$ the jets $a+s\delta$ and $a$ agree on every crossing wire,
  so they have the same boundary key, and cut and paste
  (Lemma~\ref{lem:lowerbounds-algebraic-single-cut-fibres}) between them
  shows, on the coefficient of $s$, that the part of $\delta$ on $X_S$ moves
  the wires of $S$ as $\delta$ does and fixes those of $T$, and symmetrically
  for the part on $X_T$. So both parts lie in $Z$; let $V$ and $W$ be the
  sets of these parts, so that $Z\subseteq V+W$, and (a) and (b) hold. For
  $v\in V$ and $u\in W$ the jets $a+sv$ and $a+tu$ agree on every crossing
  wire, their mixtures are $a+sv+tu$ and $a$, and the vanishing mixed second
  difference
  (Theorem~\ref{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-quadratic-rank-cut}) gives (c) on
  the coefficient of $st$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Rank consequences of the Taylor cut lemma]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-taylor-rank}
  \lean{Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.Taylor.blockRank_jacobian_add_blockRank_le_of_trace,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.Taylor.blockRank_jacobian_add_blockRank_le,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.Taylor.blockRank_hessian_le_of_trace,
    Algebraic.Cutwidth.MultiOutput.Taylor.blockRank_hessian_le}
  \leanok
  \uses{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-taylor-cut}
  In the setting of Theorem~\ref{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-taylor-cut}, let
  $O_S$ and $O_T$ be the outputs carried in $S$ and in $T$, and $J$ the
  Jacobian of the outputs at $a$. Then
  \[
    \operatorname{rank}J[O_T,X_S]+\operatorname{rank}J[O_S,X_T]\le|A|+|B| ,
  \]
  and for all coefficients $\mu_o\in L$ the Hessian
  $H=\sum_o\mu_o\nabla^2f_o(a)$ of the combination $\sum_o\mu_of_o$ satisfies
  $\operatorname{rank}H[X_S,X_T]\le|A|+|B|$. Both hold over every field,
  with the point and the coefficients in any field extension, for gate
  polynomials of any degree, arity, and coefficients.

  The rank of a Hessian block as a bound on the information two parties
  must exchange to evaluate a function of their joint inputs is due to
  Abelson (\emph{Lower bounds on information transfer in distributed
  computations}, J.~ACM 27, 1980); here it is applied at every cut of a
  circuit.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Restricted to its support, $V$ lies in the kernel of $J[O_T,X_S]$ by (a),
  and $W$ in that of $J[O_S,X_T]$ by (b). By (c) the restrictions of $V$ and
  $W$ are orthogonal through $H[X_S,X_T]$: for matrices $R_V$ and $C_W$ whose
  rows and columns are bases of $V$ and $W$, $R_VH[X_S,X_T]C_W=0$, and
  Sylvester's rank inequality applied twice gives
  $\dim V+\dim W+\operatorname{rank}H[X_S,X_T]\le n$. Compare with
  $\dim V+\dim W\ge n-|A|-|B|$.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[The KRW conjecture]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-algebraic-krw}
  \lean{Algebraic.KW.NonConstant, Algebraic.KW.KRWDepthWith,
    Algebraic.KW.KRWSizeWith, Algebraic.KW.KRWDepth, Algebraic.KW.KRWSize,
    Algebraic.KW.formulaDepth_compose_le, Algebraic.KW.formulaSize_compose_le,
    Algebraic.KW.not_forall_depth_of_constant_inner,
    Algebraic.KW.not_forall_depth_of_constant_outer,
    Algebraic.KW.not_forall_size_of_constant_inner,
    Algebraic.KW.not_forall_size_of_constant_outer}
  \leanok
  \uses{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-kw}
  Let $D(f)$ and $L(f)$ be the minimum depth and the minimum number of leaves,
  constant leaves included, of a De Morgan formula of
  Theorem~\ref{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-kw} computing $f$; they are defined
  for every number of inputs, $0$ included. For $f$ on $m$ bits and $g$ on $n$
  bits, the block composition $f \diamond g$ applies $f$ to the values of $g$
  on $m$ disjoint blocks of $n$ bits. Substituting formulas gives
  $D(f \diamond g) \le D(f) + D(g)$ and $L(f \diamond g) \le L(f)\,L(g)$ for
  all $f$ and $g$; both bounds are proved.

  The Karchmer--Raz--Wigderson conjecture says these bounds are nearly tight.
  It is formalized in its strong form, with a constant slack. The depth form
  says that there is a constant $c$ such that
  $D(f) + D(g) \le D(f \diamond g) + c$ for all $m$, $n$ and all non-constant
  $f$ and $g$. The size form says that there is a constant $c$ such that
  $L(f)\,L(g) \le c \cdot L(f \diamond g)$ for all such $f$ and $g$. The
  constant does not depend on $m$, $n$, $f$ or $g$; forms whose slack grows
  with $m$ or $n$ are not formalized. The conjecture is open; it is formalized
  as a statement only.

  Both non-constancy hypotheses are needed. If $f$ or $g$ is constant, so is
  $f \diamond g$, and it is proved that dropping either hypothesis makes the
  depth form and the size form false for every $c$.
\end{definition}

\section{The frontier method}
\label{sec:lowerbounds-frontier}

The cutwidth argument above is stated for Boolean circuits over the full binary
basis. The frontier method (namespace \texttt{Complexity.Frontier}) proves the
same bound in a general form: circuits over any finite alphabet $U$ with
$|U|\ge2$, over any basis (a CSLib signature with an interpretation on $U$),
with any accepting set $\mathrm{Acc}\subseteq U$, and with fan-in $r\ge2$. Only
gates of positive arity are counted (\texttt{Circuit.innerSize}), so constant
gates are free. The graph-theoretic input is a layout hypothesis measured by
the cycle rank. Gaussian layouts supply it in every fixed degree, and for fan-in
two the coefficient is that of
Theorem~\ref{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-gaussian-coefficient}. The development is
mirrored from a separate formalization by \texttt{scripts/sync\_frontier.py}. The same frontiers give bounds for linear maps, aggregate gates,
verifier circuits, and the average case, and they refine the counting by
signals, codes, pruned transitions, and trees of regions.

\begin{definition}[Rectangle-free sets and sweeps]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-frontier-sweep}
  \lean{Complexity.Frontier.rectangle, Complexity.Frontier.RectangleFree,
    Complexity.Frontier.Sweep, Complexity.Frontier.Sweep.Coherent}
  \leanok
  For a set of coordinates $X\subseteq\iota$, the rectangle with sides
  $A\subseteq U^X$ and $B\subseteq U^{X^c}$ consists of the inputs whose part on
  $X$ lies in $A$ and whose part on $X^c$ lies in $B$. A set $S\subseteq U^\iota$
  is $K$-rectangle-free when every rectangle contained in $S$, for every $X$, has
  a side with fewer than $K$ elements. A sweep of $S$ reveals coordinates over
  $T$ steps, from none to all, and sends at each step a message such that inputs
  of $S$ with the same message can exchange their unrevealed parts and remain in
  $S$. A sweep is coherent when such an exchange also keeps the message, and
  every other message equals that of one of the two inputs.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Frontier counting lemma]
  \label{lem:lowerbounds-frontier-counting}
  \lean{Complexity.Frontier.Sweep.ncard_le,
    Complexity.Frontier.Sweep.ncard_le_of_small_sides,
    Complexity.Frontier.RectangleFree.pow_ncard_compl_lt}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-frontier-sweep}
  If $S$ is $K$-rectangle-free with $|S|\ge K$, then for every sweep of $S$,
  \[
    |S| \le (K-1)^2 \sum_{t<T} \bigl|\{(m_t(x), m_{t+1}(x), x|_{\text{new}_t}) : x\in S\}\bigr|,
  \]
  where $m_t$ is the message at step $t$ and $x|_{\text{new}_t}$ the newly
  revealed coordinates. An asymmetric form allows different thresholds on the two
  sides. A $K$-rectangle-free set depending only on the coordinates $R$ satisfies
  $|U|^{|R^c|}<K$ or $|S|<|U|^{|R^c|}K$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Charge each input of $S$ to the first step at which its peers' revealed parts
  number at least $K$. At that step the peers form a rectangle, so their
  unrevealed parts number fewer than $K$, and the revealed parts at the previous
  step numbered fewer than $K$; each transition is therefore charged at most
  $(K-1)^2$ times.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[The layout hypothesis]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-frontier-layout-bound}
  \lean{Complexity.Frontier.Multigraph, Complexity.Frontier.Multigraph.cycleRank,
    Complexity.Frontier.Layout, Complexity.Frontier.LayoutBound}
  \leanok
  The cycle rank of a multigraph is $\beta_1=|E|+1-|V|$, truncated at zero. A layout
  numbers the vertices $0,\dots,|V|-1$. The layout hypothesis
  $\mathrm{LB}_d(A)$ says that for every $\eta>0$ some $C$ bounds, for every
  connected loopless multigraph of maximum degree $d$, some layout all of whose
  initial segments are crossed by at most $(A+\eta)\beta_1+\eta|V|+C$ edges.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Constraint networks over an alphabet]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-frontier-network}
  \lean{Complexity.Frontier.Network, Complexity.Frontier.Network.frontierSweep}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-frontier-layout-bound, def:lowerbounds-frontier-sweep}
  A network over $U$ is a multigraph whose edges carry values in $U$, with a
  local check at each vertex that sees the values of its incident edges and of
  the inputs read at that vertex; each input is read by at most one vertex. It
  accepts the inputs for which some edge assignment passes every check. A layout
  sweeps the accepted set: the message at step $t$ is the assignment on the edges
  crossing the first $t$ vertices.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Separator lemma and network counting]
  \label{lem:lowerbounds-frontier-network-counting}
  \lean{Complexity.Frontier.Network.Satisfies.splice,
    Complexity.Frontier.Network.ncard_accepted_le}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-frontier-network, lem:lowerbounds-frontier-counting}
  Two satisfying assignments that agree on the cut of a vertex set $L$ splice
  into a satisfying assignment of the spliced input. Hence a $K$-rectangle-free
  accepted set has at most
  $(K-1)^2\bigl(|V|\,|U|^{w+d+m}+|U|^{\text{unread}}\bigr)$ elements when some
  layout has width $w$, the maximum degree is $d$, and every vertex reads at most
  $m$ inputs.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Splice vertex by vertex: each check sees only edges on one side of the cut or
  edges of the cut itself. Apply Lemma~\ref{lem:lowerbounds-frontier-counting} to
  the frontier sweep, whose transitions are determined by the edge values at two
  adjacent frontiers and the inputs read at the new vertex; unread inputs are
  revealed in one final step.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Compiling circuits of fan-in $r$]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-frontier-compiler}
  \lean{Cslib.Circuits.Circuit.innerSize, Complexity.Frontier.Compiler.network,
    Complexity.Frontier.Compiler.accepted_network,
    Complexity.Frontier.Compiler.maxDegreeLE,
    Complexity.Frontier.Compiler.card_vertex_le,
    Complexity.Frontier.Compiler.cycleRank_add_ncard_read_le}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-frontier-network}
  A straight-line program of fan-in at most $r$ over any basis on $U$, with any
  family of outputs and accepting sets, compiles into an exact network of
  maximum degree $r+1$: signals, one junction per consumer, and output
  vertices. It accepts exactly the inputs whose outputs lie in their accepting
  sets, it has at most $2rs+3|O|$ vertices, and when it is connected its cycle
  rank plus the number of inputs it reads is at most $(r-1)s+1$, where $s$ counts
  the reachable gates of positive arity.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Each gate checks its operation against the junctions feeding it, and each
  junction chain copies one signal. An edge count over slots, junctions, and
  gates gives the cycle rank; constant gates have no slots and contribute
  nothing.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[The frontier lower bound]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-frontier-main}
  \lean{Complexity.Frontier.Decides, Complexity.Frontier.exists_graph_of_decides,
    Complexity.Frontier.eventually_lt_of_demand, Complexity.Frontier.lowerBound,
    Complexity.Frontier.lowerBound_fanInTwo}
  \leanok
  \uses{lem:lowerbounds-frontier-network-counting, thm:lowerbounds-frontier-compiler,
    def:lowerbounds-frontier-layout-bound}
  Let $r\ge2$, $A>0$, and assume $\mathrm{LB}_{r+1}(A)$. Let
  $S_n\subseteq U^n$ be $K_n$-rectangle-free with $\log K_n=o(n)$ and
  $n\log|U|-\log|S_n|=o(n)$. Then for every $\varepsilon>0$ and all large $n$,
  every circuit of fan-in at most $r$ deciding $S_n$, over any basis on $U$ and
  with any accepting set, has $s$ gates of positive arity with
  $(r-1)s>(1+1/A-\varepsilon)n$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  The support lemma forces all but $\log_{|U|}K$ inputs to be read, so the
  compiled network has cycle rank at most $(r-1)s-n+o(n)$. Every layout has width
  at least $\log_{|U|}|S_n|-o(n)=n-o(n)$ by
  Lemma~\ref{lem:lowerbounds-frontier-network-counting}, while
  $\mathrm{LB}_{r+1}(A)$ supplies a layout of width at most
  $(A+\eta)\beta_1+\eta|V|+C$. The comparison of demand and supply fails for
  small $\eta$ and large $n$ unless $(r-1)s>(1+1/A-\varepsilon)n$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Layout bounds]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-frontier-layouts}
  \lean{Complexity.Frontier.LayoutBound.of_orderingBound,
    Complexity.Frontier.layoutBound_gaussian,
    Complexity.Frontier.layoutBound_of_orderingBound_frontier,
    Complexity.Frontier.gaussianCoefficient_eq_frontierCoefficient,
    Complexity.Frontier.layoutBound_one_third,
    Complexity.Frontier.Multigraph.Compression,
    Complexity.Frontier.Multigraph.exists_layout_cycleRank,
    Complexity.Frontier.layoutBound_one}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-frontier-layout-bound,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-gaussian-coefficient,
    thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-gaussian-pathwidth}
  $\mathrm{LB}_3(2p)$ holds with $p=\tfrac{3}{2\pi}\arccos\tfrac{1+2\sqrt2}{4}$,
  hence $\mathrm{LB}_3(1/3)$; $p$ is the coefficient of
  Theorem~\ref{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-gaussian-pathwidth}. The ordering
  hypothesis of the cutwidth development at every positive slack also gives
  $\mathrm{LB}_3$ with the same coefficient.
  In every maximum degree $d$, every connected multigraph has a layout of width
  at most $\beta_1+d\lfloor\log_2|V|\rfloor$, so $\mathrm{LB}_d(1)$ holds.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Order the vertices of the cubic core by median Gaussian edge scores, as in
  Theorem~\ref{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-gaussian-pathwidth}, and expand the core.
  For the transfer, number the vertices in the given order; $(|E|-|V|)^+\le\beta_1$ and
  $3\log_2|V|\le\eta|V|+K$. For general degree, merge the blocks of a
  degree-$d$ compression along a spanning tree: a prefix of a merged block is a
  prefix of its larger part or that part followed by a prefix of a part at most
  half as large, so tree layouts have width $d\lfloor\log_2|V|\rfloor$, and each
  of the $\beta_1$ edges outside the tree adds one to every cut.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Gaussian layouts in every degree]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-frontier-degree-layouts}
  \lean{Complexity.Frontier.Gaussian.vertexLayout_bound,
    Complexity.Frontier.Gaussian.multigraph_vertexLayout_bound,
    Complexity.Frontier.Multigraph.Compression.Terminal.multi_edge_bound,
    Complexity.Frontier.Gaussian.degreeCoefficient,
    Complexity.Frontier.Gaussian.degreeCoefficient_lt_three_quarters,
    Complexity.Frontier.layoutBound_degree, Complexity.Frontier.layoutBound_two_fifths}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-frontier-layout-bound, thm:lowerbounds-frontier-layouts}
  For every fixed $d\ge2$, loopless multigraphs of maximum degree $d$ have layouts
  of width at most $\gamma_d|E|+o(|V|)$, where
  $\gamma_d=\arccos(2\sqrt{d-1}/d)/\pi$. Consequently $\mathrm{LB}_d(A_d)$ holds for
  every $d\ge3$, with $A_d=\tfrac{3d}{2d-3}\gamma_d<3/4$, and $A_4=2/5$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Score each vertex by a Gaussian form of its normalized truncated distance-kernel
  row and sort; adjacent rows have correlation at least $2\sqrt{d-1}/d-o(1)$, so
  Sheppard's bound gives the crossing probability $\gamma_d$ per edge, and
  bounded-radius second moments control a grid of thresholds, windows, and tails
  at once. Terminal block compression keeps parallel edges, has degrees between
  three and $d$, and adjacent degree sums at least $d+3$, so its $m$ edges satisfy
  $(2d-3)m\le3d\beta_1$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Unconditional bounds for dense rectangle-free sets]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-frontier-unconditional}
  \lean{Complexity.Frontier.lowerBound_gaussian, Complexity.Frontier.lowerBound_four,
    Complexity.Frontier.lowerBound_all_fanIn, Complexity.Frontier.lowerBound_degree,
    Complexity.Frontier.lowerBound_fanInThree}
  \leanok
  \uses{thm:lowerbounds-frontier-main, thm:lowerbounds-frontier-layouts,
    thm:lowerbounds-frontier-degree-layouts}
  Under the hypotheses on $S_n$ of Theorem~\ref{thm:lowerbounds-frontier-main},
  every circuit of fan-in two deciding $S_n$, over any basis on $U$, has more
  than $\bigl(1+\pi/(3\arccos\tfrac{1+2\sqrt2}{4})-\varepsilon\bigr)n\approx
  (4.5625-\varepsilon)n$ gates of positive arity, in particular more than
  $(4-\varepsilon)n$. For every fixed fan-in $r\ge2$,
  $(r-1)s>(1+1/A_{r+1}-\varepsilon)n$ with $A_d<3/4$, so circuits of fan-in three
  need more than $(7/4-\varepsilon)n$ gates; the spanning-tree bound gives the
  simpler $(r-1)s>(2-\varepsilon)n$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Instantiate Theorem~\ref{thm:lowerbounds-frontier-main} with the layout
  bounds of Theorems~\ref{thm:lowerbounds-frontier-layouts}
  and~\ref{thm:lowerbounds-frontier-degree-layouts}; $1+1/(2/5)=7/2$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[The explicit family over any basis and fan-in]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-frontier-explicit}
  \lean{Complexity.Frontier.rectangleFree_setOf,
    Complexity.Frontier.sourceReductionHardFamily_frontierHypotheses,
    Complexity.Frontier.sourceReductionHardFamily_lt_innerSize_gaussian,
    Complexity.Frontier.sourceReductionHardFamily_lt_innerSize_all_fanIn,
    Complexity.Frontier.sourceReductionHardFamily_lt_innerSize_degree,
    Complexity.Frontier.sourceReductionHardFamily_lt_innerSize_fanInThree,
    Complexity.Frontier.sourceReductionHardFamily_lt_innerSize_nondeterministic,
    Complexity.Frontier.sourceReductionHardFamily_lt_innerGates_aggregate,
    Complexity.Frontier.flatSumsetBias_of_flatSumsetExtractor,
    Complexity.Frontier.sourceReductionFamily_agreement_le}
  \leanok
  \uses{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-explicit-four-n,
    thm:lowerbounds-frontier-unconditional, thm:lowerbounds-frontier-nondeterministic,
    thm:lowerbounds-frontier-ledger, thm:lowerbounds-frontier-average-case}
  The accepted sets of the polynomial-time family
  $\mathrm{sourceReductionHardFamily}$ of
  Theorem~\ref{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-explicit-four-n} satisfy the hypotheses
  of Theorem~\ref{thm:lowerbounds-frontier-main}. Hence, for every
  $\varepsilon>0$ and all large $n$: every circuit of fan-in two deciding it,
  over any basis on $\{0,1\}$ and with any accepting set, has more than
  $\bigl(1+\pi/(3\arccos\tfrac{1+2\sqrt2}{4})-\varepsilon\bigr)n$ gates of
  positive arity; for every fan-in $r\ge2$, $(r-1)s>(1+1/A_{r+1}-\varepsilon)n$,
  in particular $(7/4-\varepsilon)n$ gates at fan-in three; the
  Gaussian bound holds for verifier circuits over any basis, uniformly in the
  number of witness inputs; and it holds for circuits with special gates of
  unbounded fan-in aggregating in a finite commutative monoid with $o(n)$ bits
  of total state, counting only the ordinary gates. Moreover the unpadded
  extractor, with error $35/72$, agrees with every circuit of fan-in two over any
  basis with at most $\bigl(1+\pi/(3\arccos\tfrac{1+2\sqrt2}{4})-\varepsilon\bigr)n$
  gates of positive arity on at most a $71/72+2^{-\gamma n}$ fraction of inputs,
  for some $\gamma>0$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Boolean rectangle-freeness of the cutwidth development is rectangle-freeness
  of the accepted set. Balanced padding of the eventually flat extractor is
  $2K$-rectangle-free with $\log K=o(n)$, and it accepts exactly half the cube,
  so the density deficit is $\log2$. Apply
  Theorems~\ref{thm:lowerbounds-frontier-unconditional},
  \ref{thm:lowerbounds-frontier-nondeterministic},
  and~\ref{thm:lowerbounds-frontier-ledger}. An extractor with error $\nu$ has
  signed sumset bias $2\nu$, so
  Theorem~\ref{thm:lowerbounds-frontier-average-case} applies with
  $b_n=35/72$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Sumset dispersers over an additive alphabet]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-frontier-sumset-disperser}
  \lean{Complexity.Frontier.SumsetDisperser,
    Complexity.Frontier.SumsetDisperser.rectangleFree,
    Complexity.Frontier.lowerBound_sumsetDisperser}
  \leanok
  \uses{thm:lowerbounds-frontier-unconditional}
  A function on $U^n$, for a finite alphabet with an addition and zero, that takes
  both values on $A+B$ whenever $|A|,|B|\ge K$ has $K$-rectangle-free fibres. If
  such dispersers have $\log K_n=o(n)$ and accept a fixed fraction $\delta>0$ of
  $U^n$, every circuit of fan-in two deciding them, over any basis, has more
  than $\bigl(1+\pi/(3\arccos\tfrac{1+2\sqrt2}{4})-\varepsilon\bigr)n$ gates.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  A rectangle with large sides, padded by zero on the opposite coordinates, gives
  sets $A$ and $B$ whose sumset is the rectangle. A fixed density fraction makes
  the density deficit constant.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Verifier circuits]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-frontier-nondeterministic}
  \lean{Complexity.Frontier.Network.hide, Complexity.Frontier.Network.accepted_hide,
    Complexity.Frontier.NondeterministicDecides,
    Complexity.Frontier.lowerBound_nondeterministic,
    Complexity.Frontier.lowerBound_nondeterministic_gaussian}
  \leanok
  \uses{thm:lowerbounds-frontier-main, thm:lowerbounds-frontier-layouts}
  Theorem~\ref{thm:lowerbounds-frontier-main} holds for circuits on $n+k$
  inputs, with arbitrary $k$, that accept $x$ exactly when some witness $y$ makes
  the output accept, uniformly in $k$; for fan-in two the Gaussian coefficient
  holds. Witness inputs are not counted in $n$; every gate of positive arity is
  counted.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Hiding an input moves its existential quantifier into the local check of the
  vertex that reads it, which preserves the network and its acceptance. The exact
  counting argument never uses uniqueness of satisfying assignments.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Linear maps and arithmetic circuits]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-frontier-linear}
  \lean{Complexity.Frontier.TotallyRegular, Complexity.Frontier.totallyRegular_cauchy,
    Complexity.Frontier.exists_affine_circuit, Complexity.Frontier.lowerBound_linear,
    Complexity.Frontier.linear_finite_gaussian,
    Complexity.Frontier.linear_polynomial_gaussian,
    Complexity.Frontier.arith_cauchy_gaussian, Complexity.Frontier.cauchyZMod_gaussian,
    Complexity.Frontier.linear_finite_degree, Complexity.Frontier.linear_polynomial_degree,
    Complexity.Frontier.linear_finite_fanInThree,
    Complexity.Frontier.linear_polynomial_fanInThree}
  \leanok
  \uses{thm:lowerbounds-frontier-main, thm:lowerbounds-frontier-layouts,
    thm:lowerbounds-frontier-degree-layouts}
  Under $\mathrm{LB}_{r+1}(A)$, every circuit of fan-in at most $r$ with linear
  capacity computing $x\mapsto Mx$ for a totally regular $N\times N$ matrix has
  $(r-1)s>(1+1/A-\varepsilon)N$, counting gates of positive arity. With the
  Gaussian coefficient: over finite fields for every basis of fan-in two; over
  infinite fields for every basis of polynomial operations, via a dual-number
  linearization that produces an affine circuit of the same size, inner size, and
  fan-in; for arithmetic circuits computing the Cauchy map over every field of
  characteristic zero; and for the Cauchy map over $\mathbb{Z}/q$ for every prime
  $q\ge2N$, over any basis. Over finite fields, and over infinite fields with
  polynomial operations, every fixed fan-in $r$ gives
  $(r-1)s>(1+1/A_{r+1}-\varepsilon)N$, and fan-in three gives $(7/4-\varepsilon)N$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Every layout of the compiled network passes a vertex set that reads inputs $X$
  and holds outputs $Y$ with $|X|+|Y|=N$. Splicing shows that the blocks
  $M[Y,X^c]$ and $M[Y^c,X]$ see the input only through the values on the cut, and
  total regularity makes both nonsingular, so those values determine the input;
  linear capacity then forces at least $N$ cut edges. Over infinite fields the
  polynomial gates are replaced by their first-order Taylor expansions at a base
  point, computed over the dual numbers.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Mixed fibers and MDS maps]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-frontier-mds}
  \lean{Complexity.Frontier.OutputSplicing.card_le,
    Complexity.Frontier.Compiler.pow_le_cutSignals_mul_fibers,
    Complexity.Frontier.IsMDS, Complexity.Frontier.TotallyRegular.isMDS,
    Complexity.Frontier.IsMDS.permute, Complexity.Frontier.lowerBound_mds,
    Complexity.Frontier.mds_gaussian, Complexity.Frontier.mds_degree}
  \leanok
  \uses{thm:lowerbounds-frontier-linear, thm:lowerbounds-frontier-degree-layouts}
  For any map and any split of its inputs and outputs, the number of inputs is at
  most the number of used boundary messages times the largest fibres of the two
  complementary mixed observations; in a compiled circuit the messages are the
  distinct signals crossing a cut. A map $F:U^N\to U^N$ is MDS when any $N$
  coordinates of its graph code $(x,F(x))$ determine $x$. Under
  $\mathrm{LB}_{r+1}(A)$, every circuit of fan-in at most $r$ over any basis
  computing an MDS map over any finite alphabet satisfies
  $(r-1)s>(1+1/A-\varepsilon)N$, uniformly in the alphabet; hence the Gaussian
  coefficients at fan-in two and in every fixed degree. Totally regular linear
  maps are MDS, and the MDS property survives coordinatewise permutations of the
  alphabet.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Every layout passes a set of vertices reading $|X|$ inputs and holding $|Y|$
  outputs with $|X|+|Y|=N$; the MDS property makes both mixed fibres singletons,
  so at least $N$ symbols cross that cut.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Ledgers for gates of unbounded fan-in]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-frontier-ledger}
  \lean{Complexity.Frontier.Ledger, Complexity.Frontier.Ledger.total_run_eq_iff,
    Complexity.Frontier.Aggregates,
    Complexity.Frontier.LayoutBound.exists_layout_componentwise,
    Complexity.Frontier.lowerBound_ledger, Complexity.Frontier.lowerBound_aggregate,
    Complexity.Frontier.lowerBound_aggregate_gaussian}
  \leanok
  \uses{thm:lowerbounds-frontier-main, thm:lowerbounds-frontier-layouts}
  Under the hypotheses of Theorem~\ref{thm:lowerbounds-frontier-main}, let the
  special gates of a circuit, of any fan-in, have a ledger in a finite commutative
  monoid $M$ with $\log|M|\le\beta(n)=o(n)$: each wire contributes an element of
  $M$ according to its value, and each special gate is read off the product. Then
  the ordinary gates of positive arity, of fan-in at most $r$, satisfy
  $(r-1)s>(1+1/A-\varepsilon)n$. Gates aggregating in one monoid $T$, such as
  AND, OR, parity, and counting modulo a fixed number, have a ledger in
  $T^{\text{special}}$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Guess the special outputs, replace the special gates by placeholders, and lay
  out the erased program one connected component after another, carrying the
  product of contributions in the messages; a guess is consistent exactly when the
  ledger total confirms it. The counting lemma finds a component that reads almost
  every input and whose layout is wide, up to $2\log|M|$, and its cycle rank gives
  the comparison of demand and supply.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Average-case hardness]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-frontier-average-case}
  \lean{Complexity.Frontier.Sweep.abs_sumOn_le_of_budget,
    Complexity.Frontier.RectangleBias, Complexity.Frontier.agreement,
    Complexity.Frontier.averageCase, Complexity.Frontier.averageCase_abs,
    Complexity.Frontier.averageCase_gaussian, Complexity.Frontier.FlatSumsetBias,
    Complexity.Frontier.FlatSumsetBias.rectangleBias,
    Complexity.Frontier.averageCase_sumset,
    Complexity.Frontier.averageCase_sumset_polynomial,
    Complexity.Frontier.FlatSumsetBias.affine_pullback}
  \leanok
  \uses{lem:lowerbounds-frontier-counting, thm:lowerbounds-frontier-layouts}
  For a coherent sweep, a weight bounded by $a$ whose sum over every rectangle
  with sides at least $K_L$ and $K_R$ is paid by a nonnegative budget satisfies
  $|\sum_S w|\le\sum_S\mathrm{cost}+a(K_L-1)(K_R-1)T$, where $T$ counts the
  transitions. Consequently, if $f_n$ has bias at most $2b_n$ on every coordinate
  rectangle with both sides at least $K_n$, with $\log K_n=o(n)$, then under
  $\mathrm{LB}_3(A)$ every Boolean circuit of fan-in two over any basis with at
  most $(1+1/A-\varepsilon)n$ gates of positive arity agrees with $f_n$ on at most
  a $1/2+b_n+2^{-\gamma n}$ fraction of inputs, for any $\gamma<\min(1,A\varepsilon)$;
  the same bound holds for the absolute correlation. For the Gaussian coefficient
  and for flat-source sumset extractors with error $b_n$, some $\gamma>0$ works.
  Extractor error at most $Cn^{-a}$ gives agreement at most $1/2+(C+1)n^{-a}$.
  Sumset bias is preserved by every injective affine change of the input
  coordinates.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Peel thick rectangles of peers, which the budget pays for, and delete their
  message classes; coherence keeps the remaining sweep a sweep. The thin remainder
  pays the maximum atom per transition. Apply this to both output classes of the
  circuit along a Gaussian layout.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Signal capacity, linear syndromes, and transition codes]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-frontier-boundary}
  \lean{Complexity.Frontier.Compiler.signalCut_eq_hypergraph_cut,
    Complexity.Frontier.Compiler.signalCut_submodular,
    Complexity.Frontier.Compiler.ncard_accepted_le_signals,
    Complexity.Frontier.LinearBoundary.ncard_syndromes,
    Complexity.Frontier.LinearBoundary.ncard_syndromes_le_states,
    Complexity.Frontier.Sweep.transitionCount_le_codes,
    Complexity.Frontier.Sweep.abs_sumOn_le_codes,
    Complexity.Frontier.Sweep.transitionCount_le_linear_codes,
    Complexity.Frontier.LinearBoundary.joint_eq,
    Complexity.Frontier.LinearBoundary.finrank_joint}
  \leanok
  \uses{thm:lowerbounds-frontier-compiler, lem:lowerbounds-frontier-counting,
    thm:lowerbounds-frontier-average-case}
  The distinct signals crossing a cut of the compiled network are exactly the
  cut of its signal hypergraph, and their number is a symmetric submodular
  function of the cut; counting may charge distinct signals instead of edges.
  For a linear system $Lx+Ry=0$ over a finite field $F$, the feasible syndromes
  number exactly $|F|^{\operatorname{rank}L+\operatorname{rank}R-\operatorname{rank}[L\,R]}$.
  Any finite code that determines the transitions on $S$ bounds the transition
  count, in both the exact and the weighted counting lemmas. For subspaces
  $P\le U$ and $Q\le V$ of linear observations, encoding the exposed observations
  together with the shared information $U\cap V$ costs
  $\dim(U+Q)+\dim(V+P)-\dim(U+V)$, not the sum of the separate ranks.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Each signal's occurrences form a connected subgraph, so a signal crosses a cut
  exactly when its hyperedge does. The feasible syndromes form
  $\operatorname{im}L\cap\operatorname{im}R$, and the joint code is the
  modular-law identity $(U\cap V)+(P+Q)=(U+Q)\cap(V+P)$. A determining code surjects onto the
  transitions it determines.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Pruning and trees of regions]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-frontier-pruning-tree}
  \lean{Complexity.Frontier.Sweep.abs_sumOn_le_pruned,
    Complexity.Frontier.agreement_le_pruned_sweeps,
    Complexity.Frontier.RegionTree, Complexity.Frontier.TreeFrontier,
    Complexity.Frontier.TreeFrontier.abs_sumOn_le_of_budget,
    Complexity.Frontier.Network.abs_sumOn_accepted_le_tree}
  \leanok
  \uses{thm:lowerbounds-frontier-average-case}
  Restricting a coherent sweep with nested revealed sets to the inputs whose
  transitions all lie in chosen sets preserves splicing; the discarded inputs are
  paid by a union bound, and discarding probability mass $\delta$ costs at most
  $\delta/2$ in agreement. On a binary tree of regions, weighted peeling has
  residual factor $(K-1)^3$ per joint merge record. For a network with unique
  satisfying assignments that reads every input, a laminar decomposition gives
  such a tree, whose exponent at a node is the size of the union of its cut and
  its two children's cuts plus the inputs read locally.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Retained inputs that agree on a message splice into retained inputs, since
  every transition of a splice is a transition of one of its two parents. For
  trees, charge each input to a node of minimal rank whose interior is large but
  whose children's interiors are small.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Distribution-sensitive capacity]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-frontier-capacity}
  \lean{Complexity.Frontier.Sweep.cappedCapacity, Complexity.Frontier.Sweep.rootCapacity,
    Complexity.Frontier.Sweep.abs_sumOn_le_capped,
    Complexity.Frontier.Sweep.cappedCapacity_le_root,
    Complexity.Frontier.Sweep.momentCapacity,
    Complexity.Frontier.Sweep.cappedCapacity_le_moment,
    Complexity.Frontier.agreement_le_capped_sweeps,
    Complexity.Frontier.agreement_le_root_sweeps,
    Complexity.Frontier.agreement_le_moment_sweeps,
    Complexity.Frontier.Communication.rootPotential_le}
  \leanok
  \uses{thm:lowerbounds-frontier-pruning-tree}
  In the weighted counting lemma each transition may be charged the smaller of its
  frontier allowance and the absolute weight of the inputs using it. For a
  predictor $g$ with coherent nested sweeps of its two output classes and a target
  of rectangle bias $2b$, agreement is at most
  $1/2+b+\tfrac12\sum\min(\lambda,p)$ over the unconditional transition masses $p$,
  with $\lambda=(K-1)^2/|U|^n$, and at most
  $1/2+b+\tfrac12\lambda^{1-\theta}\sum p^\theta$ for every $0\le\theta\le1$.
  A potential satisfying local square-root inequalities along an encoding bounds
  the sum of square roots of codeword masses by its value at the root.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Keep exactly the transitions of mass at least $\lambda$ and apply the pruning
  bound; $\min(\lambda,p)\le\lambda^{1-\theta}p^\theta$. The potential inequalities
  telescope level by level, since each child has one parent.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Decomposable representations]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-frontier-decomposable}
  \lean{Complexity.Frontier.Decomposable, Complexity.Frontier.Decomposable.ncard_le}
  \leanok
  \uses{lem:lowerbounds-frontier-counting}
  Let a smooth decomposable DAG have unions on a common scope, products on disjoint
  scopes, and leaves with at most one assignment, with no determinism or unique
  certificate assumed. If its accepted set is $K$-rectangle-free with at least $K$
  elements, $K\ge2$, then it has at most $(K-1)^2e+(K-1)^3p$ elements, where $e$
  counts union edges and $p$ product nodes.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  A node and all contexts of its occurrences in accepting certificates form a
  rectangle. Descend a certificate to the cardinality threshold and charge the
  input to the union edge or product node reached there.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Additive generators]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-frontier-additive}
  \lean{Complexity.Frontier.SumsetFree, Complexity.Frontier.SumsetDisperser.sumsetFree,
    Complexity.Frontier.SumsetFree.ncard_lt_of_stable, Complexity.Frontier.AdditiveSweep,
    Complexity.Frontier.AdditiveSweep.ncard_le,
    Complexity.Frontier.AdditiveSweep.transitionCount_le_codes}
  \leanok
  \uses{lem:lowerbounds-frontier-counting, thm:lowerbounds-frontier-sumset-disperser}
  A subset $S$ of an additive monoid is $K$-sumset-free when it contains no sumset
  $A+B$ with $|A|,|B|\ge K$; the fibres of a sumset disperser are sumset-free.
  An additive sweep describes each input as the sum of a past and a future part,
  built by adding emitted labels, so that past and future parts of inputs with the
  same message can be recombined within $S$; labels may overlap and paths need not
  be unique. If $S$ is $K$-sumset-free with $K\ge2$ and $|S|\ge K$, then
  $|S|\le(K-1)^2$ times the number of transitions, and any code determining the
  transitions may replace them.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  As in Lemma~\ref{lem:lowerbounds-frontier-counting}: the past and future parts of
  the peers at a step form a sumset inside $S$, so one side has fewer than $K$
  elements; charge each input to the first step at which its past side is large.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Independent median updates]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-frontier-local}
  \lean{Complexity.Frontier.Multigraph.cutSize_eq_add_gain,
    Complexity.Frontier.Multigraph.cutSize_lt_of_local,
    Complexity.Frontier.LocalLayout.improve,
    Complexity.Frontier.LocalLayout.cutSize_improve_cubic_le}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-frontier-layout-bound}
  Changing the colours of an independent set of vertices changes the size of a
  cut by the sum of the local gains, and strictly decreases it when one selected
  vertex improves. In a cubic multigraph, replacing the keys of an independent set
  by the median of their neighbours' keys does not increase the number of edges
  crossing any threshold.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  No edge joins two selected vertices, so the edges at distinct selected vertices
  are disjoint. At every threshold the median agrees with a majority of the three
  neighbours.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Exponentially small correlation}
\label{sec:lowerbounds-frontier-correlation}

The rectangles of the frontier method also give exponentially small
correlation, not only bounded agreement, against quadratic forms over
$\mathbb{F}_2$ (namespace \texttt{Complexity.Correlation}). The circuit model,
the gate count, and the agreement measure are those of
Theorem~\ref{thm:lowerbounds-frontier-average-case}, so the bounds are directly
comparable. With $A=2p\approx0.2807$ the Gaussian coefficient of
Theorem~\ref{thm:lowerbounds-frontier-layouts}, put $c^*=1+1/(2A)\approx2.78125$
and $L=1+1/A\approx4.5625$. The best previously published average-case bounds
for circuits over the full binary basis are $2.5n$ (Chen and Kabanets, COCOON
2015, for an explicit affine extractor) and $2.6n$ (Golovnev, Kulikov, Smal, and
Tamaki, MFCS 2016, for non-explicit quadratic extractors).

\begin{definition}[Quadratic forms, cut ranks, and correlation]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-correlation-quadratic}
  \lean{Complexity.Correlation.correlation, Complexity.Correlation.quadForm,
    Complexity.Correlation.blockRank, Complexity.Correlation.cutRank,
    Complexity.Correlation.SubmatrixRobust, Complexity.Correlation.robustDeficiency,
    Complexity.Correlation.bilinForm, Complexity.Correlation.bipartiteMatrix}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-frontier-sweep}
  The correlation of Boolean functions $f$ and $g$ is
  $|\Pr[f=g]-\Pr[f\ne g]|=|2\,\mathrm{agreement}(f,g)-1|$. A square matrix $Q$
  over $\mathbb{F}_2$ defines the quadratic form $f_Q(x)=\sum_{i,j}Q_{ij}x_ix_j$.
  The rank of a block of a matrix is the dimension of the span of its rows, and
  the cut rank $\rho_Q(U)$ is the rank of the block of $Q+Q^{\mathsf T}$ with rows
  $U$ and columns $U^c$. A matrix is submatrix-robust with deficiency $t$ when
  every square block with $k$ rows has rank at least $k-t$. On inputs of length
  $n$, with $x$ the first $\lfloor n/2\rfloor$ coordinates and $y$ the next
  $\lfloor n/2\rfloor$, the bilinear form $x^{\mathsf T}Ry$ is the quadratic form
  of the matrix with $R$ in the block with rows $x$ and columns $y$.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Lindsey's lemma on rectangle classes]
  \label{lem:lowerbounds-correlation-lindsey}
  \lean{Complexity.Correlation.sq_sum_bilinear_mul_two_pow_rank_le,
    Complexity.Correlation.sq_sum_boolSign_quadForm_mul_le,
    Complexity.Correlation.correlation_sq_mul_two_pow_cutRank_le}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-correlation-quadratic}
  For every rectangle $R$ for a cut $X$ of the $n$ coordinates,
  $\bigl(\sum_{x\in R}(-1)^{f_Q(x)}\bigr)^2\,2^{\rho_Q(X)}\le|R|\,2^n$.
  Consequently, if a predictor $g$ is constant on the classes of a map into a
  finite set $M$ and every class is a rectangle for $X$, then
  $\mathrm{corr}(f_Q,g)^2\,2^{\rho_Q(X)}\le|M|$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  On a rectangle, $f_Q(p,q)=f_1(p)+f_2(q)+p^{\mathsf T}Bq$ with $B$ the block of
  $Q+Q^{\mathsf T}$. Cauchy--Schwarz over $p$, character orthogonality
  $\sum_p(-1)^{p\cdot v}=2^{|X|}[v=0]$, and the fibre count
  $|\{q':Bq'=Bq\}|=2^{|X^c|-\operatorname{rank}B}$ give the first bound;
  Cauchy--Schwarz over the classes gives the second.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Finite correlation bounds for circuits]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-correlation-finite}
  \lean{Complexity.Correlation.correlation_sq_le_of_unread,
    Complexity.Correlation.correlation_sq_le_of_layout,
    Complexity.Correlation.ncard_le_cutRank_add_of_bisection,
    Complexity.Correlation.exists_ncard_readIn_eq}
  \leanok
  \uses{lem:lowerbounds-correlation-lindsey, lem:lowerbounds-frontier-network-counting,
    thm:lowerbounds-frontier-compiler}
  Let every set of $\lfloor n/2\rfloor$ coordinates have cut rank at least
  $\lfloor n/2\rfloor-e$, and let $C$ be a single-output circuit over any basis on
  $\{0,1\}$. If $C$ leaves at least $k\le\lfloor n/2\rfloor$ inputs unread, then
  $\mathrm{corr}(f_Q,C)^2\le2^{1+e-k}$. If $C$ reads at least $\lfloor n/2\rfloor$
  inputs and its compiled constraint network has a layout all of whose frontiers
  have at most $w$ edges, then $\mathrm{corr}(f_Q,C)^2\le2^{1+w+e-\lfloor n/2\rfloor}$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  The cut ranks of sets of fewer coordinates are at least their size minus $e$,
  since deleting a row lowers a rank by at most one. A circuit that ignores a set
  $D$ of inputs has two output classes, each a rectangle for $D$. Along a layout,
  each vertex reads at most one input, so some initial segment reads exactly
  $\lfloor n/2\rfloor$ inputs; by the separator lemma, the inputs with a given
  output and given values on that frontier form a rectangle for the inputs read,
  and there are at most $2\cdot2^w$ of them. Apply
  Lemma~\ref{lem:lowerbounds-correlation-lindsey}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Exponentially small correlation for quadratic forms of large cut rank]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-correlation-asymptotic}
  \lean{Complexity.Correlation.eventually_correlation_le,
    Complexity.Correlation.eventually_correlation_le_of_isLittleO,
    Complexity.Correlation.eventually_correlation_le_gaussian}
  \leanok
  \uses{thm:lowerbounds-correlation-finite, def:lowerbounds-frontier-layout-bound,
    thm:lowerbounds-frontier-layouts}
  Assume $A>0$ and $\mathrm{LB}_3(A)$, and let $Q_n$ be $n\times n$ matrices over
  $\mathbb{F}_2$ such that for all large $n$ every set of $\lfloor n/2\rfloor$
  coordinates has cut rank at least $\lfloor n/2\rfloor-\tau n$. Then for every
  $c\ge1/2$ and every $\gamma<(c^*-c)/(2L)-\tau/2$, where $c^*=1+1/(2A)$ and
  $L=1+1/A$, for all large $n$ every circuit of fan-in two over any basis with at
  most $cn$ gates of positive arity has correlation at most $2^{-\gamma n}$ with
  $f_{Q_n}$. If the deficiency is $o(n)$ instead of $\tau n$, every
  $\gamma<(c^*-c)/(2L)$ works; for the Gaussian coefficient,
  $c^*=1+\pi/(6\arccos((1+2\sqrt2)/4))$ and $L=1+\pi/(3\arccos((1+2\sqrt2)/4))$.
  (For $c<1/2$ apply the bound with $c=1/2$.)
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Put $\theta=(1/2-A(c-1))/(1+A)$, so that $A(c-1+\theta)-1/2=-\theta$ and
  $(c^*-c)/(2L)=\theta/2$. With at least $\theta n$ unread inputs the first finite
  bound gives exponent $-(\theta-\tau)n+O(1)$. With fewer, the compiled network
  has cycle rank at most $(c-1+\theta)n+1$ and at most $4cn+3$ vertices, so
  $\mathrm{LB}_3(A)$ supplies a layout of width at most
  $(A+\eta)(c-1+\theta)n+5c\eta n+O_\eta(1)$, and the second finite bound gives the
  same exponent up to $O(\eta)n$. Choose $\eta$ small.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Submatrix-robust matrices exist]
  \label{lem:lowerbounds-correlation-robust}
  \lean{Complexity.Correlation.card_spannedBy_mul_le,
    Complexity.Correlation.exists_submatrixRobust_of_lt,
    Complexity.Correlation.exists_submatrixRobust}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-correlation-quadratic}
  If $3m<(t+1)^2$, some $m\times m$ matrix over $\mathbb{F}_2$ is
  submatrix-robust with deficiency $t$. In particular $t=2\lfloor\sqrt m\rfloor+1$
  works for every $m$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  A $k\times k$ block of rank $r$ has $r$ columns spanning the others. Given
  their positions, a matrix is determined by the coefficients expressing the
  other $k-r$ columns and by its entries outside the remaining $k\times(k-r)$
  block, so at most $2^{r(k-r)}2^{m^2-k(k-r)}=2^{m^2-(k-r)^2}$ matrices arise.
  Summing over the at most $2^k$ positions, at most $2^k\,2^{m^2-(t+1)^2}$
  matrices have a block of rank below $k-t$ on a given pair of $k$-sets of rows
  and columns. A union bound over fewer than $4^m$ pairs leaves fewer than
  $2^{m^2}$ bad matrices when $3m<(t+1)^2$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Exponentially small correlation below $2.78n$ gates]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-correlation-explicit}
  \lean{Complexity.Correlation.robustMatrix, Complexity.Correlation.hardForm,
    Complexity.Correlation.half_le_cutRank_hardMatrix,
    Complexity.Correlation.eventually_correlation_hardForm_le,
    Complexity.Correlation.exists_eventually_correlation_hardForm_le}
  \leanok
  \uses{thm:lowerbounds-correlation-asymptotic, lem:lowerbounds-correlation-robust}
  Fix, for every $m$, a submatrix-robust $m\times m$ matrix $R_m$ with deficiency
  $2\lfloor\sqrt m\rfloor+1$, and let $h_n=x^{\mathsf T}R_{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}y$.
  Every set of $\lfloor n/2\rfloor$ coordinates has cut rank at least
  $\lfloor n/2\rfloor-O(\sqrt n)$ in $h_n$. Hence for every $c\ge1/2$ and every
  $\gamma<(c^*-c)/(2L)$, for all large $n$ every circuit of fan-in two over any
  basis with at most $cn$ gates of positive arity has correlation at most
  $2^{-\gamma n}$ with $h_n$. In particular some fixed $\gamma>0$ works for all
  circuits with at most $2.77n$ gates of positive arity, beyond the previous
  $2.5n$ and $2.6n$. In Lean, $R_m$ is chosen by \texttt{Classical.choose}.
  Taking instead the lexicographically first robust matrix makes the family
  computable in $\cc{E}^{\cc{NP}}$: whether some robust matrix extends a given
  prefix is one $\cc{NP}$ query of length $2^{O(n)}$ (guess the matrix and check
  its fewer than $4^{n/2}$ pairs of row and column sets), so the matrix is found
  bit by bit in time $2^{O(n)}$. This uniformity is a remark about the
  construction and is not formalized.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  The block of the polar matrix at a cut $U$ contains, on disjoint columns, the
  block of $R$ with rows $x\cap U$ and columns $y\setminus U$ and the transpose of
  the block with rows $x\setminus U$ and columns $y\cap U$; their ranks add. When
  $|U|=\lfloor n/2\rfloor$, their smaller sides sum to at least
  $\lfloor n/2\rfloor-1$, and robustness costs $2t$. Apply
  Theorem~\ref{thm:lowerbounds-correlation-asymptotic} with the Gaussian
  coefficient; $2.77<c^*$ because $2p\le9/32$.
\end{proof}

\section{Essential inputs and gate elimination}
\label{sec:lowerbounds-gate-elimination}

\begin{definition}[Essential input]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-essential}
  \lean{Complexity.IsEssentialInput, Complexity.essentialInputs}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:bitstring}
  Input $i$ is essential for $f : \{0,1\}^N \to \{0,1\}^M$ if flipping bit $i$
  changes $f(x)$ for some $x$.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Essential-input bound]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-essential}
  \lean{Complexity.Circuit.card_essentialInputs_le_totalFanIn,
    Complexity.Circuit.card_essentialInputs_le_mul_size}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-essential, def:circuit, def:circuits-bases}
  Over any basis, the number of essential inputs of the function
  $\{0,1\}^N \to \{0,1\}^M$ computed by a circuit is at most the circuit's
  total fan-in, the number of gate-input occurrences with output gates
  included. For circuits over the AND/OR basis of fan-in at most $k$, it is at
  most $k$ times the size $G + M$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Every essential input is read by some gate input occurrence.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Parity]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-parity}
  \lean{Complexity.Schnorr.xorBool}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:bitstring}
  The parity family $\oplus$ maps $x \in \{0,1\}^N$ to
  $x_0 \oplus \cdots \oplus x_{N-1}$, with value $0$ on the empty string.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Schnorr]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-schnorr}
  \lean{Complexity.schnorr_lower_bound_circuit, Complexity.sizeComplexity_xorBool_ge}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-parity, def:circuits-size-complexity}
  Every fan-in-two AND/OR circuit computing $N$-bit parity or its complement,
  $N \ge 1$, has size at least $2N - 1$, that is, at least $2(N - 1)$ internal
  gates. Hence $\mathrm{size}(\oplus_N) \ge 2N - 1$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Gate elimination: fixing a well-chosen input removes at least two gates and
  leaves parity or its complement on one fewer input.
\end{proof}

\section{Depth, formulas, and communication}
\label{sec:lowerbounds-depth}

\begin{theorem}[Valiant's depth reduction]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-valiant}
  \lean{Complexity.Valiant.depth_reduction}
  \leanok
  Let $G$ be a finite acyclic digraph with $S$ edges whose longest simple path
  has at most $2^k$ vertices, and let $r \le k$. There is a set $F$ of edges of
  $G$ with $k\,|F| \le r S$ whose removal leaves a digraph whose longest simple
  path has at most $2^k / 2^r$ vertices.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Label vertices by longest-path depth, partition the edges by the first bit
  in which the labels of their endpoints differ, and remove the $r$ smallest
  classes.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Depth reduction for circuits]
  \label{cor:lowerbounds-valiant-circuit}
  \uses{def:circuit}
  Let $C$ be a circuit whose dependency graph has longest paths of at most
  $2^k$ vertices, and let $r \le k$. There is a set $F$ of dependency edges
  with $k\,|F| \le r \cdot \mathrm{totalFanIn}(C)$ whose removal leaves a
  graph whose longest paths have at most $2^{k-r}$ vertices.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
  \uses{thm:lowerbounds-valiant, lem:circuits-dependency-graph}
  The dependency graph is acyclic and has at most $\mathrm{totalFanIn}(C)$
  edges.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Spira]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-spira}
  \lean{Complexity.BoolFormula.exists_spira_balanced}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:circuits-formula}
  Every Boolean formula $\varphi$ of size $s$ has an equivalent formula of
  depth at most $15 + 12 \lceil \log_2 s \rceil$ and size at most
  $80 s^8 - 4$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Find a subformula of between one third and two thirds of the size, and
  recombine the two cases for its value; recurse on both parts.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Monotone formula]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-monotone-formula}
  \lean{Complexity.MonotoneFormula, Complexity.MonotoneFormula.eval,
    Complexity.MonotoneFormula.depth, Complexity.MonotoneFormula.leaves,
    Complexity.IsMonotoneBoolFun}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:bitstring}
  A monotone formula over $N$ variables is a tree built from variables
  $x_0, \dots, x_{N-1}$ and binary conjunction and disjunction, without
  constants or negation. Its leaves are its variable occurrences, and its depth
  is the length of a longest root-to-leaf path, variables having depth zero. A
  function $f$ is monotone if $x \le y$ pointwise and $f(x) = 1$ imply
  $f(y) = 1$.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Monotone formulas]
  \label{lem:lowerbounds-monotone-formula}
  \lean{Complexity.MonotoneFormula.eval_monotone,
    Complexity.MonotoneFormula.card_essentialInputs_le_leaves_of_computes}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-monotone-formula, def:lowerbounds-essential}
  Every monotone formula computes a monotone function, and a monotone formula
  computing $f$ has at least as many leaves as $f$ has essential inputs.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Induction on the formula; an essential input must occur as a leaf.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Karchmer--Wigderson protocol]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-kw}
  \lean{Complexity.KarchmerWigderson.Protocol,
    Complexity.KarchmerWigderson.RootProtocol,
    Complexity.KarchmerWigderson.Protocol.depth}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:bitstring}
  A protocol is a tree indexed by a rectangle $X \times Y$ of sets of inputs in
  $\{0,1\}^N$, $X$ on the one side and $Y$ on the zero side. At an internal
  node, Alice splits $X$ or Bob splits $Y$ by an arbitrary predicate, and the
  two children are indexed by the two parts. A leaf names a coordinate $i$ with
  $x_i = 1$ for all $x \in X$ and $y_i = 0$ for all $y \in Y$. A protocol for
  the monotone Karchmer--Wigderson game of $f : \{0,1\}^N \to \{0,1\}$ is one
  indexed by the root rectangle $f^{-1}(1) \times f^{-1}(0)$, so every node's
  rectangle lies in it. The depth is the height of the tree.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Monotone Karchmer--Wigderson]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-kw}
  \lean{Complexity.KarchmerWigderson.Protocol.exists_protocol_of_formula,
    Complexity.KarchmerWigderson.Protocol.exists_formula_of_protocol,
    Complexity.KarchmerWigderson.Protocol.exists_protocol_depth_iff_formula_depth}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-kw, def:lowerbounds-monotone-formula}
  Every monotone formula computing $f : \{0,1\}^N \to \{0,1\}$ yields a
  protocol for the monotone Karchmer--Wigderson game of $f$ of the same depth,
  and every such protocol yields a monotone formula of the same depth computing
  $f$. Hence, for every $d \in \mathbb{N}$, the game has a protocol of depth at
  most $d$ if and only if some monotone formula of depth at most $d$ computes
  $f$. There is no monotonicity hypothesis on $f$; by
  Lemma~\ref{lem:lowerbounds-monotone-formula}, both sides fail when $f$ is not
  monotone.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  An OR node is an Alice split and an AND node is a Bob split; both
  translations preserve depth exactly.
\end{proof}

\section{Restrictions, normal forms, and decision trees}
\label{sec:lowerbounds-restrictions}

\begin{definition}[Restriction]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-restriction}
  \lean{Complexity.Restriction, Complexity.Restriction.On,
    Complexity.Restriction.applyTo, Complexity.Restriction.comp,
    Complexity.BoolFormula.restrict}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:circuits-formula}
  A restriction assigns each variable $x_i$, $i \in \mathbb{N}$, either a
  fixed bit or ``free''; the finite-arity version does this for exactly $N$
  variables. The composition $\rho_1 \circ \rho_2$ fixes a variable to
  $\rho_1$'s value when $\rho_1$ fixes it and to $\rho_2$'s value otherwise.
  A restriction acts on a total assignment by overwriting the fixed variables,
  and on a Boolean formula by replacing each fixed variable with the
  corresponding constant.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Restriction and evaluation]
  \label{lem:lowerbounds-restrict}
  \lean{Complexity.BoolFormula.eval_restrict, Complexity.BoolFormula.restrict_comp,
    Complexity.BoolFormula.restrict_size, Complexity.BoolFormula.restrict_depth}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-restriction}
  Evaluating $\varphi|_\rho$ at $\alpha$ equals evaluating $\varphi$ at
  $\rho$ applied to $\alpha$. Restricting by $\rho_1$ and then by $\rho_2$
  equals restricting by $\rho_1 \circ \rho_2$, and restriction preserves
  formula size and depth exactly.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Induction on the formula.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[CNF and DNF]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-normal-forms}
  \lean{Complexity.CNF, Complexity.DNF, Complexity.CNF.width, Complexity.DNF.width,
    Complexity.CNF.complexity, Complexity.DNF.complexity}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:bitstring}
  A CNF (resp.\ DNF) over $N$ variables is a list of clauses (resp.\ terms),
  each a list of literals. Its width is the largest number of literals in a
  clause (term), and its complexity is the number of clauses (terms). These
  finite-arity normal forms are distinct from the CNF formulas used for
  satisfiability and resolution.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Restricting normal forms]
  \label{lem:lowerbounds-normal-form-restrict}
  \lean{Complexity.CNF.restrict, Complexity.CNF.eval_restrict,
    Complexity.CNF.width_restrict_le, Complexity.DNF.restrict,
    Complexity.DNF.eval_restrict, Complexity.DNF.width_restrict_le}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-normal-forms, def:lowerbounds-restriction}
  For a finite-arity restriction $\rho$ and a CNF or DNF $\varphi$ over $N$
  variables, the simplified restriction $\varphi|_\rho$ satisfies
  $\varphi|_\rho(x) = \varphi(\rho \text{ applied to } x)$ for every
  $x \in \{0,1\}^N$, and its width is at most the width of $\varphi$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  In a CNF delete falsified literals and satisfied clauses; in a DNF delete
  satisfied literals and falsified terms.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Decision tree]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-decision-tree}
  \lean{Complexity.DecisionTree, Complexity.DecisionTree.On,
    Complexity.DecisionTree.On.eval, Complexity.DecisionTree.On.depth}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:bitstring}
  A decision tree queries a variable at each internal node and outputs a bit
  at each leaf. The finite-arity version queries only variables below $N$.
  Its depth is the length of a longest root-to-leaf path.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Decision trees to normal forms]
  \label{lem:lowerbounds-dt-normal-form}
  \lean{Complexity.DecisionTree.On.eval_toDNF, Complexity.DecisionTree.On.eval_toCNF,
    Complexity.DecisionTree.On.width_toDNF_le_depth,
    Complexity.DecisionTree.On.width_toCNF_le_depth}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-decision-tree, def:lowerbounds-normal-forms}
  A decision tree of depth $s$ is computed by a DNF of width at most $s$ and
  by a CNF of width at most $s$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Take one term per accepting path, and dually one clause per rejecting path.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Parity at depth two]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-parity-depth-two}
  \lean{Complexity.DNF.two_pow_le_complexity_of_xorBool,
    Complexity.CNF.two_pow_le_complexity_of_xorBool}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-normal-forms, def:lowerbounds-parity}
  For $N \ge 1$, every DNF and every CNF computing $N$-bit parity has at
  least $2^{N-1}$ terms, respectively clauses.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  A satisfiable term that omits a variable accepts two inputs of different
  parity, so every satisfiable term accepts exactly one input, while parity
  accepts $2^{N-1}$ inputs. CNFs follow by negation.
\end{proof}

\section{The switching lemma and $\cc{AC}^0$}
\label{sec:lowerbounds-ac0}

\begin{definition}[Sparse random restriction]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-random-restriction}
  \lean{Complexity.RandomRestriction.Seed, Complexity.RandomRestriction.decode,
    Complexity.RandomRestriction.eventCount, Complexity.RandomRestriction.card_seed,
    Complexity.RandomRestriction.card_decodeSymbol_none,
    Complexity.RandomRestriction.card_decodeSymbol_some}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-restriction}
  For $q \in \mathbb{N}$, a seed assigns to each of $N$ coordinates one of
  $2q + 1$ symbols: one free symbol and $q$ labelled copies of each fixed bit,
  so there are exactly $(2q + 1)^N$ seeds. A seed decodes to a finite-arity
  restriction, so a uniform seed leaves each coordinate free with probability
  $1/(2q+1)$ and fixes it to each bit with probability $q/(2q+1)$,
  independently. Events are measured by exact counts of seeds rather than by
  probabilities.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Switching lemma, counting form]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-switching}
  \lean{Complexity.DNF.switchingBad,
    Complexity.DNF.switchingBad_width_encoding_bound,
    Complexity.DNF.switchingBad_consistentPart_width_encoding_bound,
    Complexity.CNF.switchingBad_width_encoding_bound,
    Complexity.CNF.switchingBad_consistentPart_width_encoding_bound,
    Complexity.DNF.eval_consistentPart, Complexity.CNF.eval_consistentPart}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-random-restriction, def:lowerbounds-normal-forms,
    def:lowerbounds-decision-tree}
  Let $\varphi$ be a DNF of width $w$ over $N$ variables, no term of which
  contains a complementary pair of literals, and let $q, s \in \mathbb{N}$.
  Call a restriction bad if the complete-block switching decision tree of the
  restricted formula has depth at least $s$. This tree, rather than the
  canonical tree that queries one variable at a time, is the one used here: at
  the first surviving term it queries all of that term's free variables before
  accepting or continuing.
  Then
  \[ \#\{\text{bad seeds}\} \cdot q^s \le (2q + 1)^N \, (4(w + 1))^s . \]
  Every DNF agrees with its sub-DNF of consistent terms, whose width is no
  larger, and the bound holds for that sub-DNF with $w$ the original width.
  The same bounds hold for CNFs, where the condition is that no clause
  contains a complementary pair, and the switching tree and bad event are
  defined through the De Morgan dual DNF. Reading the counts as
  probabilities, which is not a separate formal statement: a uniform seed is
  bad with probability at most $(4(w+1)/q)^s$ when $q \ge 1$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Encode each bad restriction, together with the labels of its first $s$
  queried coordinates, injectively as a seed plus $s$ advice symbols from an
  alphabet of size $4(w + 1)$ (Razborov's encoding). CNFs follow by De Morgan
  duality.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[$\cc{AC}^0$ formula]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-ac0-formula}
  \lean{Complexity.AC0Formula, Complexity.AC0Formula.eval, Complexity.AC0Formula.size,
    Complexity.AC0Formula.depth}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:bitstring}
  A negation-normal unbounded formula over $N$ variables is a tree built from
  constants, literals, and AND and OR gates of any fan-in; an empty AND is true
  and an empty OR is false. Its size counts all nodes (constants, literal
  occurrences, and gates), and its depth counts gates on a longest
  root-to-leaf path.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Normalization of $\cc{AC}^0$ circuits]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-ac0-normalization}
  \lean{Complexity.Circuit.outputAC0Formula, Complexity.Circuit.outputAC0Formula_spec}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-ac0-formula, def:circuits-bases}
  Let $C$ be an unbounded-fan-in AND/OR circuit, with negations only through
  the free per-input flags, with $N$ inputs and $G$ internal gates, and let
  output $j$ have depth $D$, counting the output gate. Some negation-normal
  unbounded formula computes output $j$, has depth at most $D$, and has size at
  most $(2(N + G) + 1)^{D+1}$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Push negations to the literals by De Morgan duality, remove duplicate signed
  inputs of each gate, and unfold the circuit into a tree.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Iterated switching]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-staged-switching}
  \lean{Complexity.AC0Formula.stagedDecisionTree,
    Complexity.AC0Formula.eval_stagedDecisionTree,
    Complexity.AC0Formula.stageEventCount_stagedBad_mul_pow_le,
    Complexity.AC0Formula.exists_shallow_stagedDecisionTree_of_counting}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-ac0-formula, def:lowerbounds-random-restriction,
    def:lowerbounds-decision-tree}
  Let $\varphi$ be a negation-normal unbounded formula over $N$ variables with
  depth at most $d$, and let $s \ge 2$ and $q \in \mathbb{N}$. For any
  sequence of $d$ restrictions, one per level, a staged compiler turns
  $\varphi$ into a decision tree that computes $\varphi$ under their composition,
  earlier restrictions taking precedence. For $d$ independent sparse seeds, the
  number of $d$-tuples of seeds for which this decision tree has depth at least
  $s$, multiplied by $q^s$, is at most
  $\mathrm{size}(\varphi)\,((2q+1)^N)^d\,(4(s + 1))^s$. Consequently, if $q > 0$
  and
  \[ ((2q+1)^N)^d\, s\, q^s + \mathrm{size}(\varphi)\,((2q+1)^N)^d\,(4(s+1))^s N
     < N\, ((2q+1)^{N-1})^d\, q^s , \]
  then some tuple of seeds leaves at least $s$ variables free and reduces
  $\varphi$ to a decision tree of depth less than $s$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  \uses{thm:lowerbounds-switching, lem:lowerbounds-dt-normal-form,
    lem:lowerbounds-normal-form-restrict}
  At a gate, the earlier stages have compiled the children to decision trees;
  their conjunction is a CNF and their disjunction a DNF of width at most the
  largest child depth. Unless some child already failed, that width is below
  $s$, so the switching lemma bounds the failures at the gate. A union bound
  over the nodes and the exact first moment of the number of surviving
  variables give the criterion.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Finite parity obstruction]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-parity-obstruction}
  \lean{Complexity.AC0Formula.parity_counting_obstruction}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-ac0-formula, def:lowerbounds-parity}
  Let $\varphi$ be a negation-normal unbounded formula over $N$ variables
  that computes parity, and let $d \ge \mathrm{depth}(\varphi)$, $s \ge 2$ and
  $q \ge 1$. Then
  \[ N\, ((2q+1)^{N-1})^d\, q^s \le ((2q+1)^N)^d\, s\, q^s
     + \mathrm{size}(\varphi)\,((2q+1)^N)^d\,(4(s+1))^s N . \]
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  \uses{thm:lowerbounds-staged-switching}
  Parity restricted to a set of free variables needs a decision tree querying
  all of them, so the conclusion of Theorem~\ref{thm:lowerbounds-staged-switching}
  is impossible and its numeric hypothesis must fail.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Parity is not in $\cc{AC}^0$]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-parity-not-ac0}
  \lean{Complexity.xorBool_not_mem_AC0}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:NC-AC, def:lowerbounds-parity}
  The parity family is not in $\cc{AC}^0$: no family of polynomial-size,
  constant-depth, unbounded-fan-in AND/OR circuits, with negations free on gate
  inputs, computes $N$-bit parity at every input length $N$. The analogous
  statement for the imported library's $\cc{AC}^0$ model is
  Theorem~\ref{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-parity-ac0}.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  \uses{thm:lowerbounds-ac0-normalization, thm:lowerbounds-parity-obstruction}
  Suppose a family of depth at most $c$ and size at most $a N^k + a$ computes
  parity. At every length $N \ge 1$, normalization gives a parity formula of
  depth at most $c$ and size at most $B N^K$, where $B = (3 + 4a)^{c+1}$ and
  $K = (k + 1)(c + 1)$. Apply Theorem~\ref{thm:lowerbounds-parity-obstruction}
  with $d = c + 1$ stages, $s = (d + 1)K + d + 1$, $q = m$, and $N = m^{d+1}$,
  where $m = 2B\,3^d(4(s+1))^s + 2s\,3^d + 1$. Dividing by
  $((2q+1)^{N-1})^d$ and using $(2m+1)^d \le 3^d m^d$, each of the two terms
  on the right is less than half of the left side, so the inequality fails.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Håstad's size bound]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-hastad-size}
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-parity, def:circuits-bases}
  For every $d \ge 2$ there is $\varepsilon_d > 0$ such that, for all
  sufficiently large $N$, every unbounded-fan-in AND/OR circuit of depth $d$
  computing $N$-bit parity has size at least $2^{\varepsilon_d N^{1/(d-1)}}$.
  Theorem~\ref{thm:lowerbounds-algebraic-hastad} proves an explicit
  non-asymptotic form of this bound for the imported library's $\cc{AC}^0$
  model; the statement here, for the circuits of this library, is not yet
  formalized.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \uses{thm:lowerbounds-ac0-normalization, thm:lowerbounds-staged-switching}
  The same argument with the restriction parameters optimized, keeping the
  bottom fan-in bound separate from the size.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Parity in $\cc{TC}^0$]
  \label{lem:lowerbounds-parity-tc0}
  \lean{Complexity.Circuit.thresholdParity, Complexity.Circuit.eval_thresholdParity,
    Complexity.Circuit.size_thresholdParity, Complexity.Circuit.depth_thresholdParity_le,
    Complexity.CircuitFamily.thresholdParity, Complexity.xorBool_mem_TC0}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:circuits-TC, def:lowerbounds-parity}
  For $N \ge 1$, a threshold circuit with $N$ internal gates, one output gate,
  and depth at most two computes $N$-bit parity. Hence the parity family is in
  $\cc{TC}^0$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  For $1 \le j \le N$ let $T_j$ be the threshold gate ``at least $j$ ones''
  over all inputs. The output gate reads $T_j$ for odd $j$ and $\neg T_j$ for
  even $j$. If the input has $w$ ones, exactly
  $\lfloor N/2 \rfloor + (w \bmod 2)$ of these signed inputs are true, so the
  output threshold $\lfloor N/2 \rfloor + 1$ computes parity. The empty input
  has its answer, false, stored separately.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}
  \label{cor:lowerbounds-ac0-ne-tc0}
  \lean{Complexity.AC0_ssubset_TC0}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:NC-AC, def:circuits-TC}
  $\cc{AC}^0 \subsetneq \cc{TC}^0$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  \uses{prop:circuits-depth-classes, thm:lowerbounds-parity-not-ac0,
    lem:lowerbounds-parity-tc0}
  $\cc{AC}^0 \subseteq \cc{TC}^0$, and parity is in $\cc{TC}^0$ but not in
  $\cc{AC}^0$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Pseudorandom switching lemma]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-pseudorandom-restrictions}
  \uses{thm:lowerbounds-switching}
  \notready
  The bound of Theorem~\ref{thm:lowerbounds-switching} holds, up to an additive
  error $\varepsilon$, when the free/fixed pattern and the fixed values are
  drawn from a distribution that $\varepsilon$-fools CNFs of size
  polynomial in $N$ instead of the uniform product distribution
  (Trevisan--Xue). With an explicit generator fooling CNFs, such restrictions
  can be sampled from $\mathrm{polylog}(N / \varepsilon)$ random bits.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  The bad event of the switching lemma is itself decided by a small CNF-like
  test on the restriction, so fooling that test preserves its probability.
  This requires a formal notion of pseudorandom distributions and of fooling a
  class of tests, which the library does not yet state; pseudorandom
  generators for $\cc{AC}^0$ would build on it.
\end{proof}

\section{Polynomial approximation and $\cc{AC}^0[3]$}
\label{sec:lowerbounds-smolensky}

Smolensky's method approximates circuits with $\mathrm{MOD}_3$ gates by
low-degree polynomials over $\mathbb{F}_3 = \mathbb{Z}/3$ and shows that parity
has no such approximation (Razborov--Smolensky). The formalization works with
functions $\{0,1\}^n \to \mathbb{F}_3$ instead of formal polynomials, and it
replaces the random choice of the OR approximators by exact counting over all
choices.

\begin{definition}[Low-degree functions over $\mathbb{F}_3$]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-smolensky-low-degree}
  \lean{Complexity.Smolensky.bitVal, Complexity.Smolensky.monomial,
    Complexity.Smolensky.lowDegree}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:bitstring}
  Read each bit as $0$ or $1$ in $\mathbb{F}_3$. The monomial of a set
  $S \subseteq [n]$ is the function $x \mapsto \prod_{i \in S} x_i$, and a
  function $\{0,1\}^n \to \mathbb{F}_3$ has degree at most $D$ when it is an
  $\mathbb{F}_3$-linear combination of monomials of sets of size at most $D$.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Smolensky's approximation]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-smolensky-approximation}
  \lean{Complexity.Smolensky.exists_lowDegree_approx}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-smolensky-low-degree, def:circuits-ACC}
  Let $C$ be a single-output circuit over the AND/OR/$\mathrm{MOD}_3$ basis with
  $n \ge 1$ inputs and depth at most $d$, and let $\ell \ge 1$. Some function
  $P$ of degree at most $(2\ell)^d$ satisfies
  $\#\{x : P(x) \ne C(x)\} \cdot 2^\ell \le \mathrm{size}(C) \cdot 2^n$, where
  $C(x)$ is read in $\mathbb{F}_3$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  A $\mathrm{MOD}_3$ gate on $u_1, \dots, u_m$ is exactly $(\sum_i u_i)^2$. An OR
  gate is approximated by $1 - \prod_{j < \ell} (1 - (\sum_{i \in T_j} u_i)^2)$
  for subsets $T_1, \dots, T_\ell$ of its inputs, and an AND gate by De Morgan
  duality; each multiplies degrees by at most $2\ell$. If $u \ne 0$ and
  $u_k = 1$, pairing each subset $S$ with $S \mathbin{\triangle} \{k\}$ shows that
  at most half the subsets have $\sum_{i \in S} u_i = 0$, so at most a
  $2^{-\ell}$ fraction of the tuples err at $u$; if $u = 0$ none err. Counting
  pairs of a tuple and an input, some tuple errs on at most $2^{n - \ell}$
  inputs, evaluated on the gate's true input vector. Fix such a tuple at every
  gate and compose along the circuit: the result is correct at every input at
  which every gate approximator is.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Parity is hard to approximate]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-smolensky-parity}
  \lean{Complexity.Smolensky.card_agree_xorBool_le}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-smolensky-low-degree, def:lowerbounds-parity}
  A function of degree at most $D$ agrees with $n$-bit parity, read in
  $\mathbb{F}_3$, on at most
  $\#\{S \subseteq [n] : |S| \le \lfloor n/2 \rfloor + D\}$ inputs.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Put $y_i = 1 + x_i \in \{1, -1\}$, so that $\prod_i y_i = 1 + \oplus(x)$ and
  $y_i^2 = 1$. On the set $G$ where $P$ agrees with parity, $1 + P$ agrees with
  $\prod_i y_i$, so every $y$-monomial $y_S$ with $|S| > \lfloor n/2 \rfloor$
  equals $(1 + P)\, y_{[n] \setminus S}$ there, of degree at most
  $D + \lfloor n/2 \rfloor$. The $y$-monomials span all functions, because point
  indicators have degree at most $n$ and every $x$-monomial expands into
  $y$-monomials. Hence every function $G \to \mathbb{F}_3$ is the restriction of
  a function of degree at most $\lfloor n/2 \rfloor + D$, and comparing
  cardinalities, $3^{|G|}$ is at most $3$ to the number of such monomials.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Binomial estimate]
  \label{lem:lowerbounds-smolensky-binomial}
  \lean{Complexity.Smolensky.ten_mul_card_le}
  \leanok
  If $100 (t + 1)^2 \le n + 1$, then
  $10 \cdot \#\{S \subseteq [n] : |S| \le \lfloor n/2 \rfloor + t\} \le 6 \cdot 2^n$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  By complementation at most $2^{n-1}$ subsets have $2|S| < n$, and the others
  counted have one of at most $t + 1$ sizes, each with at most
  $\binom{n}{\lfloor n/2 \rfloor}$ subsets. Induction on $m$ gives
  $\binom{2m}{m}^2 (2m + 1) \le 16^m$, hence
  $\binom{n}{\lfloor n/2 \rfloor}^2 (n + 1) \le 4^n$ and
  $10 (t + 1) \binom{n}{\lfloor n/2 \rfloor} \le 2^n$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Razborov--Smolensky size bound]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-smolensky}
  \lean{Complexity.Smolensky.parity_counting_bound,
    Complexity.Smolensky.parity_size_lower_bound}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:circuits-ACC, def:lowerbounds-parity}
  Let $C$ be a single-output circuit over the AND/OR/$\mathrm{MOD}_3$ basis with
  $n \ge 1$ inputs and depth at most $d$ that computes $n$-bit parity. For
  every $\ell \ge 1$,
  \[ 2^\ell \cdot 2^n \le
     2^\ell \cdot \#\{S \subseteq [n] : |S| \le \lfloor n/2 \rfloor + (2\ell)^d\}
     + \mathrm{size}(C) \cdot 2^n , \]
  and for every $\ell$ with $(40 \ell)^{2d} \le n$,
  $5 \cdot \mathrm{size}(C) \ge 2 \cdot 2^\ell$. Taking
  $\ell = \lfloor n^{1/(2d)}/40 \rfloor$ gives
  $\mathrm{size}(C) \ge 2^{\Omega(n^{1/(2d)})}$; this asymptotic reading is
  not a separate formal statement.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  \uses{thm:lowerbounds-smolensky-approximation, thm:lowerbounds-smolensky-parity,
    lem:lowerbounds-smolensky-binomial}
  The approximation of Theorem~\ref{thm:lowerbounds-smolensky-approximation}
  agrees with parity on all but $\mathrm{size}(C) \cdot 2^{n-\ell}$ inputs, and
  Theorem~\ref{thm:lowerbounds-smolensky-parity} bounds the agreement. If
  $(40\ell)^{2d} \le n$ and $d \ge 1$, then $100((2\ell)^d + 1)^2 \le n + 1$,
  and Lemma~\ref{lem:lowerbounds-smolensky-binomial} bounds the number of
  subsets by $\tfrac{6}{10} \cdot 2^n$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Parity is not in $\cc{AC}^0[3]$]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-parity-not-acc3}
  \lean{Complexity.xorBool_not_mem_AC0Mod_three}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:circuits-ACC, def:lowerbounds-parity}
  The parity family is not in $\cc{AC}^0[3]$. Since
  $\cc{AC}^0 \subseteq \cc{AC}^0[3]$
  (Proposition~\ref{prop:circuits-ac-subset-acc}), this gives a second proof
  that it is not in $\cc{AC}^0$; Theorem~\ref{thm:lowerbounds-parity-not-ac0}
  proves that statement through the switching lemma.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  \uses{thm:lowerbounds-smolensky, prop:circuits-ac-subset-acc}
  If a family of size at most $p(n)$ for a polynomial $p$ and depth at most $c$
  computed parity, then at length $n = (40\ell)^{2c}$
  Theorem~\ref{thm:lowerbounds-smolensky} would give
  $2 \cdot 2^\ell \le 5\, p((40\ell)^{2c})$ for every $\ell \ge 1$, which fails
  for large $\ell$.
\end{proof}

\section{Monotone circuits}
\label{sec:lowerbounds-monotone}

\begin{definition}[Monotone circuit]
  \label{def:lowerbounds-monotone-circuit}
  \uses{def:circuit, def:circuits-bases}
  A monotone circuit is an AND/OR circuit all of whose negation flags are
  false. Every monotone circuit computes a monotone function.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Monotone Boolean matrix product]
  \label{thm:lowerbounds-monotone-matrix}
  \uses{def:lowerbounds-monotone-circuit}
  \lean{Algebraic.Monotone.MatrixProduct.mul_mul_le_andCost,
    Algebraic.Monotone.MatrixProduct.cube_le_andCost}
  \leanok
  Consider a circuit over the imported library's binary AND/OR signature
  (fan-in-two AND and OR gates, no constants and no negations) in which the
  entries $x_{ik}$ of an $I \times K$ matrix and $y_{kj}$ of a $K \times J$
  matrix are distinct inputs (other inputs may be present). If, for all $i$
  and $j$, some output computes $\bigvee_k x_{ik} \wedge y_{kj}$ (other outputs
  are unconstrained), then the circuit has at least $IJK$ AND gates. Every AND
  gate of the circuit is counted, used or not, and OR gates are free. In
  particular, a circuit with the entries of two $n \times n$ matrices on its
  $2n^2$ inputs and their Boolean product on its $n^2$ outputs, all in
  row-major order, has at least $n^3$ AND gates. The schoolbook circuit, with
  exactly $IJK$ AND gates, shows the bound is tight; neither this nor the
  companion lower bound of $IJ(K - 1)$ OR gates (Paterson; Mehlhorn--Galil)
  is formalized.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Following Wegener (\emph{The Complexity of Boolean Functions}, Theorem~6.8.1,
  after Paterson and Mehlhorn--Galil), call $x_{ik}$ and $y_{kj}$ the variables
  of type $k$, and re-evaluate the same circuit in stages $k = 1, \dots, K$.
  Stage $k$ sets the variables of type below $k$ ($x$ to $1$, $y$ to $0$), so
  the outputs become the product over the types from $k$ on, and forces to the
  constant $1$ every gate that was constant $1$ under this restriction at the
  previous stage, or whose function accepts each of two distinct type-$k$
  variables on its own. Forcing keeps the outputs: below an input rejected by
  an output lies a rejected input that sets at most one type-$k$ variable to
  $0$, and every forced gate is already $1$ there. Call a gate a creator of $(i, k, j)$
  if its stage-$k$ function has the prime implicant $x_{ik} y_{kj}$ and neither
  argument's function has it. The output for $(i, j)$ has this prime implicant,
